=== PAGE 002 ===
The book:
“Reverso” means “| turn over’. The word is the
name of, and at the same time explains, the
“quick-change artist” of the watch world —
Jaeger-LeCoultre’s REVERSO. This watch
has become a living legend, as this book
shows. Time, like the REVERSO, has two
different faces: one that is shaped by time-
conscious use of the hours in each day; the
other, a time of rest and relaxation during
which time should be of no importance, when
a small turning movement can transform
the REVERSO into the “time-free watch”.
But the REVERSO also derives its mythical
aura from its origins, as it has been a genuine
piece of Art Deco ever since it was invented
in 1931. It symbolizes an important part of
the identity of one of the world’s last true
watch factories, Jaeger-LeCoultre. This tribute
to the REVERSO, written by a well-known
journalist and watchmaking expert, extends
well beyond the scope of a purely non-fiction
book. In an entertaining style the author
relates how this watchmaking “turn of the
century” came about. He also links the
REVERSO’s sixty-year life with the fasci-
nating period of world history from 1931 to
1991. The reader is then introduced to the
remote Vallée de Joux — “Watch Valley” — in
the Swiss Jura mountains, home of the Jaeger-
LeCoultre “Manufacture” and its highly-skilled
employees. The text and illustrations describe
and portray in detail the time-consuming
manufacture of this classic timepiece, which
still requires a large amount of traditional hand
craftsmanship. The author also describes the
commemoration of the REVERSO’s 60th
anniversary in 1991, when the launch of
the limited-edition jubilee model marked the
beginning of a new future for the REVERSO
and confirmed its role as a vehicle of new
watch ideas. A sizeable appendix reproduces
the original 1931 patent, inter alia, and pro-
vides the collector with information about the
REVERSO’s former and present mechanical
movements.

FOREWORD

m1 Manfred Fritz, the author of this remarkable book
Trey, who manufactured gold and china
Iso obsessed by precision watches,

As we will learn fror !
the brilliant Swiss César de
dentures in the 1920s, was a

Although nota genius, lam also passionately interested in watch-
an | fail to be, after having frequented for more

king perfection. How Ce vin
ue ee workshops, watch factories, exhibitions and all the other
places in which the timepieces of our dreams are made? We owe the

fulfilment of these dreams to a handful of watchmakers and firms which
have attained the status of nobility in the profession over the centuries,
They have given a face to time and have revealed this face to the world,

Today this noble rank is perpetuated by, among other things, one
of Jaeger-LeCoultre’s many creations, the Reverso, a watch first made in
4931. After more than sixty years this watch is as timeless as ever ~ ina

fast-changing, fast-moving world.

In retracing the history of the Reverso, Manfred Fritz is not only
innovating by writing a book about a product but is also adding a new
chapter to the long history of master-watc hmakers and the great names
of watchmaking.

The reader will be plunged into the unique world of the high
valleys of the Swiss Jura, where tradition and dedication to fine watch-
making have been preserved and where the drive to achieve beauty and
perfection is almost Heaven-sent.

This book not only describes the creation and sixty-year-plus
history of an exceptional watch. It also reveals the secrets of its manufac-
ture and of its future prospects, which have rightfully earned the Reverso
the name “The living legend”.

Jaeger-LeCoultre deserves our gratitude for having kept Swiss
watchmaking at such a high level of perfection.

=== PAGE 007 ===
Jaeger-LeCoultre’s Reverso could have been invented yesterday instead
of in 1931 — or in only sixty years’ time. This incomparable watch is one
of the few objects that do not grow old. For six decades the Reverso has

icently withstood the onslaught of the times. The watch which
can be turned and reversed at will and which has been made and worn
virtually unchanged for two generations deserves to be acknowledged
as a fascinating part of the history of time.

In the German language the Reverso is sometimes respectfully
referred to -— and not without reason ~ as a “Kultuhr’ (cult watch), This
play on words emphasizes the contribution it has made to the culture
of the age. In other words, it has created a new approach to handling

time.

Indeed, the “old innovation” is casting a spell over us as never
before. Although we are living at an ever faster pace, at the same time
we feel a need to cherish sound, lasting values. Like the Reverso, for
example. It is the small things we see every day that give shape to our
lives - like the Reverso’s simple turn, the monogram engraved on the
back of its case, its flattering, streamlined Art Deco shape. Not many
objects with such a heritage have survived unchanged for so long. The
fact that even today the Reverso is not a museum piece emphasizes the
topicality of its function: to measure time, but also to allow time to pass.
And so it has been since 1931.

The “Manufacture” Jaeger-LeCoultre has a motto ~ “the hand,
heart and eye” ~ which refers to the garded skills and virtues of
the watchmakers from Le Sentier e Vallée de Joux in western
Switzerland, But the motto could also be interpreted as meaning: the
hand that wears it, the heart that enjoys it and the eye that reads it - the
time that the Reverso shows or conceals, as the case may be.

The *hand, heart and eye” are
essential tools when manufac-

turing a watch like the Reverso.

REVERSO -
THE LIVING
LEGEND

fi ounter with it took place a few years ago at 30,000 feet on a
My first wt to New York. The unusual object on the wrist of the
flight ae ae riassitil lunch trays across the rows of seats, aroused
es oe and then my immense curiosity. Itwas neither a watch
atracelet just a square golden case with an elegant monogram on

a dark leather strap.

The young man wearing his father's heirloom on his wrist antici-
pated the question that was on the tip of my tongue, since he had
already answered it hundreds of times. However, instead of saying
something, he performed an amazing little trick with the thumb and
index finger of his right hand. His finger pushed the case sideways, his
thumb flipped it over, whereupon it clicked gently into position to
reveal a watch of the utmost elegance. “Voila, a Reverso”, said the
steward simply and hurried on.

A Reverso? The charm and the provocative nature of that little
ritual are deeply engraved in my memory. How arrogant, simply to turn
your back on time! And yet, at the same time, what patrician ease and
confidence in that little gesture, that pleasant game of hide-and-seek
with one’s own time.

This inimitable watch amongst watches celebrated a landmark
anniversary in 1991. The story began sixty years ago on the threshold
between the “Roaring Twenties” and the no less exciting thirties. It was
atime of change in every respect — politically, culturally and in people's
carefree joie de vivre. The external design of this “reversible watch” was
imbued with the spirit of “Art Nouveau”, a period of art whose classical
and yet modern, flowing lines were later to evolve into “Art Deco”. Even
today the furniture, jewellery, architecture and design of that period still
reveal traces of the rapid emergence of modernism

But the real impetus for the creation of the Reverso was anything
but artistic — rather, it came from the world of sport. It owes its origins to
an extremely practical consideration. At the time wristwatches were still
very delicate instruments and, if their triumphal march was to continue,
they somehow had to be proved capable of withstanding more rigorous
uses and thus of combating the stresses of everyday wear. The military
had clearly recognized since the First World War the advantage of a

=== PAGE 009 ===
REVERSO -
THE LIVING
LEGEND

twatch that could be read easily and they had protected the sen-
WHTsDWe Ie sf

elass of these usually bulky desc endants of the pocket watch with
ve ent 3 si ?

hy metal bars. The story of the Reverso 1s, however, a classic example
of the final emancipation of the elegant wristwatch as well. And fine
proof that war does not always have to be the father of all things. The
thirties were not only a very jashton-conscious age but an era that finally

established sport as a leisure activity. The Reverso owes its creation to

both influences.

The protective function alone of the turning mec hanism invented
for it soon became superfluous with the advent of unbreakable glass
and shock-resistant movements. But the fact that the Reverso has out-
lasted all subsequent fashion trends and innovations virtually un-
changed since it first appeared in 1931 and that it has detied all changes
in taste over the past six dec ades makes it as unique — and sought-after -
asa star. [tis more than ever a watch and an individual piece of jewellery
at the same time, its wearer's very personal visiting card. The famous
words once uttered by Oscar Wilde are well-suited to the Reverso: “If
you surround me with luxury | can easily do without necessities”.

And the legend lives on, as. an incomparable part of the history of
time and a perfect example of an optimal combination of aesthetics and
technology. At that time it was made - and it still is today — by the
world’s best watchmakers in the Vallée de Joux, 40 miles north of
Geneva and 3,000 feet above sea level, which offers the peace and
quiet needed for creative work. Over these six decades it has per-
petuated its myth of being an elegant sign of good taste, on the polo
fields of England and India, on the golf courses of Italy, in the salons of
Paris, at the gaming tables of Baden-Baden and in the offices of New
York and Tokyo. With its turning movement the “timeless” watch has
built a bndge to history. Yet it also reveals the contemporary dilemma of
an age that suffers from the disease of a chronic shortage of time. In his
book “Vol de Nuit” published in 1931, the same year the Reverso first
began to count the seconds and minutes, Antoine de Saint Exupéry
wrote the profoundly true words: “Each second Carries off something
with it”. The protective function the Reverso had at that time has
assumed a completely new significance from this point of view: the way
in which te ts faded out and turned away makes its relentless march
more bearable. So today “Reverso” is not just a fine-sounding name in

itself but also suggests the desirable state of being as tree as possible
from ume constraints.

Finally, the watch from Le Sentier also carries oul into the world a
message from the watchmakers of the Jaeger-LeCoultre Manufacture in
the sleepy Vallée de Joux: one should have the freedom to manage
one’s own time and to attain perfection in what one does. As long ago
as 1779 Goethe, that keen traveller, waxed enthusiastic about the Vallée
de Joux and the benefits of its peace and quiet. The remote region,
which in days gone by used to be cut off from the outside world for six
months during the winter, has to this day preserved most of its ancient
originality. The inventive people had = and still have today - enough
time to immerse themselves in the arts of time measurement and
micro-mechanical engineering without being distracted. By opening the
first workshop in Le Sentier in 1833 Antoine LeCoultre laid the founda-
tion for the famous “Manufacture”, whose sole ambition has always
been to make the best, long-lasting and highly-sophisticated watches.
Sixty years later the long-term results of the Reverso are an impressive
endorsement of the founder's objectives. Here again a quotation from
Oscar Wilde is appropriate: “My taste is quite simple, | always take only
the best”.

Jaeger-LeCoultre would like to share its great pleasure at the
long-lasting success of the Reverso with as many friends as possible. The
1991 European Watch, Clock and Jewellery Fair in Basle became the
starting point for the second triumphal march of this watch, which is not
something out of the future but from history. At that event the public
hada chance to gaze in amazement at some new “faces” of the Reverso,
including the model in classic Art Deco style, with which the Reverso
had already set a trend back in 1931. But sixty years later in Basle the
18-carat rose-gold jubilee Reverso - a limited, 500-piece edition — also
celebrated a sparkling premiere: fitted with a date display, power-
reserve indicator and a small seconds-hand, it pays an incomparable
tribute to the old Reverso principle. The sapphire glass on the upwardly-
rotatable base of its case reveals the mechanism of the nev -designed
hand-winding movement with its solid-gold, decorated bottom plates
and bridges. The Reverso as a watch on the one hand and as a “living”
piece of jewellery on the other. The timepiece whose reverse becomes
the showcase for the fascinating play of mechanics. A mere push of

invention is clearly still an ingenious, futuristic idea, Itis as if the designer
had foreseen in 1931 that precision-engineered watch movements
would eventually become visible through the back of the case.

But the Reverso’s anniversary has also brought Jaeger-LeCoultre
out from their secluded mountain valley: starting in Basle, a me-
ticulously organized travelling exhibition featuring irreplaceably rare
watches began a world tour. This “Journey to the Centre of Time” gave
thousands of visitors in Paris, Munich, Milan, Singapore and Hongkong a
limpse of the Vallée de Joux - known as “Watch Valley” — which can
claim to be the cradle of cultivated, highly-developed precision watch-
making, In a movement of the “Reverso 60th” magnified 700 times, the
history of watchmaking was traced from its beginnings until the present
day, by means of historic illustrations, documents, tools and valuable
watches from then and now. This high watermark of a traditional craft is
experiencing a happy rebirth at the end of the 20th century.

THE
“TURN OF THE

CENTURY”

Quite a few of the remarkable inventions made down through the ages
have been hit upon by chance. Such as porcelain, which the alchemists
How the Reverso discovered when trying to make “gold ina test-tube”. Another example
was an explosive substance such as guncotton, which only replaced
smoking black powder in the mid-19th century because a slightly ab-

Act I:

came to Le Sentier on

a strange detour sent-minded chemistry professor in Basle spilt some sulphuric and nitric
acids in his kitchen. He wiped up the caustic liquid with his wife’s cotton

ia India and Paris and
apron and then, when drying the cloth over a stove, saw to his surprise

why a gold-denture how it exploded in a hissing tongue of flame.
dealer, of all people,

The history of the Reverso also began in 1931 with a fortunate aa
played the most : piece of inspiration. And it involves the flair of some participants who - PN TIER
important role. in watchmaking terms - made it into the “turn of the century”. But, B DU Lac DE

taking them in order of appearance, the protagonists included, accord- a Gusytl |
ing to witnesses’ reliable oral reports and to the written sources, a HORS CONCOL
lt, couple of “eccentric” Englishmen (eccentric in the nicest possible sense, _

of course), the dynamic gold-denture dealer César de Trey, a French
engineer called Chauvot, the two watchmakers Edmond Jaeger in Paris
and Jacques-David LeCoultre in Le Sentier, western Switzerland, who
were already collaborating closely, the competent employees of Jaeger
S.A. and LeCoultre & Cie, not forgetting several gentlemen who would
probably prefer to remain anonymous as they ruined themselves by
attempting to copy the Reverso.

Until the mid-twenties Swiss-born César de Trey had made his
money by making “third” sets of gold and porcelain dentures in a
flourishing firm in England. From 1927 onwards he began to convert his
second passion — a love of beautiful timepieces — into a profitable
occupation. In the western Swiss city of Lausanne he at first established
- along with other companies in Paris and London — the watch sales
organization “Hermetica SA”, followed by the “Société Specialités oe
horlogeres” in 1931. As the name indicates, it was a company spe-

farques-David Le cializing in selected watches at the luxury end of the market.

: © to the Manutactun Those who knew de Trey describe him both as an “eccentric alge
Senter was instrument genius” where watches were concerned and as a sophisticated entre- awards, she
ther with his busiiess preneur with a marked flair for understanding what customers wanted.

1 the “hare At that time no-one had ever heard of the word “marketing”. People still

20
=== PAGE 014 ===
THE
“TURN OF THE
CENTURY

believed a product need only be good enough to sell. Advertising too
was still in its infancy, and was regarded - especially by naturally modest
watchmakers - as. an extremely vulgar form of public ingratiation. Was
this really necessary? However, many a business disappointment suf-
fered in those years was due to this very likeable but entepreneunially
naive view of human consumption patterns.

But César de Trey was already thinking and acting quite dif-
terently. His newly-established and discreet firm in Lausanne was a res-
ponse to the traditional reticence of renowned watchmakers. De Trey’s
aim was by no means to produce watches of his own. But he saw clearly
that the highly-developed watchmaking craft in western Switzerland
could be marketed better and on as worldwide a basis as possible,

Assisted in business by his son Jean-Pierre and his closest colla-
borator Savary, Cesar de Trey saw himself and his financially independ-
ent firm as a kind of innovation centre, as a birthplace for good watch-
making ideas which he put into practice on a case-by-case basis with
existing manufacturers Of Movements or cases and which he then sold
with the help of his international connections. These included, for
example, such unusual specialities as the “Ermeto”, a pocket watch
wound up by opening the divided folding cover and which was pro-
duced by Movado. A lot of money was wasted on such experiments.
But de Trey was never short of the courage to embark on new ventures,
even in the midst of one of the deepest recessions to hit the Swiss Jura
mountains and the world economy as a whole.

Moreover, de Trey’s firm initially shared its low profile with the
long-established, dignified watch manufacturer LeCoultre in Le Sentier,
with which he soon established close contacts and business relations.
Although for almost 100 years LeCoultre had been devising and produc:
ing what were clearly the most refined, technically complex and
highest-quality watches of that time, the firm had virtually never sold
them under its own name. Yet renowned Geneva firms such as Patek
Philippe, Vacheron & Constantin or the aristocratic Paris firm of Cartier
owed their fame to movements made by the “genius watchmakers”
trom the Vallée de Joux. Léon Constantin, who had been employed

the customers knew it was a LeCoultre movement, only a watchmaker
who opened the case saw it, just as a car mechanic who opens a bonnet
can immediately identify whether it is a Bentley, a Volkswagen or a

Cesar de Trey liked travelling and did so extensively. This passion ideally
suited his job as an international merchant. In 1930 one of his business
trips took him to India, which at that time was, of course, still under
British administration. One day he met among his majesty’s colonial
officers some old friends who took the opportunity to demonstrate to
him with great enthusiasm the rather robust version of British fair playon
the polo field. History does not tell us whether César de Trey himselt
was able to make much of this “hockey on horseback”. What is certain,
however, is that the wristwatches ~ still sensitive, but now more fashion-
able ~ worn by those English sportsmen were regularly among the losers
of such Polo matches: although broken watch glasses bore silent
testimony to the great fighting spirit of their owners, they served only
to underline the limited suitability of such timepieces for sporting activ-
ues - and, of course, the man of the world would insist on wearing his
wristwatch when playing polo, golf or tennis. The watches were simply
unable to withstand the rigours of the new sportiness, De Trey’s British

24

friends knew he had by now switched from dentures to watches and so
they innocently asked him whether it might be possible to make a
watch that could be covered or possibly even turned over to protect
the fragile glass during the game. The word “turn over” stuck in the mind
of their Swiss guest and watch enthusiast like an arrow in its target. There
were sufficient watches with a covering device, most of them were
round, thick and not at all fashionable. De Trey grasped at once what a
revolutionary idea (literally!) had been handed to him on a plate. One
has to imagine the scene: after a dusty polo match, over a cool drink at
the bar of the officers’ club, one of de Trey’s British friends dangles a
watch with a broken glass under his nose and asks a seemingly naive
question, unaware that he had just stimulated the intellectual act of
creating the Reverso. It was a fine, stylish beginning.

During the return journey César de Trey could not get the idea
out of his head. Of course, a reversible watch had to be feasible and
would certainly become a commercial success, he thought. Rectangular
watches were in fashion at the time, which of course made the matter
easier from a technical point of view. And he already knew to whom
he had to turn with this tricky problem: Jacques-David LeCoultre in Le
Sentier, who not only maintained close relations with Geneva and Paris
as well as, through the company Jaeger, with London, but was always
the unchallenged number one in this circle of co-operating business
friends where all watchmaking matters were concerned.

In the meantime de Trey and LeCoultre were co-operating inten-
sively with each other. For example, it was de Trey who had introduced
the inventor of the “Atmos”, Jean-Léon Reutter, to LeCoultre and who
had brought the production (which was just beginning in 1930) of this
sensational clock to Le Sentier, Lausanne was now acting as the most
important staging post between the Manufacture and its numerous cus-
tomers and markets. So de Trey travelled to the sleepy Vallée de Joux,
described to Jacques-David LeCoultre the original idea he had brought
back from India and, as he had expected, found the interest and com-
mitment necessary for the “reversible watch” to become a reality.

The French capital will now set the scene for the next chapter of this
story, which lasts quite some time. One autumn day in 1930 Jacques-
David LeCoultre boarded the train from Vallorbe to Paris. His destina-
tion was rue du Louvre no. 33 in the city centre. Jaeper SA’s “Watchmak-
ing Division” had been located here for a long time. These workshops
were separate from the firm’s production shops for instruments and car
and aircraft components, most of whose mechanical parts were also
manufactured in Le Sentier.

Although that is another story, it cannot be separated from the
creation of the Reverso. Jaeger in Paris and LeCoultre in Le Sentier can
jointly claim the credit for creating the Reverso. Which is why the dial
now reads, quite correctly: “Jaeger-LeCoultre”. This calls for a brief flash-
back: the close collaboration between the two firms had begun at the
turn of the century and had attained a first zenith in 1914 with the out-
break of the First World War. Working methods were very unconven-
tional and pragmatic in those days. It was not so much contracts in
written or legal form that constituted the framework for the successful
co-operation between the firms as the close human contacts between
the leading employees.

Edmond Jaeger (1850-1920), an Alsatian who bore the official title
of “state-appointed watchmaker’ initially produced marine chronom-
eters in Paris and later, on an ever-larger scale, instruments for cars and
above all for the first civilian and military aircraft. In addition, some
excellent specialists were on hand as partners: Gustave Delage, for
example, from the family of the car manufacturer Delage, who was also
a navy officer, a pilot since 1910, world record holder for long-distance
ilights and designer of the first seaplane and some fighter aircraft. And
Edmond Audemars, another of the first French pilots, holder of licence
number 20, of the record fora non-stop flight from Paris to Berlin in 1913
and of the altitude record with 6,700 metres in 1916. The circle of
friends, who also had business connections with Edmond Jaeger,
included famous names such as Raoul-Edouard Badin, a pilot and engi-
neer, Leon Morane, then the world’s fastest flier, and the American
Charles Weymann, a pilot and designer of both aircraft and auto-
mobiles. Jaeger was, of course, able to exploit profitably his connections
with the aircraft and automobile industries. But he could also draw on

This history of close transnational reer echo
that had been launched since the First World War si aves oe
the mind once again of Jacques-David LeCoultre, t — age Lite or
autumn of 1930as he was travelling to Paris, this time by ee —
economic depression was still affecting many sectors, ne ing :
watch, automobile and aircraft industries. He felt that new products an

new ideas could do no harm.

As the Manufacture in Le Sentier had little experience of produc
ine wristwatch cases itself, LeCoultre needed to rely on the tried-and-
tested co-operation with the specialists at Jaeger for the project of

28

making a reversible watch. He also wanted to know what his French
friends thought of the slightly crazy idea of the golf and polo-playing
Englishmen. Jaeger’s engineers and managers listened to the story, took
a liking to it and entrusted the further details to their acquaintance, the
designer and engineer René-Alfred Chauvot, who lived at no. 7 rue
Bachaumont.

On March 4, 1931 at 1.15 p.m. Chauvot filed a patent application
under the number 712 868 at the French Ministry of Trade and Industry
fora watch “that can be slid in its support and completely turned over’.
In his first patent specification - which was to be followed in the same
year by two additions with improvements to details - Chauvot docu-
mented with 17 simple drawings how he had designed the mechanism:
a separate bottom plate is provided with guide slots on its two side parts
which are angled upwards at 90°. The watch proper, which Chauvot had
depicted as square-shaped in his first drawings, therefore fits perfectly
between the two upturned side parts of the support. On two opposite
sides of the actual case there are to be fixed, first, two pivots which can
be slid and turned in the guide slots and - parallel to them on the oppo-
site sides — catch devices, for example, spring-mounted balls, so that
“the case and support can be removably locked in their two possible
positions: with the face either concealed or visible”. Chauvot added:
“Thanks to this invention the watch can be worn optionally in one
or other of these two positions, and it is extremely simple and easy
to change from one position to the other’. (See the reproduction of

the original text of the first patent specification dated March 4, 1931 in
the Appendix).

Chauvot had already specified in his first plan all the essential
design features of the Reverso, which remain unchanged to this day:
the two-part design of the whole device with the bottom plate and
case, the guide grooves on the upwardly angled sides of the bottom
plate, the pivots that are recessed into the case and are designed to
run in the guide slots and, finally, the spring-mounted spherical or
hemispherical balls, which are also recessed into the case, act as a
latching means and which, after rotation, lock the case again to the side
of the bottom plate. In order to prevent the watch from moving side-
ways when reversed, he suggested providing a raised glass face which,

after reversal, would slot into a corresponding recess in the bottom
plate.

It was certainly not an elegant solution, as it highlighted even
more the squareness of Chauvot's design. In fact, the engineer's initial
drawings show no sign of any flair for what was fashionable or “good
taste” in design at that time. His first improvement to the design con-
cerned in particular the shape and mechanics of the lateral pivots. But
the second improvement to the patent specification - made after
lengthy discussions with the principals - does reveal the Reverso’s
typical outer shape ~ rectangular, rounded and streamlined. The
projecting glass became redundant as a result of the improvement to
the turning mechanism in favour of a rounded, glass mounting which is
flush with the case and which ~ in the reversed position — slots into a
corresponding recess in the bottom plate. The watch could be rotated
about its axis in the two cavities on either side of the bottom plate.
A mitre joint (Fig. 15, 22) that had originally been provided on the right-

hand edge of the case to make it easier to turn the watch was dropped
for aesthetic reasons.

But apart from this the customers were pleased with René
Chauvot’s “revolutionary” watch and so convinced of its potential suc-
cess that on July 31 of the same year, 1931, they sealed the launch of
the watch with a contract which also appropriately remunerated the
inventor's work: in addition to a one-off payment of 10,000 Swiss francs
for the assignment of all the rights to the invention, Chauvot was to be
paid a further SFr. 2.50 for each watch sold with the case he had design-
ed. The makers of the case committed themselves hurriedly so that

the watch would be in the shops in time for the Christmas trade of the
same year. They were envisaging the production of a total of 5,000
pieces per year ~ in steel and gold and in two sizes, for gentlemen and

ladies.

The document was signed in Lausanne by René-Alfred Chauvot,
Messrs. A. & E. Wenger from Geneva and by César de Trey and his son
Pierre. LeCoultre and Jaeger, whose interests were represented here by
César de Trey, realized it was difficult to make such an unusual case on
their own. They therefore entrusted this job to the renowned specialist
Wenger from Geneva, who had only a short time to make the compli-
cated tools and to start production. As Article 14 of the contract reveals,
the seller - in this case de Trey - had been allowed to use a movement
made by Tavannes, Movado or Jaeger (i.e.: LeCoultre). This clause
ensured that it would not prove impossible to market the new watch
in the near future due to the lack of suitable movements in Le Sentier.
But this text states that a special movement would then have to be
developed which would be “completely suited” to the dimensions of
the Chauvot case. It was obvious this movement would be built by
LeCoultre. Finally, it was also César de Trey who spontaneously found
a suitable name for the product on the basis of the drawings available

to him: Reverso.

The plan to market the watch before the end of 1931 caused con-
siderable embarrassment — for the first and only time in its history - to
the Manufacture in Le Sentier, where the watches were to be assembled
with the cases supplied by Wenger and finished: the manufacturer of
fine watches par excellence lacked the right calibre of movement for its
attempt to sell a wristwatch of its own under its own name. The round
movements that were in production were too small and the famous
“Duoplan” shaped movement, which would have been most suitable,
being a LeCoultre movement of the best quality, was too thick.

And so it happened that movements made by Tavannes Watch
Co. ticked in the first Reversos, up to about the end of 1932, calibre 064
in the gentleman's model and calibre 050 in the smaller ladies’ model,
both without a seconds-hand display. The Reverso was launched in
keeping with LeCoultre’s view of itself, that is, with a touch of watch-
making infidelity. Nevertheless it opened a new chapter in Le Sentier,

32

marking the Manufacture’s breakthrough, after almost 100 years as a
barely-known maker of fine movements, to being a maker of complete
watches. And, last but not least: the English polo players at last had their
sports-resistant watch. So, of course, did the Italian, French and soon
also the American and German golfers and tennis lovers.

As always with innovations, it immediately attracted imitators.
Léon Constantin, who as a salesman with Jaeger-LeCoultre travelled half
way round the world with the Reverso to bring it to the customers, still
smiles today when he recalls how half a dozen imitators, evading the
licensing fees, tried unsuccessfully to copy the Reverso - and ruined
themselves in the process.

THE

“TURN OF THE
CENTURY”

The Reverso did not leave secluded Le Sentier simply with the irre-
sistible attraction of all things new. The first purchasers had quickly
discovered that its turning mechanism could be a wonderful toy and
at the same time a welcome topic of conversation on many social
occasions, which were numerous at that time. An event for which the
exuberant term “party” had just come over from America. And what did
people ask to start a short conversation: Quelle heure est-il, Monsieur?
Un moment, Madame, ~ click, voila - click; half past five.

The back of the Reverso, which can be turned upwards with a
quiet but audible “click” and which was originally designed to be a
protective shield like the back of a tortoise, proved to be an ideal place
for imaginative creations: family coats of arms, emblems, stylish mono-
grams or unusual club badges with which membership was proclaimed
in an exclusive manner. But dedications, acknowledgements, declara-
tions of love, mottos or, More prosaically, the wearer's blood group
were also engraved in the gold or steel backs. At the customer's request
the Manufacture provided this service through a Geneva workshop
within a few days of an order being placed.

By now the production of sapphire glass for watch applications
had become operational. Jacques-David LeCoultre was in fact one of
the first in the industry to use this improvement. As of about 1932 a “de
luxe” version of the Reverso was already fitted with sapphire glass.
Although this might appear to deprive the still-young “reversible watch”
of its raison d’étre, its Art Deco shape was highly fashionable and
customers liked the discreet charm of its reverse.

César de Trey had, of course, not forgotten the country to which

he owed the glorious inspiration for the Reverso. Between 1931 and

1932 he therefore sent a first consignment of watches to India via his

English subsidiary. At that time it was already rather a luxury to have a

special taste in watches. A gold Reverso selling for the equivalent of 500

Swiss francs was only affordable for affluent people wishing to show off
their wealth. Some of the British gentlemen who represented the crown
in India and were now wearing the Reverso used to frequent the court
of the maharajah of Karputala, then one of the richest men in India. The
noble prince was said to love his wife, the extremely beautiful maharani,

34

beyond all measure. Exalted romances of this type have a special tradi-
tion in India, as the magnificent Taj Mahal in Agra demonstrates. But the
Indian nobleman was apparently quite taken with himself as well, as he
immediately ordered 50 Reversos with his portrait (and some now-lost
pieces with that of his beautiful wife) on the reverse. He had the models
made by a painter in the tradition of Indian miniatures, in the typical
style in which reigning demigods were depicted at that time in India:
a maharajah apparently hovering on clouds, clad in saffron and sur-
rounded by the insignia of his power.

The Geneva workshop which did this work for de Trey or, as the
case may be, for LeCoultre, was very surprised when it saw the models.
Even with an unusual engraving technique, the customer's wish could
not be fulfilled by engraving. The unique “maharajah Reversos” were
therefore created by using the costly technique of enamel painting. The
watches had to be dismantled for this purpose and the back of the two-
part case had to be fired separately before re-assembly, as the move-
ment obviously would not have withstood the 800° C combustion
temperature without being damaged. A few years ago Jaeger-LeCoultre
was fortunately able to buy back for its museum a steel-and-gold ver-
sion of this small series bearing the image of the maharajah, which the
prince dedicated to important guests. Today the difficult art of enamel

he etgy

Maharan:

has been tound to date
=== PAGE 021 ===
decoration is unfortunately hardly practised any longer because the
specialists are almost all dead. But the maharajah’s idea is still waiting
for an art-minded Reverso owner, who might one day take up the

challenge.

Léon Constantin, our chronicler for the Reverso’s early years, had
heard about this delightful story after he joined the firm in 1936. The
watches he now took with him half way round the world had until then
borne on the face either simply the name “Reverso” or in addition the
company name “LeCoultre” or, in France, “Jaeger”. It was his job to con-
vince customers that they needed a watch which not only displayed the
time but also concealed the time.

The next few years, however, saw a radical change in the firm’s
structures, which - importantly for collectors — affected the Reverso’s
faces: Jaeger-Paris and LeCoultre in Le Sentier merged in 1937 and from
now on traded under the name “Jaeger-LeCoultre”. Jacques-David
LeCoultre took over the “Société Spécialités horlogeres” in the same
year (César de Trey had died shortly before aged 61), which from then
on marketed the joint make. It was therefore only logical that the firm
should be called “Société de vente des produits Jaeger-LeCoultre SA”.
One year later, in 1938, LeCoultre took a shareholding in Vacheron &
Constantin, and the Jaeger-LeCoultre sales organization moved from
Lausanne to the Geneva premises of the watchmaker which had been
founded in 1755 by Jean-Marc Vacheron.

“The first great enthusiasm for the Reverso subsided somewhat in
about 1939 with the outbreak of the Second World War’, Léon
Constantin tells us. The reason was that Swiss watch exports were
restricted, a large proportion of production capacity was needed for
the production of aircraft parts and instruments, many employees were
mobilized and in view of the lean times in prospect people were begin-

ning to save on things which they had otherwise been only too willing to
SYNTHESE spend money on. It was.not a good time for fine, expensive watches.
Even after the end of the terrible war which had devastated half of
Europe production of the Reverso still accounted for only a modest
share of Jaeger-LeCoultre’s orders. Automatic watches such as the

ET DU GourT FRANCAIS Futurematic or, a few years later in the mid-fifties, the legendary Memo-

Vox with alarm clock, were better suited to the spirit of new rationalism

that marked the period of reconstruction. The craze for Art Deco shapes
had been submerged in the upheavals of History. Orders that were
nevertheless received from time to time were met from a fairly large
stock of pre-fabricated cases; nor was there any shortage in Le Sentier of
movements or spare parts for servicing. But Geneva-based Wenger,
which until then had been the exclusive manufacturer of the Reverso
case, not only ran down its production but also - which was worse -
destroyed the costly tools needed to make them,

Quoting once again from Léon Constantin, who lived through
these times: “The fashion had changed, it was not a time for avant-
gardism, Art Deco was regarded as old hat, people wanted to look
towards the future, not back into the past. Even the argument that one’s
monogram could be engraved on the back of the watch was no longer
all that important. What's more, we didn’t have the courage to revive
the old tradition, although now with Coca-Cola coming over from
America the advertising techniques were emerging with which a myth
like that which already surrounded the Reverso could easily have been

72. Atjaeger-LeCoultre in Le Sentier business was deteriorating
as it was in the Swiss watchmaking industry as a whole. The quary
invasion from Japan was just beginning. The end of the mechanica,
watch seemed nigh and the Swiss industry appeared incapable f
coming up with an appropriate answer to the electronic challenge from
the Far East. And the Reverso was securing for itself an honourable by
definitive place in history. No-one in the Vallée de Joux really though,
of reviving itany more.

Itwas 19

One day the telephone rang in the office of the Jaeger- LeCoultre
general manager. The caller was Léon Constantin. He had just returned
froma trip to Milan where he had had a long talk about business Policy
with Giorgio Corvo, the Manufacture’s sole representative for Italy,
Corvo had for years been one of Jaeger-LeCoultre’s most important
representatives abroad, as Italy was already the trendsetter in watch
fashion. In addition, the country was one of the most profitable sales
markets. What customers in Milan, Rome or Turin liked could be sold
successfully all over the world.

As the general manager happened to be out, the secretary took
the call. Constantin asked her to find out how many Reversos were till
in stock at Jaeger-LeCoultre and said he would call back later, which he
did the same day. He was told that 200 steel cases without any move:
ments were in the warehouse. A few days later Corvo called from Milan,
having in the meantime talked again to Constantin: “I'll take them all’

People shook their heads in disbelief in Le Sentier. What did
Signore Corvo want to do with all the old watches? Besides, the 11- and
9-line movements that had last been used were no longer in produc
tion, The Manufacture called back Corvo’s Milan office three times to
make sure he really was serious about his order. Each time he said yes.In
Le Sentier people simply could not believe it. But when the seriousness
of the order could no longer be doubted, they tried to talk him out of
it. twas simply impossible, they said, because the movements were nol
available and in those difficult times it was absolutely inconceivable
to start production again. But the Italian, who was as polite as he wa WA
obstinate, did not give up, for the words of many of his customers wele * os
still ringing in his ears: “Signore Corvo, please bring us back the aS eA
Reverso!”. Moreover, he himself felt the time was again ripe for the

Reverso. Everywhere in Europe people were starting to recreate imita-
tions of Art Deco-style jewellery, furniture and basic necessities. Yet in
Le Sentier the originals were in mothballs!

Giorgio Corvo, the cultivated elderly gentleman who wore a
1930s steel Reverso on his wrist, told us the story with a smile: “In those
days Jaeger-LeCoultre’s collection was far less interesting than it is today,
and the retailers slammed the door in our faces. That was why it was
important to find something to revive the name again”. So he asked
Leon Constantin whether there was something left in Le Sentier with
which old customer contacts could be re-established. By this Corvo
naturally meant the Reverso. After Constantin had inquired and Jaeger-
LeCoultre had shown little inclination to re-invest in the classic watch
that had been considered defunct, Corvo himself drove to Le Sentier.
There he told watchmakers and engineers that he wanted movements
tor the 200 cases. And once again he heard the words: “That's impos-
sible”. Then he took one of the cases with him back to his workshop in
Milan and had fitted into the case a small round movement from the
Manufacture’s current production, instead of the shaped movement
that was out of production. When back in the Vallée de Joux a few
weeks later, he showed the watch to the same people and said: “You
see, it works”. This sorely wounded the engineers’ pride. From then on
they paid great attention to Corvo’s request. They came up with a far
more suitable, oval-shaped movement - which was in fact the precursor
of the one used until today. The 200 watches were fitted with it and sent
to Milan. In less than a month Corvo had sold them all. The Reverso was
back, although at first only in Italy.

But then a new problem arose for the Manufacture. In contrast to
1931 when, fora short time, LeCoultre had not had any suitable move-
ments available for Mr. Chauvot’s revolutionary cases, there was now a
shortage of cases to satisfy the new demand. The old Geneva case-
maker was no longer producing and the complicated tools that were
required had been dismantled. Not even the detailed design and con-
structional drawings could be found. But Giorgio Corvo wanted more
watches for his exclusive clientele everywhere, including ones with gold
cases,

What then followed is probably an unpleasant memory for all
concerned: in Geneva six or seven other case-makers were asked

42

whether they could produce Reverso cases. Almost all of them refused
the complicated assignment. One tried to copy the Reverso using a
model - although this time it was at the Manufacture’s official request!
It took him months to complete a first specimen and enormous sums
of money were invested. The pivoting mechanism proved extremely
difficult to make. At the “Société de vente” in Geneva they were tearing
their hair out at the fresh “development costs” to be paid by the firm.

It took three years until Corvo was able to sell the first “new” gold
Reversos to his best retailers in Italy. It was regarded as a privilege to
obtain one at all. Fashion creators, singers, actors, film-makers and
industrialists were all wearing Reversos. Corvo tells us that Milan’s
largest and most respected retailer, Fiumi, had come up with a novel
idea: whenever a customer purchased and paid fora golden Reverso, he
was told “Just a minute, please”; the shop-owner took a photograph and
stuck it in his “golden album”: Versace, Mina, Ferrari, Agnelli - most of
Italy’s celebrities appear in Fiumi’s album.

Meanwhile back in Le Sentier, in view of the never-ending
problems with the “reconstructed” case, they were quite pleased that
the Reverso’s second spring was confined to Italy for a few years. Under
these circumstances no effort was made at first to interest the other
markets in the watch. The teething troubles and weaknesses in the case
mechanism were simply taking too long for the engineers in Le Sentier
and were likely to damage Jaeger-LeCoultre’s good reputation. So at the
beginning of the eighties Jean Lebet, as technical director and produc-
tion manager at Jaeger-LeCoultre, broached the matter with his staff: the
challenge to the world’s best watchmakers to make the most complex
watch case was taken up: the vigorous line now adopted was “We're
going to make this ourselves”.

At this juncture it is appropriate to mention the justification for
the minor proviso that always has to be made when it is said that
the Reverso has been built “virtually” unchanged for more than sixty
years. For “virtually” means that it has retained al $ typical features such babii

dilicrence: acamie as the pivoting mechanism, the shape and external style elements of
J nen Art Deco. In other words, all the features that constitute its unmistake-

Reverso cases able character. But the more intensively the engineers concerned them-

they found to change. The case designer at Jaeger-LeCoultre was, and

sull is today, Daniel Wild, a perfectionist in the best sense of the word,
The idea that crossed his mind was: the Reverso has been made
unchanged for half a century, let’s now make it so perfect that no-one
need consider changing it for the next fifty years.

A connoisseur can see at a glance the result of the extensive
detailed work trom only a few features: the “old” Reverso appears to be
thicker, the new one flatter and more elegant. In the slightly bulkier
Chauvot design, the horns on the ends of the bottom plate are there-
fore turned slightly downward, so that the watch can fit more snugly to
the arm. If the case is pushed to one side, the two characteristic lateral
hollows can be seen on the bottom plate. The slightly wider central part
I the Manutacture’s in between also forms a marked hollow, This rounding serves as a “bed”
 mordemized tor the case that is slightly curved on either side: it also lends the bottom
supedshy Ure case Romero plate greater bending rigidity. This shape corresponds to the likewise
50 yeas slightly rounded guide slots in the side parts, in which the pivots of the
case run. The old Reverso can only be reversed if itis pushed completely
to one side in the guide slots. This is due firstly to the size of the case,
and secondly to its shape: seen from the side, it is reminiscent of the
spine of a book with only slightly rounded edges. During turning, these
edges require more space, which is why the lateral hollows are provided
in the bottom plate,

case des

The flanks of the new Reverso, on the other hand, are perfectly
round. In any position it can be rotated about its axis ona continuously
and only imperceptibly downwardly curved bottom plate. The striking
differences, when both cases are viewed from the side (the old Reverso
is in fact only one millimetre thicker than the new one) also explain the
various shapes of the winder crowns: on the old Reverso a large-sized,
flat crown that nestles close to the case, whereas the new model has a
small crown which must, of course, project slightly further to allow it to
be handled conveniently. This is another optimal distinguishing feature.

this point: first of all, this winding method is not comfortable because
the case moves in the pivots. Secondly, the convinced advocate of
manually-wound watches is not averse to making the slight: effort
required to pick up his watch at the same hour every day, preferably
in the morning, and to give it new energy with measured movements
of the hand.

But to return to the description of details just begun: the bottom
plate of the old design consists of the carrier plate, and the horns with
the transverse guide slots were welded or soldered firmly to it. In the
new design the horns are attached by two screws each to the sides of
the bottom plate. The guide pins for the pivot mechanism now run ina
straight groove between the two parts. The difference is that, on the
Chauvot case, in order to separate the watch from the support, the two
spring-mounted pivots have to be pushed inwards. On the new
Reverso, two opposite screws are loosened, the horns raised and the
case is separated from the support. As far as the pivoting mechanism is
concerned, both arrangements are very good solutions from the tech-
nical point of view. But as mentioned, the new design has overcome the
drawback that the watch could only be turned in two positions, either at
the left or right-hand stop of the guide groove. Any attempt to do so in
another position placed a strain on Chauvot’s construction, which might
damage the pivots and thus impair the safety of the connection
between the watch and the support.

As described earlier, the case-makers who tried their hand with
the Reverso after the original case-maker Wenger, occasionally had
problems with this part of the pivoting mechanism — to say nothing of
the imitators, Sometimes the case and bottom plate came apart in-
voluntarily because the bottom plate was too weak and might be bent
when subject to extreme stress, which was why Daniel Wild paid great
attention to this point.

In contrast to the old Reverso, on the new version a conical pivot
runs in a likewise-conical groove (see figure). The bottom plate and
watch thus form a mobile but indissoluble connection. As explained
above, in order to separate the two main components of the Reverso,
the two horns have to be partially separated from the carrier plate and

then be raised from the bottom plate. It is no longer possible to bend
the pivols any more either, as the watch with its now-round case sides
can be turned in any position. The most important detail of the Reverso,
i.e, what gives it its charm, had thus been improved and made fail-safe.

The complete manufacture of a Reverso is described and illus-
trated in detail in chapter five. However, mention should be made at this
point of another important difference between the new and the old
Reverso: the internal design of the actual case and the greater protec-
tion this affords from soiling of the movement. Chauvot’s watch was still
designed in the old case-making tradition, i.e. the two halves, the bezel
and the lower part of the case are not fixed to one another by means of
screws or otherwise, but they rest like two half monocoques, snapped
onan inner, very solid chassis. The shaped movement together with the
dial is in turn embedded into this chassis. On the old Reverso this solid
component also contains the four elements of the pivoting mechanism
— the two spring-mounted pivots and the two likewise spring-mounted
balls for latching the case into the side of the bottom plate. These four
elements must, of course, be able to function outside the case. This
requires four openings in the case for the pivoting and latching mecha-
nism alone. In addition, there is the opening for the winding crown and,
obviously, the very narrow gap between the two halves of the case. It
represented a genuine challenge for the Jaeger-LeCoultre engineers
who wanted to protect the Chauvot case from outside influences as
well.

“We realized right from the beginning”, says Daniel Wild, “that
this would become a complex and technically demanding solution, as
we had, of course, to remain as close as possible to the original design,
which had never been planned to be airtight’. The two halves of the
case could easily have been rendered airtight, as could the case. But
what about the four openings for the parts of the pivoting mechanism,
subjected as they are to constant movement and stress? For a while the
designers thought too much in terms of the cause, in keeping with the
principle: “Here there is a hole, so there must also be a seal there”. How
is It possible, for example, reliably to seal a hemisphere tor the latching
mechanism which is pushed slightly into the case whenever the watch is
turned over?

Under these circumstances none of the approaches initially
adopted promised lasting protection. And then at some time the pro-
verbial penny dropped: it was not necessary to make the whole case
perfectly airtight, it sufficed to protect securely the movement and dial,
The solution, as the enlarged drawing shows, is sophisticated and sur-
prising. It also highlights the assertion that the rather unprepossessing
Reverso is fitted with one of the most lavish cases, or to be more accy-
rate, with two.

In the so-called guard ring (5), a kind of second case, is housed the
casing-ring (4) together with the movement. The guard ring with the
built-in movementis fastened by four sealed screws made of corrosion-
resistant beryllium to the upper part of the case (2). A flat seal is pressed
firmly between the bezel and this guard ring. The case bottom (7) is also
firmly attached to the guard ring (5) by four lateral screws, A second,
sophisticated seal (6) between the floor and the guard ring closes off
the movement, which is open on the bridge side, from the rest of the
interior of the case. And there, in the “unprotected” part, are located on
two lateral platelets (10) the mounted individual parts for the pivoting

and latching mechanism, which have access through the case towards
the outside.

This was the solution with which the new Reverso appeared in
1985. Although the successful “facelift” could be seen from the outside,
the numerous other improvements could not. And a small stylistic
change from that era remained an episode, fortunately: for a short time
the Reverso “lost” one of its three characteristic stripes which trim the
case at each end — a recollection of the classic influence on Art Deco.
But the third stripe quickly returned again to its traditional position,

At the Jaeger-LeCoultre premises in Le Sentier workstations were
set up for the production of cases and new tools and machines were
installed. This rejuvenation of the reversible watch was accompanied by
an incursion into the quartz age, but a “Made in Le Sentier” version of
course. The electronic alternative to the hand-winding movement
pleased those Reverso lovers who did not want to rewind their time-
pieces each morning. And “electrification” opened up completely new
Opportunities for models with date or moon-phase displays.

The story of a superlative Reverso to mark the 60th anniversary
began in the spring of 1990 in Le Sentier with the almost “historic”
decision to enlarge for the tirst time the case dimensions in proportion
to the original — by exactly 13%. Sacrilege? On the contrary. This was
the go-ahead for a new, greater age for the Reverso which triggered
the greatest efforts in many of the “Manufacture’s” departments. Once
again Daniel Wild’s creativity as a designer was in demand. Janek
Deleskiewicz, the Jaeger-LeCoultre designer, acted as the “mask crea-

the Reverso. The next few years will bring further watchmaking climaxes,
with the Reverso playing a leading role.

Oscar Wilde, who bequeathed the world many remarkable
pieces of wisdom, once said: “Nothing is as dangerous as being overly
modern. One easily runs the risk of suddenly getting out of fashion”. But
for something that has seen as many years as the Reverso, the concept
of “fashion” lost its threatening undertone long ago. Values like dura-
bility, dependability, timelessness, purity of style and aesthetics come
into their own, as does the endless fascination of its turning movement.

long list. The dead admiral’s heirs would, however, have had tg

But for all of them the watch invented by René-Alfred Chauvot in
1931 came too late ~ centuries too late for some of them. A pity really,

Those bor later fared better. The French film director Claude
Lelouch, for example, who inscribed the back of his Reverso with his
lucky number “13”. Or that British gentleman who, for obvious reasons,
would prefer to remain anonymous because he carries on the back of
his steel Reverso the number of his safe combination, which he would
otherwise forget. Other numbers, which the owner would like to be
valid for ever, sometimes become out of date more quickly than
expected: after certain female body measurements had been engraved
on his watch for the fifth time, a German Reverso owner rightly decided
to purchase a new reversible watch from Le Sentier due to a lack of
space. And a Russian spy once reported that he decorated the back of

his Reverso with the Stars and Stripes by way of a disguise, and carried
microfilms inside.

Two mountaineers who in the thirties reached the summit of the
Eiger via the notorious north face were each rewarded with a gift of a
Reverso that recorded the sporting achievement and the date on the
back, so that their grandchildren could be proud of them. We are also
assured that the wife of another mountaineer later gave her husband an

identical watch, in memory of these “Eiger Reversos”, after he too had
returned safely from the north face,

The Reversos which the Swiss Football Association purchased
from Gubelin to present to the members of the Swiss national football
team after their excellent performance in the football world ¢ hampion-
ship in Paris in 1938 constitute a rarity - with political overtones. Their
victory over the Pan-German team in particular, which the French

accompanied with the battle cry “Ona gagne", raised some eyebrows in
view of the tense political situation in Europe at that time. Even to this
day Sirio Vernal, a member of that winning team, wears the watch with
the inscription “Mit Dank fur die Siege” ‘With thanks for the victories

The unique maharajah Reverso with the artistic enamel image of
the Maharajah of Karputala on the back has already been mentioned. It
is not possible to count the many other Reversos that bear a date of
personal significance, such as one’s wedding date or first romantic date,
the wearer's or his children’s birthday; or a company’s words of grati-
tude to a long-serving employee, the deepest dive or the highest
mountain climbed as a lasting memory on their backs

What does all this prove? Simply that the purchase of a Reverso ts
notan end in itself but the beginning of a long and intimate relationship
between the owner and his watch. For this reason virtually no second-
hand Reversos are available on the market. The engraver’s burin trans-
forms a Reverso from an exceptional but “anonymous” timepiece into a
highly personalized object closely associated with a particular individual
and with his or her life history. Although the Reverso is numbered, like
other exclusive timepieces, a series of numerals inscribed on its case
would not be sufficient to make it exclusive. When it leaves the work-
shop, the back of each Reverso is an empty, highly-polished surface on
which the wearer can indulge in some selt-fulfilment. This is not true ot
any other wristwatch. The creative imagination that may unfold there to
emphasize the wearer's individuality meets a deeply-felt human need.
Depending on the owner's taste and imagination or on the artist’s skill,
the back of a Reverso is an excellent place for anything that has to be
constantly remembered or that characterizes its wearer. This brings us
to the most frequently used technique by which Reverso owners trans-
form their timepieces into an individual piece of jewellery: by engraving
monograms, family coats of arms, club emblems, signs of the zodiac or
other symbols that are highly significant tor them.

TWO LETTERS
LIKE A KEY

Let us now use our imagination to visualize how Stone Age man dis-
covered writing and reading, as journalists and historians were unforty-
nately not on hand at that time.

A fire is glowing in a dark rock cave. Burning branches are crack.
ling, smoke is drifting up to the roof. Around the fireplace are crouched
a few people with long, shaggy hair. One of them feels too hot, stands
up and takes the skin from his shoulders. The small boy opposite looks
attentively at his father, as he removes the piece of clothing, Suddenly
he stares spellbound and frightened at the rock wall behind him, on
which he can see an obscure, shadowy figure which was not there
before, moving in the firelight. Fearfully, the child points at the wall, the
others also look and laugh. The man standing gazes in amusement at his
own shadow formed on the rock behind him and comforts his fright-
ened child. Another adult takes a piece of charcoal from the fire, goes
up to the wall and draws in the outlines of the shadow. This is what the
scene might have looked like some 40,000 years ago when human
beings began to create a written communication system.

Not long afterwards cavemen were painting and scratching in the
rocks the things they had experienced during the day: hunting scenes
and portrayals of family and sex life. These earliest forms of written com-
munication are called ideograms. The ideograms became not only
clearer and more skilful with time, but also more precise and significant.
Soon certain paths were being sketched and the hazards caused by
lurking wild animals or swamps marked on stones. Universal symbols of
life and death completed the primitive system. It is estimated that in
about 6000 BC signs appeared for the first time by which members of
certain clans and ethnic groups displayed their membership of those

sroups. For the first time people sought to set themselves apart from the
crowd.

Two to three thousand years later the highly-organized Semites
in Mesopotamia were developing the first writing system that could be
read — pictography: series of pictures told stories from their everyday
life. This is precisely what is still done today with the help of the film
camera. It was only a small step, even in time, from pictography to the
Egyptians’ hieroglyphs: in about 3000 BC it became possible for the first

TWO LETTERS
LIKE A KEY

time to use written characters, which were sti strongly reminiscent of
the earlier pictograms, to record sounds, or so-called phonemes. The
hieroglyphs became more and more stylized as they developed into a
genuine script and the images grew more abstract. Some 500 years later
the Sumerians’ cuneiform script was the last important intermediate
stage before the creation, in Phoenicia in about 1300 BC, of the first
alphabet with syllables; it contained 22 characters or letters. In this
system only the consonants were written, the vowels were spoken and
the words separated from one another by full stops. They read from
right to left.

The Greeks learnt the system from the seafaring Phoenicians and
expanded it in around the year 1000 to form the first complete alphabet
with consonants and vowels, although they wrote and read from left
to right. The characters were to change several times during the few
centuries that elapsed until the classical Greek alphabet, as we know
it, finally took shape. Greek travellers, merchants and settlers brought
their communication system with them when they came to Italy. There
the Etruscans adopted the method and eventually developed from it
the Latin alphabet. At the foot of the famous column dedicated to the
Emperor Trajan in Rome stands what is in effect the “yardstick” of the
whole of western typography: an inscription chiselled in marble in large
capital letters became the prototype of all subsequent scripts. All the
variants that form the basis of western writing culture are derived from
the “capitalis monumentalis” of Trajan’s column: in the first centuries
after Christ rustic capitals and italic script, which was better suited to
writing, then the Merovingians’ uncial writing, Carolingian small-letter
writing and primitive Gothic script in the 8th century.

During the Renaissance people tended to hark back to Ancient
times again after the barbarism of the Middle Ages; and when Guten-
berg invented printing with mobile letters, writing, influenced by Italy,
moved closer to the original script on Trajan’s column in Rome:
so-called humanistic script, together with Gothic script, became the
predominant typography. Roman script, which was developed later, is
even more strongly based on the original Roman alphabet. But all the
subsequent great upheavals in the history of ideas also influenced ways
of writing and printing. Even the computer age produced its own

machine typeface. Yet never before has the range of scripts been as
great as itis today. Apart from the technically perfect image, writing, as it
has developed from prehistoric picture language, remains the most
important means of recording and depicting reality. And this brings us
very close to the monogram.

Writing has retained its original function - viz. to “depict” some-
thing - most strongly where it refers to a particular person — by his name.
The main function of the surname is to differentiate an individual from
others by describing him more precisely. Many surnames have geo-
graphic origins, most of them are derived from former occupations. This
applies to the Frankes and Woods, Meiers, Millers, Smiths and Boulan-
gers. Forenames are used to distinguish between people in the small
family group of people with the same surname. The complete name
thus stands for an individual, unique person, who naturally sets great
store by not being confused with someone else. The name is the prime
and personal differentiating feature in the crowd, in dealings with
government, companies or society, as any salesman or psychologist
knows: nothing breaks down the barrier between himself and a stranger
more quickly than addressing the latter by name.

But the form of the name abbreviated with the initials, the mono-
gram (from the Greek monos and gramma ~ a single letter) already has
an age-old tradition. The monogram was used on contracts and docu-
ments until well into the Middle Ages in place of a person’s full signature
or title. On coins for example, it designated the seigneur having the right
of coinage. A well-documented example of a typical monogram is
Charlemagne’s signature. The monogram was written on a contract by
the scribe of his state chancellery, with the letters K,R, L,S, tor “Karolus”
arranged in the shape of a cross. The emperor “completed” the valid
signature simply by joining up the four letters with a diamond-shaped
box. Apart from this, he did not know how to write.

But the monogram became even more important in the late
Middle Ages: craftsmen and artists already had good reason to protect
their works from possible forgeries and imitations. They did so with
so-called house marks that consisted mainly of the initials of their name.
Albrecht Diirer signed his pictures with what was probally the best-

e and “completed

his signature by joining up the

TWO LETTERS
LIKE A KEY

known monogram of this type. Many artists used only the initial of their
surname for this purpose, while others used three or more letters,
depending on the number of their forenames; others again added to
the monograms vivid symbols or the year in which the work was
created. The intention was always to prove the originality of the work
and to create a link with the maker.

Today the monogram has lost nothing of the fascination jt
possessed in those far-off days. In watchmaking in particular, com.
Panies’ logos are often monograms. One of the finest examples in this
industry was created after the merger of Jaeger and LeCoultre, Jaeger-
LeCoultre’s logo is composed of the first initials “J” and “L” of the two
lirms’ names and at the same time represents the stylized pallet of a
watch. What better way of expressing one’s identity and trade so conci-
sely and elegantly?

A practice used by firms, artists, master-craftsmen and even
whole nations to express their identity in a few initials really comes into
its own for each individual. The alphabet consists of 26 letters; by com-
bining only two letters, 676 different permutations are available. These
pairs of letters are like a key which unlocks the personality behind the
Monogram. “Nomen est omen’, the ancient Romans used to say - our
name influences our destiny. Some monograms have even become
famous without being promoted by the person concerned: in the late

fifties and sixties “BB” became indissociable with the French idol Brigitte
Bardot.

Engraving the Reverso with a personal monogram was, from the
outset, part of the service provided by a Geneva workshop in the early
thirties within a few days of the watch being purchased. Customers
were able to choose from a dozen different types of characters, and
they could, of course, also have special orders fulfilled. The perfection
and imagination with which monograms were designed and executed

in those years, quite in keeping with Art Deco style, are still a source of
fascination to this day.

The letters fashioned from the steel or gold stood out against
a rectangular, square or rhombic background of dark varnish. But cus-
tomers could also choose monograms in “negative technique’, in

which the countersunk letters were afterwards filled with a special red
or black lacquer. The craftsmen capable of performing such work have,
unfortunately, become few and far between Anyone who knows a
good engraver capable of working not only with an engraving machine
but by hand with the traditional burin can consider himself lucky: the
Manufacture in Le Sentier, for example, whic h has engraving ili done
by hand by a master-craftsman. But many of the Manufacture $ repre-
sentatives abroad also co-operate with such specialists, who turn an
anonymous Reverso into an individual “watch personality” as soon as
possible and in accordance with the customer's wishes. Indeed, this is
the source of its incomparable charm. The new Reverso “Grande Taille”
with its correspondingly larger back almost invites an engraver to take
this sophisticated “minor art form” to extremes. Our ancestors would
have appreciated the fact that one can display not only one’s name but
also one’s “arms” on a Reverso: this is what a personal coat of arms
symbolizes. Admittedly, not everyone's family tree contains such noble
trappings and not every family coat of arms was hard earned in battle by
a valiant ancestor. However that may be, the Reverso bears them both
with nobleness and dignity.

HERALDRY FOR
BEGINNERS

There were no limits to the imagination when selecting the motifs
that were later to decorate “bourgeois” coats of arms. Virtually every-
thing was possible for so-called “common” figures ~ as Opposed tg
noble ones like the lion and eagle - provided there was some connec.

tion with the bearer or with what he wished to express on his coat of

arms. Stars, the sun, moon, Mountains, castles, unicorns OF Cals, swords
and ploughshares, fish, birds, etc. featured particularly in family and
municipal coats of arms. Coats of arms were often transferred from ono

family to another.

Coat-ol-arms dealers, who were referred to by the French rather
disparagingly as “blackbird dealers”, sought, espec ially in the 18th and
19th centuries, to supply “nouveau riche” families with their own
emblems. In many cases former coats of arms or membership of a family
that already bore a coat of arms, could be traced by means of registers
and family trees. In other cases a new symbol was simply created which
displayed a link with a tamily’s origin or traditional occupation. Some-
timesa tamily’s slightly modified coat of arms was taken over by another
family with a similar-sounding name. In England people tried to make
heraldry a little more orderly by drawing up at an early stage registers
intended to prevent coats of arms with the same content being allo-
cated to two or more families.

The practical application of the “bourgeois” coat of arms was a
matter of self-portrayal: it could be painted on coaches or affixed to the
walls of houses; later on it could be printed on writing paper or be cut
into the stone of a signet ring. Since 1931 it can be engraved on the back
of a Reverso - and it has been, hundreds of times.

Needless to say, every football club and rugby team and all post-
men, policemen and soldiers bear, on their sports gear and uniforms
respectively, an insignia which, like that of the knights of old, identifies
them as genuine members of a specific group. The reasons are the
same, be it the police, the military or two different sports teams.

The need for differentiation in people’s ordinary day-to-day lives,
on the other hand, has shifted the emphasis more to status symbols
Discreet status symbols are the more gratifying kind and here the

Reverso owner has a distinct advantage by being able modestly to
conceal his coat of arms or to show it off proudly, depending on the

circumstances

qualities of clarity, creativity and imagination are absolutely essential, as
is a brief attempt to gain an insight into the art of engraving, which,
unfortunately, has become rare nowadays.

We are talking here not about industrial engravers who can only
operate numerically or matrix-controlled machines, but about one of
the oldest trades in the world, which came into being more or less when
man discovered metals. Basically, the descendants of the skilful Egyp-
tians who lived at the time of the Pharaohs or of the famous goldsmiths
of Troy do not work any differently today than their ancestors, and
the tools of their trade are more or less the same: chasing tools, files,
hammers, chisels and burins. Whether it was the splendid armour of a
medieval knight, the richly-decorated dagger of a great mogul or an
adorned late-Baroque goblet, goldsmiths and engravers ~ who for a

Stone Age form of the very first tool, the hand axe. Small chisels usually
have a round wooden handle designed to fit the hand. The cutting
edges are driven through the material solely by the pressure of the h
The engraver, who usually has a dozen or more such burins with differ.
ent cutting edges in front of him on the table, grinds his instrument him.
self with a grinding stone, then an emery stone and finally with a fine
oilstone. The quality of his work depends on the meticulousness of his
preparation; for example, only ifa burin has a spotlessly polished cut
edge can it create the wider inner surface of

When Jacques-David LeCoultre decided to make the Reverso and
René-Alfred Chauvot was pondering the design plans in Paris, they Were
either still a twinkle in their parents’ eyes or they were just in Nappies
five of Jaeger-LeCoultre’s employees always celebrate their birthdays
together with the Reverso, as they were born in 1931. They haye all
spent most of their working lives in and with the “Manufacture” jn le
Sentier. What are 60 years? Almost the span of a human lifetime. This isa
yardstick by which we can measure and express time most graphically
which is why the rightful place for the names of the “co-celebrators" is
at the beginning of a chapter that recalls the first 60 turbulent years of
the Reverso’s existence:

Claude Berney is head of Technical Quality Control and Methods, which com-
prises inspection and verification of all components and movements
and final testing of watches. A Swiss citizen, Mr. Berney has been with
laeger-LeCoultre since 1952,

André Hugonnet, who comes from a vill ige near Le Sentier, has been working
for Jaeger-LeCoultre as a mechanical engineer since 1951.

Livio di Lorenzo came to the Vallée de Joux trom Rome in 1961, stayed on there
and has collaborated in Jaeger-LeCoultre’s Components Production
Department on, among other t gs, production of the Reverso.

Maria Meszaros, was born in Budapest but is now a Swiss citizen. She has been
loyal to the “Manufacture” since 1957, working in Components Produc-
tion for the Atmos and in the Heat Treatment Department.

Raymonde Martin comes from Besancon in France but has been living in the
Valley since 1968. She works in the Movement Production Department
and is thus also involved in the manufacture of the Reverso calibres

us permissiveness, the emancipation of
women in everyday life, the “bob” hairstyle and the Vvamp’s strapless
evening dress, the skirt that ended above the knees and garish nail

the first non-stop flight across the Atlantic. The people living in those
days experienced so many “firsts” that the future for them was
constantly turning into an electrifying present. Life in the Metropolises
was lived at full tilt and everybody wanted to jump onto the whirling
merry-go-round. The French painter Fernand Léger described it at the
time with the words: “tis certain that the development of the means of
locomotion and their speed have something to do with the new vision”

Bugatti or Panhard, the window of

an express-train compartment or the
