WITH THE REVERSO
THROUGH THE DECADES

The screen stars had a lasting influence on the new moral Code
and spread the illusion of the carefree, beautitt e, Marlene Dietrich
was appearing in small night-clubs as Lola in the “Blue Angel” in 1939
The anti-war film “All Quiet on the Western Front’, based on the nove
by Erich Maria Remarque, or Fritz Lang's “M”, showed the cinema's
other dimension, as an effective instrument of social criticism, Increas-
ingly often, totalitarian regimes resorted to banning films and, logically
enough, used the cinema for their own propaganda purposes. The
phenomenon of the star cult and of mass hysteria was witnessed for the
first time when the celebrated Rudolph Valentino died and hundreds of
thousands of people turned his funeral into a show. But the personality
cult method was also being exploited by the new political upstarts, the
Fascist and Communist leaders of the day. At the same time a new type
ot beaming hero was striding onto the world stage, to the audience's
applause: the person who was capable of riding the tiger of the techno-
logical age; this tiger might take the form of a racing car being driven
around the Brand’s Hatch circuit, or that of the aeroplane with which
Charles Lindbergh crossed the Atlantic for the first time non-stop in 33
hours. Art and the crafts were discovering the cool charm of the
machine and creating an aesthetics of the machine that expressed
speed and power. The 8reat passenger liners of the age like the “Nor- a
mandie” and the “Queen Mary” were regarded in the battle for the ay |
coveted Blue Ribband as national declarations of faith in unlimited
progress. Sport became a socially-recognized leisure-time occupation

and - at the Berlin Olympic Games for the first time — an extension of
the political conflict by other means.

America, the land of unlimited opportunities which had moved
closer to the old continent again as a result of its engagementin the First
World War, was shooting upwards with the incredible 1,050-foot high
Chrysler Building. The quintessence of Art Deco architecture was sur-
passed by 200 feet only a few months later by the 1,250-foot Empire
State Building. The architects based their self-confidence on months of
pie Opinii successful testing with models of the two skyscrapers in wind-tunnels.
" The wind-tunnel, that ingenious device which enables Nature to be
Aiitlow tron depicted as a predictable variable, was a typically American expression
of modernity. This veneration for streamlined shapes meant that even
the most trivial consumer objects, such as toasters, cigarette-lighters or

There is no more fitting way to introduce the history of the Manulacture,

which to a certain extent is also the history of the Va than with this

anecdote: one July evening in 1990 some employees are mecting alter

the Valley work in the Jaeger-LeCoultre canteen, a rather plain room in an old

o— annex on rue de la Golisse. The chairman of the board of directors,
and its inhabitants. ‘

Mr. Gunter Blumlein, ts present, as is the managing director, Mr. } lenty-

John Belmont and, as the centre of attention at the small rec eption with

- open sandwiches and a glass of wine, a modest-looking, grey-suited

- man who ts visibly bewildered at all the fuss being made about him. Hy

is the Manutacture’s chief accountant, Mr. Blattmer, who is retiring this

summers evening after reaching the official retirement age. Nothing

unusual, apart trom the kind gesture with which the company is saying

thank you” to one of its senior employees after many years’ loyal

service. But then Mr. Blattmer tells the astonishing story of how, as

a young man, he had come to the Valley by bicycle from Northern

Switzerland, originally intending only to spend a tew weeks’ holiday

JkeoreneGouas here and to learn French at the same time. He was to stay with Jaeger-

cas LeCoultre tor 42 years, nsing trom trainee to chief accountant. What

years they were, including periods of deep recession when the Manu-

facture’s very survival was at risk, with some months when he was
unable to pay

The Manufacture,

spnthese anyone's salary, including his own. This part of the
company’s history — when told as an anecdote — sounds more harmless
than it actually was. People like Mr. Blattmer and many others who have
et | remained loyal to their “factory” in hard times, are among the incon-

spicuous heroes hardly mentioned by any chronicler.

de la technique suisse

du gout francais

What the anecdote reveals, above all, is something else: not

everyone who comes to the Vallée de Joux stays there — that would not
Jibei AitCous . be to the liking of its withdrawn yet self-confident inhabitants. But
anyone who has been to the Valley leaves behind at least a small part
of himself and feels a melancholy yearning for the peacefulness of this
unique region, The visitor leaves this valuable feeling somewhere down
on the broad floor of the Valley, between the gentle, green mountain
| slopes, knowing he will re-experience it on every visit.

tant chapter in Switzerland’s watchmaking history. The LeCoultres
those notoriously modest “genius watchmakers”, have been hel Jing to

write that history ever since the 18th century. Even though the name of
the “Vallee de Joux’ can be traced back to an old French word for
jorest’, the inhabitants were never backwoodsmen, just withdrawn
and sometumes cut off for months on end when the icy North wind
blows enormous snowdrifts over the Valley’s few roads, something
that can still happen today at any time in winter

Anyone approaching the Valley for the first time by car along
the winding road uphill from Vallorbe throt gh the forest has the IMpres-
sion tora few miles that at any moment the road might suddenly end
with a sign saying “No point in continuing = the inhabited world ends
here”. The traveller imagines he will have to drive on his own through
the forest for days until, having passed the highest point, he notices a
bluish-green expanse of water glinting in the sun between the tops of
the pine trees. Itis the small but pic turesque Lake Brenet, which looks
though it had been poured out between the trees from a giant water-
ing-can. This is only the modest approach to the Va ey, with its much
larger Lac de Joux. Where the two lakes almost meet is the hamlet of
Le Pontat the foot of the Dent de Vaulion, a 4,860 fc vot-high mountain
that towers above the Valley and closes it off to the north-east, like
the petrified crest of a wave in a primeval sea. But the traveller sh nul
not look round yet, as the first time this unique view has to be seen

Les Charbonniéres. Today only the name of the small village = whose
house facades are reintorced with corrugated iron — reveals that its earl
inhabitants once earned their living as charcoal burners and thus as fuel
suppliers to the iron industry around Vallorbe. These old-fashioned
craftsmen, who first processed the wood from the area’s extensive
forests into charcoal and later on themselves increasingly turned to
iron-working trades, are regarded — not without reason — as the early
ancestors of watchmakers in the Valley. What has been handed down
is the unusual receptiveness to all technological innovations of the
Valley’s inhabitants, the “combiers”, as they call themselves. The pub-
sher Valentin Philibert, an excellent connoisseur of the Valley, explains
this apparent contradiction as follows: “People here have always nur-
tured scientific and technological progress, which was intended to
release people from the arduousness of purely manual work. The “mira-
cle” that occurred in the Vallée de Joux may be explained by this enthu-
siastic yet respectful view of progress as something heaven-sent and not
the work of evil. There is a characteristic example of this in the annals:
when the railway line was to be built through the Valley to Le Brassus at
the end of the last century, the villages on either side of the long, narrow
lake fought bitterly to have the line built on their side. Les Charbon-

nieres, Le Séechey, Le Lieu and Le Sentier on the north-western side
habitants finally won.

king Vallorbe

From Les Charbonnieéres the road leads, at a certain distance from
the railway line, through the midst of splendid, lush meadows and along
the great Risoux forest to Le Séchey. Likewise, the place name indicates
how its inhabitants once earned their living: the freshly-cut wood wes
dried there (from the French word “secher”, to dry). When driving on to
Le Lieu, the visitor should be sure not to miss the view to the left of the
small, very picturesque Lake Ter. Here in the background the Dent de
Vaulion can be admired properly for the first time, with its tooth-like

peak of Jurassic limestone. It is a picture-book panorama - on a clear
= day.

isolated farmsteads to Le Sentier, which means “the path” or “the foot-
path”, Just before a sloping left-hand bend the place-name sign appears
for the first ime, together with the coat of arms - a toothed wheel and
tlute. After a sharp right-hand bend the “fac tory”, as the locals call it, can

continue the journey around Lac de Joux, past the Manufacture and to

open-air theatre formed by the Valley may be admired in all its splen-
dour: with the lake, the small villages all around and the steep peak of
the Dent de Vaulion, the guardian of the Valley, forming a majestic back-
drop. The visitor cannot help thinking of the “tooth of time” - the ambr-
guity is obvious. Should the visitor have enough time (whata question in

terrible clatter when driven over: their purpose is to prevent the herds
of cows which graze everywhere here in summer from Wandering off
their fenced pastures and onto the road. Of course, the mountain can
also be climbed on foot. Not only can one enjoy a formidable cheese
fondue in the restaurant just below the summit in summer, with me-
ringues and mountains of fresh cream for dessert, rounded off with a
throat-burning, locally distilled spirit, but when at the summit the whole
Valley and lake are spread out at one’s feet, like a fairy-tale landscape.
On the opposite side Lake Geneva can be seen 40 miles to the south,
shining golden in the evening sun and behind it Mont Blanc soaring up
towards the clouds, From the Dent de Vaulion the wide world seems
very close to the Vallée de Joux again. Perhaps it is sufficient to remind
oneself of this from time to time, so as to be able to put up with - and
enjoy ~ the isolation. The Valley's 6,000 permanent inhabitants, most
of whom work for the 40 or so watch and components manufacturers
in the Valley, probably feel the same way.

Anyone spending the night in the Valley can find comfortable
accommodation in one of the well-appointed hotels in Le Brassus, Le
Pont or Le Sentier. The conversation at the bar or over dinner almost
automatically comes round to watches and watchmaking. People know,
greet and talk to one another even if the other person works for the
competition as a watchmaker, timer or manager. It is. a small world in
the Valley and everyone knows that their opposite number is just as
proud of belonging to the watchmaking elite based on the narrow strip
of land between Le Pont and Le Brassus. An old watchmaker from
another watch factory might tell how one day his manager, when mak-
Ing an inventory, came across a handful of splendid movement-blanks
fora Grande Complication which had been lying around in a drawer for
ages, and how he decided to have these mechanical masterpieces
assembled in pocket-watch size by his best experts. Then someone asks
with a wink whether these pieces, which today are never sold for less
than SFr. 200,000, might not have been supplied many decades ago by
LeCoultre. Mr. Schmid, for example, the owner of Hotel de la Lande in
Le Brassus where the Valley's “watch aristocracy” stays, knows a
tremendous number of anecdotes about watches and watchmakers.
He himself is. a kind of “quick-change’ artist similar to the Reverso itself.
He has adjusted to his ever-changing customers in an original way: if

Anyone who goes through the wrought-iron gate of Jaeger-LeCoultre’s
premises at no. 8 rue de la Golisse Is watched over by the firm’s founder
— from a plaque on the opposite side of the street. Antoine LeCoultre,
Jaeger-LeCoultre his son Elie and Elie’s son Jacques-David have been immortalized on a
commemorative plaque in the small garden opposite the main gate
From their vantage point the three great names of the LeCoultre watch-

religious war in the making dynasty can still keep an eye on the Manufacture, even though
7 they died a long time ago. This also applies in the figurative sense, as
their spirit has never left the firm.

begins with a
16th century.

Just behind the entrance, opposite the reception, there is a taste-
= a — tully furnished room. On an old desk stands a row of worn leather-
bound books: 19th-century scientific tomes about physics and chem-
istry, but also geography. Between them stands a volume containing
plays by the great Enlightenment writer Voltaire, Anyone curious
enough to open a small door on the desk will find an old telescope in
a leather case, along with writing utensils and an ink-pot. It is easy to
imagine the person who used these objects: an open-minded man with
an inquiring intellect, interested in both natural science and the arts, but
who also lived close to Nature. It may be that the objects were arranged
that way over the years. Nevertheless the drawing room, that is, the
room with the furniture, inventions, awards and books of the legendary
founder of the firm, Charles-Antoine LeCoultre (1803 - 1881), is not used
The Manutacture’s “drawing purely as a museum, despite the showcases with the precious ma-
room” On the old desk stand chines, such as the millionometer, which LeCoultre invented and the fact

“blancs”, which were made in-house at the turn of the century, would
mos be an asset to any watch museum. But the polished wooden table in the
the epitome of watch culture middle of the room with the matching chairs is not merely collecting
dust, rather it invites the visitor to sit down, to have a discussion

is almost palpable, new developments are discussed and important
company decisions taken, while the red light burns outside: Please do
not disturb. But visitors from all over the world also often gain their first, the “tactory”, as the premises

image conveyed is not deceptive: tradition is not just a hackneyed word

These values are deeply-rooted in the founder's age: his best

Pierre LeCoultre was the first to come to Switzerland

It all began in the 16th century with an act of abject barbarism and in-
tolerance. The victims - apart from the LeCoultres — were hundreds of
thousands of French Protestants. The break that Luther and Calvin had
made with the Pope and the Roman Catholic Church spread through
Europe like a forest fire and also reached France. There too the “Hugue-
nots” — the collective term for French Protestants: Calvinists, Lutherans
and others ~ interfered with the close links between the State and the
mighty Roman Catholic Church. The basic principle: Cuius regio, eius
religio — the ruler determines his subjects’ religion — was the result of the
Peace of Augsburg in 1555 and made the religious freedom of the indi-
vidual a hazardous business. Jews were forced into ghettos, heretics and
so-called witches everywhere were burnt at the stake. In France the
Reformation teaching of Calvin in particular gained ground among all
segments of the population, despite being combated by Francois | and
Henry Il. But notwithstanding the Edict of St. Germain (1562), which was
intended to guarantee the Huguenots allied with the House of Bourbon
and sections of the higher nobility the freedom to practise the religion

136

of their choice outside the cities, they subsequently became directly
involved in the political power struggle on several occasions. When, for
example, the Protestant King Henry IV converted to Catholicism in 1593
for political reasons, the Huguenots were leit with only a tew places of
refuge - Montauban, Cognac and La Rochelle, for example. After Henry
IV's death his wife, Maria de Medici, and later also Richclieu and Louis
XIV, resumed the persecution of the Huguenots. In the wake of the
Huguenot wars, which lasted until the early years of the 18th century,
hundreds of thousands of French Protestants migrated, mainly to the
already-reformed regions of Europe: Switzerland, the Netherlands,
Britain and Germany. As a bastion of Calvinism situated close to the
French border and a first rallying point for the refugees, Geneva played
an important part in these distressing journeys.

One day around the middle of the 16th century Pierre LeCoultre
arrived in Geneva from Lizy-sur-Ourcg (Seine-et-Marne), together with
many others who had had to abandon their home country. In 1558 he
was granted civic rights before he moved on with his family to the Vallée
de Joux. A commemorative plaque on a large detached house west
of Le Sentier, which dates from 1568, names P. LeCoultre as the first
occupant. It was also the house in which Antoine, who was later to
found the firm, was born. The tact that watchmaking was already prac-
tised in this building well before the firm's foundation can still be seen -
despite numerous renovations — trom the row of small watchmakers’
windows on the north side of the house The square named Fontaine du
Planoz previously belonged to Le Lieu.

“SECRET NUMBER 1

The municipal archives and the families’ written records, which
historian Francois Jequier examined in depth during the research for
his book (“De la forge a la manufacture horlogére”, Lausanne, 1983)
show Pierre LeCoultre to have been the far-spreading root of one of the
Valley’s great family communities. This is also true in a social and politi-
cal sense. The LeCoultres made an exceptional contribution, in various
positions and offices, to the structural and industrial development of the
Valley. They first displayed their skilled craftsmanship and receptiveness
to innovation in the manufacture of knives and tools, as the life of
blacksmith and toolmaker Abraham-Joseph LeCoultre (1746-1814)
testifies. Another branch of the family specialized in the production of
complex mechanical musical boxes. In the 18th century the gift for
manual skills resulted in the manufacture of watch components.
Jacques-David Il, one of Abraham-Joseph LeCoultre’s eight children,
took over the running of his parents’ forge in Le Sentier, while his two
brothers and several sons also set up craft enterprises to manufacture
knives, keyboards or key valves for musical instruments and — already -
toothed wheels. The village coat of arms depicts both the last two
products: a toothed wheel and a flute

The founder: Charles-Antoine LeCoultre

Charles-Antoine, one of Jacques-David’s sons, began an apprentice-
ship, which he supplemented with his own research and experiments,
in the field of steel processing in particular. Metallurgical processing of
watch components is still carried out in a very similar way in the Manu-
facture’s present-day forge — which we shall be visiting in the next
chapter. Moreover, young Antoine was particularly busy manufacturing
toothed wheel gears and - like many other famous watchmakers of the
time — perfecting the escapement system of watches. In 1833, at the age
of 30, Antoine left his father’s business and, together with his brother
Ulysse who was ten years his junior, laid the foundation for his own
watch factory. This meant he first specialized in what he did best: more
rational manufacture of toothed wheels and pinions. He had already
acquired the necessary knowledge in earlier years. The first attempts
to manufacture milled steel pinions date back to 1826. To do this,
LeCoultre used so-called profile-milling machines for the first time,
which give the teeth their final shape in a single operation, whereas
previously several operations had been necessary. He also developed
the machinery required to make the m ng machines himself. Their

swift success vindicated the two young entrepreneurs. But Antoine
LeCoultre, as described by historian Francois Jequier in the light of his
research, took little interest in sales and marketing, As a gifted handy-
man and inventor, he devoted most of his energy to developing new
machines and products. Thus in 1844 he built - initially tor his own use
the famous millionometer, with which metric measurement began to
be accepted as an authoritative standard in watchmaking and which set
a new level of hitherto unattainable measuring accuracy: one millionth
of a metre or one thousandth of a millimetre. In those days, however, it
was not customary to sell tools as “branded products” under a company
name. In Switzerland patent protection for intellectual property was
available only from 1888, which was why many watchmaking patents
were registered in France. LeCoultre never patented his millionometer,
however. It was to belong to everyone who had dedicated themselves
to fine watchmaking. Today — ina slightly modernized form — it stands
on many workbenches in every watchmaking firm.
LeCoultre’s firm was expanding and the original craft business
was developing into a small factory with several dozen employees. As
mentioned above, the best employees lived together with their

The next generation: Elie and the brothers

New premises had to be built on and machinery purchased, Antoine
LeCoultre, who was gradually withdrawing from the firm's manage-
ment, had long become the biggest employer in Le Sentier and the sur-
rounding area. From then on his three sons Elie, Paul and Benjamin,
together with another partner from outside the family, who was to quita
few years later, now staked everything on expansion and surprised the
watchmaking world with ever smaller and more complex movements:
they quickly became the leading maker of movements with quarter-
hour and minute repeaters. They made chronographs and calendars
and largely shaped the reputation the Vallée de Joux already enjoyed
as the cradle of complicated watchmaking specialities. Many of the

movements were taken to Geneva on foot in rucksacks when the Valley
was still snowbound in spring. There they were assembled, encased and
helped to enhance the reputation of many a world-renowned watch-
maker. In the meantime LeCoultre was indisputably developing into the
“secret no. 1” and the three brothers in Le Sentier were investing a great
deal of money in the modernization of their equipment. The purchase
of a steam engine in 1883, the introduction of electric lighting and more
rational manufacturing methods allowed a threefold increase in produc-
tion, initially without having to expand the workforce significantly. Not
until around 1890 did sales rise to over half a million francs, when the
workforce also grew sharply, first to 400 and then to almost 500. No less
than 125 different calibres were made at that time for a wide range of
customers, which of course meant the series were small and sometimes
tiny.

Elie LeCoultre, the “primus inter pares” in the three brothers’ part-
nership, was reputed to be good at figures. All over the Jura new facto-
ries making watch movements and watches had sprung up in recent
years. Elie realized that in the face of growing competition on the move-
ment-blanks market, with a ruinous price policy at the turn of the
century, his own product range had to be re-structured. As the son of
his famous father, he decided on the right course of action in this diffi-
cult market situation: production of completely-assembled and timed
top-quality movements, frequently backed up with surprising watch-
making innovations. Elie LeCoultre was already thinking of manufactur-
ing complete watches. At the same time the flourishing firm was con-
verted into a joint-stock company.

The 20th century LeCoultre: Jacques-David

In 1897, at the age of 22, Elie’s son Jacques-David (1875 - 1948) joined
the firm after undergoing training in the region’s watchmaking work-
shops and attending the Geneva watchmaking school. Young, dynamic
and very ambitious, he motivated the watchmakers to perform ever
greater achievements: in 1903 LeCoultre presented the world’s slim-
mest pocket-watch movement - only 1.38 millimetres in height. It was
sensational for those days and exceptional even today. A movement
with chronograph measuring only 2.8 millimetres in height and finally a
repeater measuring all of 3.2 millimetres in height. At the time LeCoultre
was forging close business links with Patek Philippe in Geneva and
Edmond Jaeger in Paris. In Geneva he also set up a workshop of his own
for the assembly and timing of high-quality movements and ultra-slim
calibres. Jean Lebet, later to become managing director in Le Sentier,
knew Jacques-David LeCoultre and came into close contact with the
Manufacture in the early years, both through his uncle Jacques, who for
a long time was in charge of the Geneva workshop, and through his
father Paul, who managed Jaeger’s watch department in Paris. “He
was in the workshops every day”, says Jean Lebet of Jacques-David
LeCoultre, “from time to time he simply went into the factory, picked up
some components and checked the quality for himself. If he heard
something was wrong, whether in production or in the employees’ rela-
tions with each other, he attended to the matter personally”.

But unlike his grandfather Antoine, Jacques-David LeCoultre
spent a great deal of time not only upgrading his production but also
selling his high-quality movements. He had something to offer, and he
knew it. It was also he who gradually built up a direct sales network,
cutting out the middleman w herever possible; its efficient structure is
still one of the Manufacture’s strengths even today. Jacques-David, who
had now become one of the biggest Swiss watchmakers, was Culltivati 2
close personal contacts with his business partners, who later included
the naturalized Englishman César de Trey, whom we have already met in
connection with the Reverso, an extremely efficient man who searched
everywhere for good watch ideas in order to market them worldwide
But it was above all the “French connection” with Edmond Jaeger
in Paris which was to prove particularly significant for LeCoultre in the
early years of the new century. Jaeger, the “state-appointed watch-
maker” from Alsace, who had initially specialized in the manufacture of
marine chronometers, was co-operating closely with Cartier. Jaeger was
thus also opening the door - through Cartier - to the French luxury
watch market tor the excellent movement-maker trom Le Sentier. But in
addition to the prestigious, sometimes complicated pocket-watch
movements, LeCoultre was also making for Jaeger, in another branch of
his modern factory, large quantities of the tachometers, mileometers,
clocks or rev counters that now had to be built into cars and aircraft,
because until about 1920 the important French partner only produced
small quantities himselt, as Jean Lebet tells us. With the abrupt end of
the “Belle Epoque” and the outbreak of the First World War, the manu-

The watch outgrows the trouser pocket

War, that barbaric hiatus in the co-existence of Central European
nations, not only brought untold suffering and economic misery ~ tt also
changed people's habits: however macabre it may sound, modern
warfare requires accurate timekeeping. The pocket watch had proved
to be impractical and a nuisance in the trenches. The first “war calibres

to be worn on the wrist, mostly small pocket-watch movements in
converted cases, often still with a cover or protective grille in front ot
the glass, paved the way for the imminent triumph of the wristwatch

Jacques-David LeCoultre would have been a poor entrepreneur
had he failed to recognize the trend and not adjusted to it. 1926 saw
the launch of the Duoplan, a patented two-tier, ultra-accurate baguette
movementin a steel case. It was made jointly with Jaeger The increased
accuracy was achieved by placing the barrel and the movement on one
tier and above it a balance dimensioned across the width of the whole
movement on the second tier (viewed trom the bridge side), The Duo-
plan was sold under the company names of both Jaeger and LeCoultre.
Jean Lebet assumes that the plain steel case, which could also be set
with diamonds in the contemporary style (typically Art Deco), was first
designed and made in Paris. Two unique features characterized the
Duoplan at that time: first, LeCoultre was offering retailers for the first
time a complete “replacement motor” for the watch, a reacy-assem-
bled, regulated movement fitted with dial and hands. During the
warranty period the movement could be replaced ina few minutes tree
of charge. The old movement that had been remo ed was returned to
the Manufacture and repaired or overhauled. But the service did not
stop there: the watch was insured with Lloyds of London ex-works
against all risks such as thett, loss, destruction or irreparable damage!

in Le Sentier put into practice an inspired idea from England: the “Com-

Atmos, the low-energy marvel - since 1936

In 1936 Jacques-David LeCoultre reached another watchmaking mile-
stone. The comparison is also justified in terms of dimensions: in that
year the Manufacture began production of the legendary Atmos - the
largest clock in the firm’s product range to date. Jean Lebet tells us how a
few years earlier LeCoultre had discovered a remarkable clock under a
bell glass in a Paris branch belonging to his business friend, César de
Trey. He was very taken with the device and learnt that de Trey had
acquired the exclusive sales rights to it from the “Société de Radiologie”.
The inventor of the clock was engineer Jean-Léon Reutter from Neucha-
tel, who had studied at the Swiss Federal Institute of Technology (ETH)
in Zurich and who, since the early twenties, had been investigating the
problem of designing a clock that never needed winding. Although as
an engineer Reutter knew that even he could not invent perpetual
motion, he did come very close to this dream, closer than anyone else
at any rate. Reutter had patented in France in 1926 the ingenious prin-
ciple of a clock that “lives on air” by deriving its energy from changes in
the ambient temperature. However, when large-scale production of
the original Atmos design began, there were considerable problems.
With his trained eye, LeCoultre in Paris saw that the movement was of
inferior quality and criticized de Trey for allowing such a timepiece to
stand alongside the exquisite Jaeger and LeCoultre products. This criti-
cism sparked an intensive re-appraisal of the invention, new tests and,
finally, the development of an Atmos that worked properly. The unique
clock has been manufactured in Le Sentier uninterruptedly since 1936.

The Atmos is not only one of the most ingenious clocks ever
made but probably also the most inexpensive if measured in terms of its
life span - which the Manufacture states to be at least 600 years,
although cleaning is recommended every 20 years in view of today’s
ubiquitous air pollution. An engineer once calculated that the energy

“SECRET NUMBER 1°

steel, to which chromium and tungsten are added and which exhibits
Virtually no coefficient of expansion in the range between -10° and +309
Celsius, is used to make self-compensating balance-springs. Only about
once every five years do the metallurgists who supply Jaeger-LeCoultre
succeed in developing this exceptionally good alloy that passes muster
for the Atmos. The Manufacture then proceeds to purchase about 220
pounds of the substance, as it does not know when the next delivery
will be. The wire is further processed at Jaeger-LeCoultre according toa
secret process and is artificially aged before use. While on the subject of
ageing: a futuristic design in high-grade steel and crystal devised by
Paris-based designers Kohler & Rekow in 1988 shows that the Atmos -
the official gift of the Swiss government to its guests — does not always
have to have a “thirties” look. But most people prefer it in the classic
style of the Opéra, Beaubourg, Vend6me, Fontainebleau or Elysée - the
Atmos models are named after famous places in and around Paris. After
all, it was in Paris that Jacques-David LeCoultre saw the clock for the first
time.

1937: The joint brand name Jaeger-LeCoultre

In 1937 Paris also played a key role in the business activities: the watch
divisions of Jaeger and LeCoultre merged to form the joint brand name
and sales organization “Jaeger-LeCoultre” with headquarters in Geneva.
But the next world war was already looming with Nazi Germany’s power
politics and once again the military needed accurate chronometers.
Although the subject of “war-related” orders has been discreetly
hushed up in Switzerland for decades, collectors know exactly what
happened. During the forties the Manufacture in Le Sentier received
orders — as did other manufacturers such as Omega, Universal or |WC -—
from military forces on opposite sides of the conflict: the Royal Air
Force, for example, ordered a watertight airman’s clock, whereas the
German Luftwaffe ordered a non-watertight model. With the benefit
of hindsight, itis amusing to speculate whether Goering, as chief-of-staff
of the Luftwaffe, was assuming his pilots would never end up in the
“drink”. In any case, Léon Constantin tells us that while this order was
being fulfilled, two gentlemen from Berlin visited the Manufacture’s
Geneva sales office every month to check for themselves on the
watches’ accuracy.

156

jacques-David, died in 1948. It was not his son Roger (1906-1971) but
the industrialist and major shareholder George Ketterer from Bern who
now became managing director of the holding company “SAPIC’, a

complex corporate conglomerate to which LeCoultre (Le Sentien,
Jaeger-LeCoultre (Geneva) and - through a shareholding - parts of
Vacheron & Constantin (Geneva) belonged. A whole series of me-
chanical calibres, which had been developed or improved on during the
War and which today are highly sought-after items, made up the oud

The sixties were an uncertain time for corporate policy. Ketterer,
who had bought up all available shares in Vacheron, parted company
with Jaeger-LeCoultre virtually overnight in 1965. The holding company
with LeCoultre in Le Sentier and Jaeger-LeCoultre in Geneva was
re-named “SAPHIR”. Roger LeCoultre now headed the reduced empire
until it was sold to Henry and Barbara Favre in 1969, who were unable
to stabilize the company in view of the deep recession that was to hit
the whole Swiss watch industry in the seventies. In 1978 the German
group VDO took over the Manufacture. At the end of the eighties
Audemars-Piguet also secured itself long-term access to the watch
movements from Le Sentier by taking a 40% stake in the share capital.

Mechanical watches still have a future

At the end of the sixties and beginning of the seventies watchmakers
were focusing their efforts initially on the newly-emerging quartz tech-
nology. In 1971 Jaeger-LeCoultre developed the first quartz movement
of its own with date display, in 1979 an ultraslim, two millimetre-high
quartz movement for ladies’ watches and in 1982 the world’s smallest
quartz movement, measuring only 1.8 millimetres in height and 11.7
millimetres in diameter. Meanwhile work was in full swing on produc-
tion of the completely modernized Reverso.

Yet no later than 1984 the watchmakers were proving ~ in stark
contrast to the continuing trend towards electronics ~ their undaunted
mastery of mechanical watches: the new automatic calibre 889, only
3.25 millimetres in height, with a 50-hour power reserve, precision regu-
lation and a stop-second is regarded today by connoisseurs as the best
mechanical automatic movement ever built. In 1988 a joint project by
Jaeger-LeCoultre and IWC produced the semi-mechanical chronograph
calibre 630 - with a quartz movement and the world’s smallest me-
chanical chronograph. One year later the Manufacture continued its
great tradition of alarm watches with the Grand Réveil. This watch,
which was additionally fitted with a perpetual calendar and moon phase
display, is similar to the old Memovox only in respect of the alarm
function: the striking mechanism now activates a bell made of fine
Chinese bronze. The subtle difference cannot be overheard. 1990 saw
another innovation: at the European Watch, Clock and Jewellery Fair
in Basle the mechanical world time-zone watch Géographique with

“Sonnez et entrez” - ring and enter says the small sign on his door.
— Mr. Henry-John Belmont, the present managing director of the Manu-
The Manufacture facture in Le Sentier, is a Frenchman at heart, a Swiss in intellect and
_ rather Germanic when it comes to strategy. In short, a European, The

are =< request on his office door reflects his concept of straighttonvard con-
today and the munication - and of management: Ring and enter (without waiting)! This
congenial working invariably grinning man in his mid-fifties with thick grey hair is prepared
= to listen to his employees’ problems every day. The boss? No, this word

___ Mineepbers — does not really suit him, unless its distancing undertone is disregarded.
A father who looks after a large family of 500 members is a better de-

. ES scription of Mr. Belmont's personality. Many people do indeed think he

takes care of everything, just like a father. However, this form of pater-
nalism - which means an employee can come and see him if he has
difficulties with his children or mortgage — is part and parcel of his man-
agement duties, as he sees them: “We spend at least eight hours
together in the Manufacture every day and I would like people to look
forward to coming to work in the morning”. H.-J. Belmont invests much
time in this “miracle” of imparting above-average motivation to his
employees and in the pleasure with which they do their work. It is time
well spent. “There are scarcely two people at the Manufacture who do
exactly the same job, each employee is unique in his or her own way",
he says. He displays this respectful attitude towards other people’s work
in everything he does and the way he does it — and it pays off.

True, he is nota “combier’, that is, he does not originally hail from
the Valley. If he did, he might not be so direct and open in his dealings
with other people. Yet the Belmonts have many things in common with
the founding fathers who made Watch Valley famous. When Pierre er —
LeCoultre packed his bags in Central France in the 16th century at ER aRLEESKE ondomien
the height of the persecution of the Huguenots, Henry-John Belmont’s
ancestors were already fleeing for the same reason. The family dis-
persed all along the road to exile: some stayed in Geneva, others in sah Shoawsibhel Bi
Neuchatel, others still moved on to the area around Schaffhausen in wndhihe Mae
Northern Switzerland, while some did not stop until they had reached sanetk Deleskrowt
Germany. H.-J. Belmont is a descendant of those who settled near
Schaffhausen. Beggingen is the name of the place where his grandfather

two years. Members of his branch of the family have been watchmakers
for five generations and all of those who have taken up this occupation
have Henry as their first or second Christian name ~ it isa family custom,
One of H.-]. Belmont’s sons is called Stefan-Henry. It remains to be seen
whether he too will go into watchmaking,

In 1910 H.-}. Belmont’s grandfather moved from Schaffhausen to
Besancon, the watchmaking centre of Eastern France, and established
there his own brand name, “Titus”. His father, Henry-Louis, tollowed in
his footsteps, established first the watch factory Yema and later the
instrument factory Sormel. Meanwhile Henry-John was being trained as
an engineer at the National School of Micromechanical Engineering and
Chronometry in Besancon. Next, with a view subsequently to taking
over his father’s firm, he attended the INSEAD Institute of Business
Administration in Fontainebleau, which he left with a master’s degree in
business administration (MBA). He then trained as an officer in the navy
and served on an anti-submarine vessel. Thus far, simply the conven-
tional background for a top-flight career in the French economy.
H.-J. Belmont’s career proper began at Texas Instruments in France and
Germany. Then his father’s business needed a successor. For at least 15
years he managed Yema, which developed into the leading French
watchmaker, producing two million watches a year. The attempt to
form a strong European countenveight to the Japanese challenge by
bringing together French, German and Swiss firms took him to the top of
the watchmaking group, which was concentrated within the big Matra
group. Everything was big there, including the amount of drudgery that
had to be done: the objective was not achieved, resulting in massive
staff cuts. It was a time he recalls with unease even today - and one that
has left its mark on him. In 1985 he himself was fired without any notice:
“That was the finest day of my life”, he says with a mischievous smile,
“tough but great”. For nine months H.-J. Belmont did nothing but read,

learn and simply “live” until Mr. Ginter Blumlein brought him to Jaeger-
LeCoultre at the end of 1986.

“It’s so easy to give people the sack”, Mr. Belmont says today
when he thinks back to his time at Matra, “but it’s much more pleas-
urable to re-motivate employees”. In Le Sentier he has only had to give
two people the sack so far, but has recruited some 250 new employees.

166

Some specialists from France, whom he knew at Matra, have now fol-
lowed him to Jaeger-LeCoultre, inc luding designer Janek Deleskiewicz
who, as an observer of new time trends, lives in Paris at the weekend
and every Monday morning makes the three-and-a-hali-hour journey
by high-speed train to Vallorbe, quarter of an hour trom Le Sentier

A truly fresh start

“We made a fresh start with Gunter Blumlein’s strategy”, H.-). Belmont
recalls. There is an unmistakeable note of gratitude in this comment
on the man who has been chairman of Jaeger-LeCoultre's board ot
directors since 1984. Mr. Belmont is not the only one who respects
the vision and flair for markets and products of that man from Germany
who first put IWC in Schaffhausen back on its feet and then in recent
years also revived the great dormant potential of IWC’s sister firm
Jaeger-LeCoultre as a manufacturer of luxury watches. In Le Sentier one
often hears the words, “Since we've had Mr Blumlein...”, as if his arrival
marked the beginning of a new age, an age characterized by a new
model policy, by a conscious nurturing of the proud tradition in
mechanical watches, by synergy and co-operation with IWC in technical
and watchmaking areas where necessary and expedient = while com-
peting at the same time in the market. Mr. Blumlein, who has desks in
Schaffhausen, Le Sentier and, since 1990 also at Lange’s in Glashiitte,
eastern Germany, ensures that the brand names of the watch compa-
nies he manages within the holding company LMH, “Les Manutactures
Horlogéres”, retain their distinctive character

Henry-John Belmont, his sales manager Jean-Marc Keller, produc-
tion manager Jean-Pierre Sassard, the managers of watch engineering
and case manufacture, the designer and advertising manager - almost
all young, dynamic people = first sat down and examined the collection
critically. Gunter Blumlein often attended those meetings himseli and
determined the guiding principles. A small number of clear watch
product lines, cleverly limited initially to a total of 50 models, was de-
signed to give more protile to the product range for the fresh start. The
models that had had their day because the watch design had long been
neglected were dropped. But those that had stubbornly refused to age -
like the Reverso = were promoted. As H.-J}. Belmont says: “It’s quite
possible at one and the same time to live with old and new furniture”.

THE REVERSO IN DETAIL

Please follow me, today I'm going to show you how a Reverso is
made”, says Corinne, the very helpful assistant in Jaeger-LeCoultre’s
Communication Department

“Do you mean the whole of today?”
"Yes, but if that’s not sufficient me we can easily continue

tomorrow’

Such a question can only be asked by someone who comes to
the Vallée de Joux thinking of his always-full schedule of appointments
\With such an attitude he will constantly run up against the calm selt-
confidence that characterizes the Valley and its inhabitants. Anyone
who does not take the venerable-sounding term “Communication” lit-
erally at Jaeger-LeCoultre will soon realize that the word does refer to

communication” and not just public relations. The term “advertising
department” would therefore not accurately reflect the work done by
the small team

So how is a Reverso made? Oh no, none of the technicians turns
around curiously when strangers enter the room. Work takes prece-
dence over them. Perhaps one of them might look askance, without
losing sight of the case parts lying on the tables and workbenches. There
are kilos of gold, some of it still quite rough and unworked. But many
shapes already reveal what they will be made into: for example, the
rectangular bezels carefully lined-up ona small wire rack. And the as-vet
unmachined golden bracelet horns, which embellish the ordinary card-
board box in which they are carefully laid out, point unmistakeably to
a Reverso. The irreverent way in which the noble material is handled is
a source of annoyance to the layman, who is afraid to touch anything
at all. For the Manufacture’s specialists who make Reversos from the
precious metal every day, it 1s a material they value only because of its
good workability compared with, say, steel. But it no longer occurs to
them to think of itas “gold”, as the customer thinks when he utters the
magic word in a jeweller’s shop.

The very first Reversos in 1931 were made of English Staybrite
steel, the best that money could buy in those days, which was only
natural for such a watch. However, the engravers who blunted theit

How many functional components are needed to make the
unique Reverso case? With all the screws and seals, exactly 41! Counting
the glass but not the crown. This surprising figure alone highlights the
technical complexity of the system, whose manufacture 1s obvious)
very time-consuming. For unlike a conventional case, it is not just a
metal box for protecting the movement but, together with its pivoting
mechanism, itself forms part of the watch’s function and is designed to
work reliably for years. Narrow, constantly monitored tolerances are

pressed slightly inwards against a spring. So a way had to be found ‘
accommodate these parts of the mechanism in the case, but in isolation
from the actual inner space housing the movement. This is precisely the
function of the inner case.

Proceeding in the logical order of the drawing: from the glass (1
via the bezel (2) we jump to component number 5, the so-called guard
ring or “cercle” in French. It performs the function of an inner case
Within it is accommodated one of the three differently-milled casing
rings (4.14.3), depending on the movement used (mechanical, quartz
quartz with seconds and date display). The upper seal (3) hermetically
seals this inner case (5) together with the built-in casing ring and move-
menton the bezel side. The whole assembly is fixed to the bezel by four
screws complete with O-ring seals and washers (13.1-13.3).

As said inner case is open on the bridge side of the movement,
i.e. onthe underside, it must of course also be sealed on that side. Thisis
done by the special lower seal (6) between the inner case (guard ring)
and the case bottom. Outside the space protected in this way, viz. on
the two narrow sides of the guard ring, are fixed the two corrosion-
resistant platelets (10.1, 10.2) with the pivots and hemispherical balls
for the pivoting mechanism; during assembly the platelets are fastened
between the guard ring and the sides of the case by the four case
bottom screws (14). Once the case has been closed, only the spring-
mounted, conically-shaped pivots and the two likewise  spring-
mounted hemispherical balls project through the semi-circular holes
milled in the bezel and case bottom (7).

After closing, the case must now be fixed to the carrier plate 8
securely enough to prevent it becoming detached, but without com-
promising its mobility. Here again the redesign of the Reverso at the
beginning of the eighties came up with a new solution: the carrier plate
8) with the sides angled at 90° and the bracelet horns or flanges (9) no
longer form a single part. The three carrier plate components are man-
ufactured separately and fastened to one another by four screws (15).
This leaves free between the highly-angled side of the carrier plate 8)
and the horns (9) a conically-shaped slot, in which the likewise-conical
pivot (10.2) can slide but out of which it cannot slip when the horns have
been firmly mounted on the carrier plate (cf. detailed drawing)

Two unusual details ensure not only that the arrangement works
but that itworks pleasantly and comfortably: the pivots, which are under
slight spring pressure, push slightly outwards, The actual pivot rests on a
circular disc which is pushed by the pressure of the springs against the
inside wall of the horns and carrier plate. This can be seen clearly on the
detailed drawing. When the pivoting mechanism Is activated, these
small discs act as a brake and facilitate gentle, even sliding. The same
purpose is served by another design precaution, a small step (16) on the
bottom of the carrier plate, which the Le Senter designers imaginatively
called the “pavement”. It facilitates smooth movement of the mecha-
nism, as it is on this ridge that the case slides during release and latching.
The Reverso is thus more than a purely mechanical function, itis also the
art of completing a small turning movement with perfect elegance.

The historic inspiration for the Reverso, that small touch of Art Deco-
style “esprit”, was expressed in tangible form for the first time in the
fashionable, effervescent Paris of 1931, Although the spirit is more than
half a century old, Jaeger-LeCoultre’s employees and machines are still
imbued with it. The machines have changed: today, their turning and
milling movements are computer-controlled; but the people have not
changed

In one of the Jaeger-LeCoultre workshops, which can only be
reached after passing through a maze of staircases and narrow, winding
corridors, hissing and buzzing sounds ate being emitted by large,
orange-red painted cabinets. Orange is also the colour of the characters
on one of the monitors: “CERCLE REVERSO HOMME EBAUCHE”
followed by a couple of rows of figures and coordinates — the only
language the poor electronic brain controlling the milling tools in the
closed metal cabinet can understand. The rotating cutting edges are
removing material and fashioning curves, edges, projections and
hollows into the blank for the nickel-silver guard ring which will form
the inner case of a gentleman’s Reverso. Oil spurts out against a Plex
glass inspection porthole, turning it completely opaque. No matter -

once it has been accurately adjusted and programmed, the machine
does not need any light or curious scrutiny for the difficult milling
operation

What the technician removes from the cabinet shortly afterwards
bears no resemblance to the unworked piece of metal it was before. Its
now a milled-to-measure base for another important component of the

Reverso that is nearing completion in the adjacent machine: the brass
casing-ring. The high-precision mounting with its rounded edges for the
barrel-shaped hand-winding calibre 846 is being turned trom the blank
for the casing-ring, which once looked like a thick washer; it is given a
lateral groove to allow the winding stem to pass. Again it is remarkable
that even such a relatively simple component sull conceals a refine-
ment: the rounded edges of the casing-ring are provided with fine longi-
tudinal slots to facilitate spring-mounting of the movement in the inner
case.

When the parts come out of the machine they are, of course, far
trom finished. Holes have to be drilled, 12 of them on the inner case
alone. Threads have to be cut, edges trimmed and surfaces ground. But
itappears the machines are only good for the rough work. Even where it
is not going to be visible later on, a “hand, heart and an eye” are indis-
pensable for the Reverso

Ata small table, for example, a craftsman is toiling away with a
piece of fine wet emery paper. The objects which he is moving over the
roughened surface with his regular, circular movements, occasionally
turning them over and repeatedly examining them, are the same brass
casing-rings that the milling machine has previously transformed into
the precise outer shape. But the surtace grinding of each individual
piece is not entrusted to any machine - itis done by an industrious tech-
nician with the finest emery paper, untl the thickness of the part corre-
sponds exactly to the value set on the comparator in front of him.

A few steps further on another technician is removing from a
worn cardboard box a solid gold part which evokes the Reverso’s
characteristic shapes for the first ume: two flanges or bracelet horns in
one piece, still joined to one another in the middle by a bridge. His
machine is programmed to separate the two bevelled side parts for the
Reverso, which are screwed onto the bottom plate later on, and to mill
them so that the three main components of the carrier plate - the plate
itself and the two horns on the left and right — fit together exactly. After
this operation the machine conveys the unneeded central piece of the
golden punched part to the precious-metal chest, along with the many
fine, oil-dripping gold chips. The bottom plate, bezel and case bottom
are made in a similar way. But the machine only does the preliminary

jewellery. The next stage in the working of the case components is, in
fact, highly reminiscent of a jewellery workshop. With incredible dexter-
ity women in this department are trimming under the microscope each
individual part delivered, for the very first case assembly. The burin in
their fingers darts swiftly over the edges and corners piling tiny, curled
gold chips onto a brass plate.

Here, in Paul Faureconte’s department, is where the upper and
lower parts of each Reverso case are assembled to form a single unit
for the first time after the separate existence they have led on the
racks. From now on they remain together for ever: it is, as it were, the
Reverso’s “wedding”. This is where it acquires its identity as a watch. The
two halves of the case are now worked individually by hand, filed and
smoothed repeatedly by the gentle hand of a woman technician until
they fit together perfectly and seamlessly.

In order to ensure that the two outer-case parts hold together -
they are not screwed to one another directly but are each fastened to
the inner case referred to above - they are given a temporary “dummy”
shaped like the real inner case. It is a tool that is removed before final
assembly and is replaced by the real inner case together with the move-
ment.

Final assembly is still a long way off, of course. The next stage
in the creation of a Reverso is a very special, almost a solemn moment.
But before that, while on our way to the next stage we jump for a

second when the floor suddenly moves beneath our feet as we leave ograms of gold dust
the room: an electric shoe-cleaning machine automatically sets in ayear irom the soles of thou-
motion wire brushes that remove every particle of gold dust from our sands of shoes

soles. The useful piece of equipment is installed in front of every door ot
workshops where gold is worked. It pays for itself as well, as the shoe-
cleaners collect two kilos of gold dust from thousands of soles each
year. That may not sound like much compared with the two metric
tons of gold which Jaeger-LeCoultre turns into shining watch cases over
the same period. But .... take care of the pence and the pounds will take
care of themselves.

THE GENIUS
OF THE REVERSO’S
THREE STRIPES

What would the Reverso be without the two sets of three stripes milled
into its bezel? This small but indispensable detail belongs to the cate-
‘ gory ot art, jewellery, Art Deco or just plain good taste. For these milled
identifying mark - lines that run around the upper and lower bezels of the actual case are,

7 firstly, the stylistic continuation of the front side of the watch and thus
point the way to its no-less-charming reverse. They tell the eye: please
turn over, it continues on the other side. Or vice-versa if it is already
reversed. Secondly, they delimit the case from the smoothly polished,
bevelled horns or flanges. But, above all, they are the unmistakeable and
ingenious heritage of the “Art Nouveau” period which produced the
Reverso and later came to be known as Art Deco

More than a mere

This new form-language resulted from the merging of two fun-
damental styles: modernism with its streamlined forms symbolizing
technical progress; and a harking-back to austere Classicism with its
features borrowed straight from Hellenic architecture. The Reverso has
exactly the right measure of each: the streamlined shape of the case and
the horns. With a little imagination they can also be assimilated to parts
of a racing car’s bodywork. And the classic stripes that bear and delimit
the case below and above evoke three pillars of a Greek temple. An aes- es ee
thete could easily become infatuated with the Reverso for the sake of Sao bh 1]
of these three stripes and their visual effect. The great Jaeger designers who
created it in Paris back in 1931 deserve our belated gratitude.

the greeting

architects in

Ancient Greece. But let us now return to Le Sentier to pay a visit to master-crafts-

man René Cuhat at Jaeger-LeCoultre. It is he who mills into the cases

these three decorative rings without which a Reverso would look like

ells a Mercedes without a radiator grille. His work, like many other case-

= a production tasks in his department, is a further example of genuine
artistic craftsmanship coupled with legendary Swiss precision.

Although the outer case appears to be complete by this stage, itis
still unfinished and temporarily held together by means of a dummy
assembly ring placed inside it. This means the upper part of the case is
screwed onto this ring from inside, while the four screws of the case
bottom grip the assembly ring from the sides, thereby fixing the bottom
We will take another look at the slightly unusual way in which the
Reverso is held together when describing final assembly

long Reverso tradition that dates

THE GENIUS
OF THE REVERSO'S
THREE STRIPES

René Cuhat naturally wears a

Reverso himself. He has worked

with the watch for so Jong that

he has developed ar

relanonship with it

Mr. Cuhat, who has been with Jaeger-LeCoultre for 35 years, js
among those who have been working on the Reverso longest. As he
goes through the ritual of artistic creation he is well aware of what he is
doing for the Reverso’s inimitable prestige. He clamps the case of a
golden Reverso onto his small, slightly antiquated milling machine so
that as soon as he pushes the start button it rotates slowly against the
machine, twice to be precise. If the machine only milled around the
case once, the quality of the work would be inadequate for his taste,

Here is some detailed information for connoisseurs about the
result: the width of each of the three milled stripes is exactly 0.35 mm,
They are 0.25 mm deep and the elevated ridges between them measure
0.5 mm. The milling depth of 0.25 mm takes into account the fact that
the polishers’ brushes, polishing machines and felt discs will, in the next
stage, remove about another 0.05 mm from the case material, leaving
grooves exactly 0.2 mm deep in the end. These figures vary slightly for
the smaller ladies’ model. René Cuhat obviously knows the figures by
heart, down to the second decimal place.

But before the specialists in the Polishing Department come on
stage, one small task remains in connection with the Reverso’s stripes:
the milled grooves still have to be sand-blasted. Only in this way do they
retain, even after polishing of the whole case, a matt, granular surface
in the grooves that makes them stand out from the high-gloss ridges in
between. It may be a minor point, but it is characteristic of the sheer
perfection of the craftsmen’s work, right down to the last nook and
cranny. But should one not expect this of a watch that has been made

for more than 60 years?

René Cuhat is proud of having always worked on the Reverso
during his many years with Jaeger-LeCoultre. Only a few Reversos
crossed his workbench in the fifties, a decade conspicuous for its dearth
of good taste. This explains why he is delighted there are more Reversos
today than ever, even if it means working overtime. Naturally, he too
wears a golden Reverso with a “face” that was modern in the early
eighties — bearing Roman numerals. He fastens it to his wrist with the
patented metal bracelet which was specially designed for the Reverso
and which is almost as soft and close-fitting as a leather strap. Bul
dyed-in-the-wool Reverso wearers have almost insuperable differences

188

of opinion about leather as opposed to metal, about beautiful and
authentic bracelets on the one hand and practical, indestructible ones
on the other. These differences should be respected.

Up to now we have only mentioned the Reverso’s carrier plate in
passing. For while people like R. Cuhat are doing their utmost to perfect
the case itself, other craftsmen, working separately, are concentrating
on the “carrying” element of the bottom plate and on the horns
attached to it. The distance between the horns is measured precisely
and corrected, while the carrier plate is given a bevelled edge on the
underside. Here again dozens of additional manual operations are
necessary before the completed parts can move onto the small rack
where they wait to be transferred to Didier Aveline’s workshop. There,
in the Polishing Department, they and the Reverso’s other components
have - literally — brilliant prospects.

the rotating disc, inspects itwith the magnifying glass, re-measures, po}-

what his diligent “pensioner” is capable of.

Polishing is not merely cosmetic surface treatment, but also the
part of the made-to-measure work a machine cannot do. For example

the carrier plate is ground on the grinding wheel and is given a matt
surface polish on the underside. The part has to pass an accuracy test to
within 1/200ths of a millimetre. The same applies to the bevelled and
polished edges on the underside of the bottom plate, which are first
ground and then polished until they attain a high gloss. A limit stop on
the grinding and polishing machines ensures that the correct angle 's
maintained. Circular graining, which is conventional circular grinding
with a superb visual effect, is of course also carried out by hand, stroke
by stroke. Didier Aveline usually does it himself on a small machine
In between times, he checks one colleague’s work, advises another
colleague who may have a problem and waits until the small tray on his
table slowly fills with polished cases. He then examines each one stemly
before picking up the tray and taking it to Lydia Pedrina next door
Otherwise it might soon land on his workbench again. In the in-house
jargon anything that is waiting once the Polishing Department has

192

pleted its work is known imaginatively and affectionately as “visit-

com
~ inspection of the finished case during the first quality control.

ag e”

With an unerring gaze Mrs. Pedrina examines the work that has
been done so far, inspecting every case to see whether its surface is
spotless, using her magnifying glass to detect scratches or perhaps
corners and edges that have not been polished properly. She looks to
see whether the circular graining on the base part is good enough and
thus decorative enough. Only if she thinks the part is up to scratch does
she reach for the small bottle with the brush in the lid. Every case that
has passed the visual test is now given a carefully-applied coat of a spe-
cial protective blue varnish, so that not even a fingerprint can tarnish the
Reverso’s spotless outer skin during subsequent work - such as
assembly and fitting of the movement. The cases with which she finds
even the slightest fault go back to Didier Aveline for retouching or
re-polishing, as the case may be. Although the complaints are not
meant personally, both of them are obviously much happier if there are
no complaints at all.

FROM THE CASE TO
THE WATCH

Y Outwardly, itis already unmistakeably a Reverso by this stage. Or, to be

Why the Reverso first more precise, a well-shaped, magnificently polished case still coated

liieiaesD i with a thin protective layer of blue varnish. But it 1s not yet a proper

has to take a bath be- watch. The next operations are therefore more closely connected with

— the as-yet unfitted movement. After polishing, these begin with re-

unit anulics measuring of the holes bored in the case and, if necessary, Correction,

ment that makes it tick. Just to be on the safe side. In the meantime at another workstation

— skilled hands are preparing the guard ring, thatis, the real inner case (part
: = : 5 on the enlarged drawing) for fitting.

fore receiving the move-

The reader will remember that in order to make sure the two
halves of the case could be joined to one other for polishing, a tool had
acted as a dummy. The real guard ring looks slightly different as it carries
on its sides the most important elements of the pivot mechanism -
hemispherical balls spring-mounted on pins and pivots fixed to the two
side platelets (10.1 and 10.2 in the drawing). They have to be adjusted
and mounted before the complete guard ring is fitted into the case for
the first time.

This was also done slightly differently on the old Reverso. On that
model the spring pins, together with the balls and pivots, were inserted
from inside into four outwardly-tapered holes in the casing-ring, before
the movement was fitted. Only the tips of the conically-shaped pivots
and part of the hemispherical balls still protruded after assembly. The
components of the pivot mechanism were therefore not freely acces-
sible even after the case was opened. If the pivot mechanism suffered
damage or wear, the movement first had to be completely removed as

well and the whole casing-ring usually had to be replaced. =

The semi-assembled cases await their turn on the bench of one of
the department's excellent specialists, who incidentally mixes her adho.
sive composition herself. Although the short procedure by which the
glass is fitted appears to be strikingly simple, it still requires a great deal
of instinctive feel: the opened case upper part is placed onto a plate
under a microscope and a small round block is inserted into the aper-
ture for the glass, so that the block protrudes from the aperture. With a
thin injection needle constantly supplied with fresh adhesive through a
narrow hose, the young woman applies an even, continuous layer of
adhesive to the narrow support surface for the glass. She then places the
slightly curved glass onto the protruding block and raises the case upper
part carefully and unerringly so that the glass fits exactly into the aper-
ture provided. One last dexterous push of the finger on the glass, a burst
of air from the compressed air-gun and it’s ready.

The case upper part now has to spend a night in an oven heated
to 40° C to allow the adhesive to harden. If the adhesive composition
permits, however, the bonding expert prefers to allow it to harden in
the open air, even though it takes two whole days. But what time is that
for a watch which Jaeger-LeCoultre makes to last for many decades?

After this short break the case bottom is mounted. A seal made
specially and exclusively for the Reverso and on which designer Daniel
Wild spent a lot of time and effort, is placed in a groove milled on the
underside of the guard ring. As the detailed drawing shows, the rectan-
gular sealing ring with a rounded triangular cross-section has a broad
base and can nevertheless be compressed well on its narrow side. This
tailor-made solution corresponds exactly to the requirements of closing
the inner case, which is open on the underside, by applying the seal on
the case bottom. The broader base of the special seal rests firmly on the
case bottom while the narrow end can be compressed in the groove o!
the guard ring, Many attempts were necessary to optimize even thistiny

The enlarged drawing at the beginning of the chapter, to Which

way of assembling the case is unique and specific to the Reverso: itis the
only way of leaving the case bottom completely free of screws of other
disruptive opening devices. Its future owner thus has an immaculate
surface on which to give free rein to his imagination and the engraver

to display his skilfullness.

But before reaching that stage, the closed case first has to pass
another test after having been fitted with the crown and its seal. This
time, however, the purpose is not to enhance the appearance but to
protect the precision movement from dirt and humidity. Two or three
dozen assembled cases are placed in the pressure testing box, where
they are initially subjected to an air pressure of 3 bar for several minutes
The small metal platform on which they rest is then lowered into a
mixture of water and alcohol, without the round pressure box having
to be opened. Any case that is not watertight is betrayed by rising air
bubbles over the next two hours. This is then marked on the glass andit
's sent back to the Assembly Department for therapy. Looking through
the reinforced plate-glass window of the testing box, the tester’s trained
eye recognizes at a glance where the defect is and after the chamber has
been opened he writes the results on the watch glass with a coloured
felt-tip pen.

The cases that have passed the test are then allowed to move
hare — to be subjected to a second watertightness test

Watertightness here should not, of course, be taken to mean
the depths commonly reached by divers. But even though this degree
of watertightness cannot be explicitly guaranteed with such a difficult
case design, the Manufacture does its utmost to protect the heart of
the case. Right at the end of the long manufacturing process another
pressure test is carried out when the movement has been fitted.

But before the Reverso receives all the attributes of a watch in the
form of the movement, dial and hands, there is one more minor inter-
mediate stage. Hitherto it has been lacking the carrier plate with the
horns. This component is now fitted so that both components can, if
necessary, be harmonized precisely with one another. Nothing must
grind or jam when the Reverso performs its pivoting movement.
Although the case and carrier plate have to be separated from one
another again for fitting of the movement, all that needs to be done is to
loosen four screws with which the horns are attached to the carrier plate
from inside, and the actual case may again be removed.

CALIBRE 846

A change of scene. For the time being, enough has been said about the
manufacture of the extraordinary case without which the Reverse
would certainly not have been able to celebrate its 60th anniversary,
Let us now turn to a different — or rather the — speciality of the house,
which had been perfected in Le Sentier long before a single watch case
was ever made there. It is the fascinating creation of a small machine
referred to as the 846. Correct, it is the mechanical movement of the
Reverso as described here. The game with the slightly unusual termi-
nology may be taken a stage further: a mechanically wound watch is the
oldest and smallest machine we use. In actual fact, it was for centuries
the unrecognized precursor of the technical age. Unrecognized prob-
ably because it generates only an even, but commercially “unproduc-
tive” motion from an energy supply which is fed to it by tightening a
spring or winding up a weight. Winding up can be done much faster
than unwinding. Essentially, this time difference constitutes the whole
essence of the clock as a “time machine”. It is also significant that the
quality of what “makes” the watch is measured by the constant regular-
ity of the process. That is why the true school of watchmaking does not
begin only in the silent watchmakers’ or timers’ workshops but in the
workshops in which the small individual parts of a movement are made
on usually large machines utilizing either old or very modern tech-
niques. The goal determines the method. Progress and tradition are not
mutually exclusive. It has always been this way at Jaeger-LeCoultre.

Behind the grey wooden door on ground level leading to one of
Jaeger-LeCoultre’s numerous annexes, One notices a strong but not
unpleasant smell of oil. The large room is filled with the buzzing and
humming of at least two dozen long machines which have been instal-
led diagonally to the wall on account of their length. At one end of each
steel monster there is a head on which move control cams and the
claws of the cutting tools. The picture is reminiscent of the jaws of a
giant insect. The comparison is not far-fetched, as with every cycle the
machine bites off a short piece of steel wire that is automatically pushed
up from behind. It chews and spits out one of the Reverso’s smallest
parts in an oily rivulet. With the naked eye it can still just about be
detected as a dark dot which is washed into a small sieve with the oil, as
over a child’s slide in the swimming pool: it is an “escape pinion”

Apinion whic h, when joined to the escape-wheel, will later be driven by
the second wheel and will constantly be stopped by the pallet. Every 22
seconds the “Tornos-Bechler M4" makes such a pinion from the three-
metre-long AP-20 steel wire until all the raw material has been cut into
2.44-millimetre long pieces and the individual parts have been reduced
to the necessary diameter and have had ultra-fine journals milled at
both ends to fit into the bottom-plate bearings.

In the Screw-machining Department output in quantitative terms
is inversely proportional — almost grotesquely so - to the time and
trouble taken and to the size of the equipment. Michel Jeannin, who is
in charge down here and who occasionally lubricates the joints of his
steel “ants” with a red oil can, holds a small plastic bag out towards us:
“Two thousand pieces”, he says proudly, “half a week's work”. Incred-
ibly, the whole thing would barely fill a thimble. What he does not tell
us is that it had taken all his and his fellow employees’ dexterity, many
years’ experience and hours of work to set up and adjust the screw-
cutting machine, one of many which now carry out their remarkable
miniature-scale work regularly, reliably and almost without any inter-
vention on his part. Othenvise the contents of his plastic bag would
now be mainly micromechanical scrap.

We will come across the steel pinion, which is so tiny it can hardly
be grasped between two fingers, on a few other occasions. First of all, in
the Tooth Cutting Department, where it receives its “leaves” and again
later during burnishing when it is made really fine, when its two bearing
pivots with the almost inconceivable diameter of 0.096 mm are indi-
vidually polished and cambered under the microscope. It is a world of
tiny dimensions that arouses astonishment and admiration - not so
much because most of the parts worked are so tiny but because of the
extreme dedication of the workers, who, for example, patiently polish
the bearing surface of a balance pivot or polish a small wheel to make it
look more beautiful. The ambitious attitude to this work and the pride in
one’s own ability reveal Jaeger-LeCoultre’s 160-year history more than
anything else. This deep-rooted, omnipresent self-contidence denves
from the feeling of belonging to the few who can still manufacture their
Movements completely themselves and in-house. It also means they

remain in control of all decisions affecting the real quality of a watch
movement.
