CALIBRE 846

Michel Jeannin is happy with the way things are going on his
Tornos”. He puts aside the red oil can and wipes his hands ona piece of
cleaning wool. On the long wooden bench running along the windows
next to the numerous measuring instruments, the descendants of the
old millionometer, lies a thick file with each sheet of paper individually
protected from contact with oily fingers by a plastic sleeve. Many of
the pages already look rather yellow and dog-eared. These are the
design drawings of the older calibres. The “846” is one of those, as it has
been manufactured since 1975. The dimensions indicated for the parts
in fractions of a millimetre were inscribed witha stencil in the design and
engineering office. Other more recent designs bear the mark of the
computer. For example, parts for the calibre 822 of the Reverso “Grande
Taille’. But numerous calibres are manufactured at Jaeger-LeCoultre
and many new ones have been added recently, When Michel Jeannin
receives a new drawing from the Design Department, he first has to
make an important decision on the choice of machinery: computer-
controlled or conventional? This means either one or at least three days’
setting-up time until the first part - a pinion or arbor — slides down the
small chute into the collection container.

Two ultra-modern numerically-controlled screw-cutting ma-
chines are in operation in a side room. They do not provide greater
capacity but easier ways to make complex or difficult-to-machine
components such as calendar star wheels and the conically-shaped
stainless-steel pivots for the Reverso case. The wheels and pinions of
the calibre 846 are made exclusively on conventional screw-cutting
machines. “With these”, says M. Jeannin, pointing to a drawer full of
saucer-sized steel discs with irregularly milled apertures and eleva-
tions on the edge: they are the “programs” with which all the opera-
tions of the turning machine are controlled. It looks simpler than it is.
During one rotation of the control cam in 22 seconds for the “escape
pinion”, for example, a total of 67 different operations has to be
accommodated on the 360-degree radius of the program cam, in the
form of curves that have to be calculated precisely on the cam. These
curves are scanned by the machine and translated into changes in the
motion of the tools. The diagram in the dog-eared file, which was hand:
written and calculated by M. Jeannin himself, records the movement
steps to within a fraction of a second. As we have seen, the pre-
parations for a new screw-machined part take three days of mental

206

work, and on top of this the control cam itself must be produced and
hardened. The numerically-controlled machine, on the other hand, is
supplied with coordinates for the directional changes and with the
number of revolutions for each tool intervention. A dozen switchable
stepping motors operate there simultaneously and independently of
one another. Conventional screw-cutting machines are driven only by
a single power source whose speed of rotation has to be transmitted
and transposed many times. A few decades ago in Le Sentier it was still
possible to take the well-known pictures of the many transmission
belts leading from the motor shaft on the ceiling to the individual
machines.

“But the quality that can be attained is the same in both cases”,
M. Jeannin pointed out when he noticed we had some doubts about
the capabilities of his older machines: + 3 microns from the target value,
which expressed in figures is 0,003 millimetres. We were convinced.

On the bare wall above the table the balance of a highly-polished
“Atmos Elysée” swings leisurely to and fro. The unique clock, which
adorns the studies of, among other people, many politicians who at
some time in their careers came to Switzerland on official visits, spreads
an aura of watch and clock culture, even in this austere environment. Its
pinions, which are more than ten times larger than those of the calibre
846, were also made here, of course, and every day they confirm the
quality of the blue-coated technicians’ work

ON THE TRAIL OF THE

Gérard Meylan, head of the

for the bearing and until the recesses for receiving the ruby pallet-stones
have been cut out of the two arms. Like all other steel parts, they will
have to go through the fire several times in order to attain the proper
hardness. During “tonnelage” they will spend days rotating In a wooden
drum together with sawdust and several mysterious ingredients, before
emerging immaculately polished.

Everything that needs teeth receives expert treatment in Gérard
Meylan’s “Tooth Cutting” Department on its journey through the Manu-
facture’s workshops: escape-wheels for the Reverso, train wheels for
the Géographique or large pinions for the Atmos. They are in excellent
hands with the “tooth of time” experts. The more refined the work, the
smaller, quieter and more precise the processing machines become in
turn. It is a precision engineering workshop in which the spirit of the old
watchmaking art is immediately perceptible, despite the ultra-modern
equipment for milling, testing, polishing, counting and measuring. Of
course, although one concern here is the number of components pro-
duced, the prime concern is every single piece. Even a steel pinion from
the next-door department and measuring only a few millimetres is just
as entitled to perfection as the barrel with its teeth for the Atmos, the
largest component currently made by Jaeger-LeCoultre.

Alot of scientific detail could be given about how the wheels and
pinions obtain their “teeth”, as not all teeth are alike. But whether the
tooth profile is epicycloidal, hypoid or involute, what is more important
here is the basic insight that the manufacture of toothed wheels is
closely connected with the progress of watchmaking. And that the
firm’s founder, viz. Antoine LeCoultre, also played a considerable part in
that progress,

But in the days of ancient Egyptian water wheels that had lateral
pins instead of teeth, the transmission of forces from one shaft to
another was an imprecise, rough-and-ready business. And so it re-
mained until the hand-wrought toothed wheels of the first mechanical
clocks, when each tooth was still filed individually by hand. Only with
the invention in the 16th century of gear-cutting machines equipped
with precise index discs for specific numbers of teeth and with the first
tooth milling machines did it become possible to make equally-spaced

teoth, smaller toothed W heels and thus also portable clocks But in
those days only the slots were cut into the wheels at first, as the tooth
profiles still had to be formed by hand or with suitable files for a long
time to come. Later on tooth-cutting was perlormed by $0-Called
rounding-off machines. It was Antoine LeCoultre who began to pro-
duce milled steel pinions in his tirst workshop in 1826, For this Purpose

he even developed the first profile milling machines, with which it 2

possible to give teeth their final protile in a single operation

Today, with the advent of numerically-controlled Machines
almost everything is possible. It starts already with toolmaking. In order
to cut the teeth on our small escape pinion = which has a diameter oj
one millimetre at its “Widest” point - for the calibre 846, Gerard Meylan
takes one of the smallest tooth milling machines out of the drawer, Its
perhaps just about as large as the frictional wheel of a cigarette lighte,
which it resembles. The only difference is that its razor-thin blades, be-
tween which not even a human hair will pass, run night around its radial
surface. The milling device is camped into the machine with its axis
facing forward. The position can be imagined most easily by thinking of
mounting a wheel on a jacked-up car. If the wheel is turned, it moves
exactly as it one were to drive on the road. The tooth milling cutter
also moves in the same position, although it can travel to and fro in the
honzontal direction as well. The part to be machined, on the other
hand, viz. our escape-wheel, whose teeth or leaves are to be cut parallel
to the direction of the axis just like any other toothed wheel), is clamp-
ed into a bearing exactly the other way around, i.e. transversally to the
direction of travel of the milling cutter. The pinion, which also rotates
is then brought towards the milling cutter on a tixed sliding track. The
milling cutter cuts, with a precisely coordinated sequence of move-
ments for both parts, around the recesses and into the surface of the
pinion, leaving the actual teeth or leaves in position

the procedure ts similar when cutting the teeth of the wheels
those made of brass are, as a matter of principle, gold-plated betore
tooth cutting, so that not even the thinnest layer of gold can remain in
the space between the teeth and lead to disruptive abrasion. Som
wheels, for example those for the beautifully decorated movement ol
the “Reverso 60th”, also receive a glossing polish around the hub. The
Preparations for this tooth-cutting procedure involve counting out 40

214

wheels each time and lining them up like pearls on a wire. To mark
them, the technician threads on a brightly-coloured steel platelet
before the next 40 wheels are placed on the wire. A chain half a metre
long can easily take several thousand wheels. The batch of 40, taking up
perhaps five millimetres, is placed on an axis before tooth-c utting and is
machined by the tooth milling cutter in the axis direction, as with the
escape pinion of the calibre 846 descnbed earher

In every mechanical watch there is one wheel that plays a
strikingly different role in terms of tooth engineering: the escape-wheel,
which on the calibre 846 has exactly 20 heel-shaped teeth. The mode ot
operation Is explained and illustrated in our briet description of the
mechanical movement: the heel-shaped teeth provide, in cooperation
with the pallet and its bevelled ruby pallet-stones, the constant “stop
and go” movement of the esc ape-wheel. Here again the toolmakers

ON THE TRAIL OF THE
“TOOTH OF TIME”

have proved to be watchmakers’ most important allies. The Tooth
Cutting Department is not unstinting with its gratitude. An expensive

special tool, the ultra-modern, computer-controlled escape-whee|

milling machine, handles the five delicate operations necessary to cut

the unconventional shape of the heel-shaped tooth from the wheel

edge, ina single stage. ILis almost a work of art. Probably only someone

who has understood the spirit of the highly-developed watchmaking
craft in the Vallée de Joux in its cultural and historical context will appre-

ciate how, once the teeth have been cut on the tiny five millimetre-
diameter escape-wheel for the calibre 846, it is ground and polished at

all the points where it will later come into contact with the ruby pallet-
stones of the pallet. About 60 additional manual operations for a tiny
wheel that ends up costing almost as much as an entire “Swatch”. The
comparison is erroneous. The agreeable anachronism of noble me-
chanical watchmaking does not need to be compared with the mani-
festations of the throw-away age’s watch culture. That is why no time or
effort should be spared when it comes to taking quality perfectionism to
extremes: this maxim also applies to the small “pivoting” workshop
where the wafer-thin bearing journals of the arbors are smoothed again
with particular care and where can-sized drums revolve containing the
small pinions in a special polishing solution, because it would now be
impossible to process these minute parts by hand. If it is to be convinc-
ing, mechanical engineering, that easily comprehensible and therefore
very human way of ordering things, needs this final uncompromising

| LECOULTRE FORGE

From forge to watch factory” (De la forge ala manufacture horlogere) is
the title of the indispensable reference work by historian: Francois
Jequier, which documents industrialization in the Vallée de Joux and, in
hardening of this context, details the development of LeCoultre as well. The “forge”,
from which the precision engineering workshops and later the watch-
makers’ workshops developed, still exists = right in the middle of the
most of the Manutacture’s premises. The quenching or hardening of steel by heat-
ing and immersion in liquids such as oil has been known for millennia as
one of the ways to change the properties of iron and later on of steel
done in-house. Fire, heat, soot and the stench of burning are thus the ingredients of
, the old-fashioned atmosphere in a large room on the basement level of
Jaeger-LeCoultre’s premises. Under large flue hoods furnaces roar away

alte which, though nowadays fired with gas or electricity, used to run on
charcoal many years ago. “Les Charbonniéres” is in fact the name of the
small place just after Le Pont at the north-eastern entrance to the Valley,
a name that clearly evokes the deep-rooted tradition of the charcoal
burners, who in those days were the indispensable suppliers of fuel to
smiths, tinkers, instrument makers — and watchmakers.

Even the

components and

toolmaking are

When today broad-shouldered Michel Maumary, who has been
guarding the fire for 26 years at Jaeger-LeCoultre, pulls up one of the
thick firestone-clad furnace doors and reaches into the glowing hole
with his forge tongs, the observer really is taken back to his childhood
memories of the village blacksmith with his slightly soot-blackened
jace, but the impression is deceptive; just as good-natured M, Maumary
at first sight gives the mistaken impression that iron wheels could s
= be torged here if a village farmer were to come in with some. But “high
mech” is at home in the “Heat Treatment Department”, as it is known
officially. Only three people work there: M. Maumary, his young boss
Thierry Mesnier and Maria Meszaros. The fire glows in the furnaces
giving the onlooker an attractive and mysterious impression. Here they
heat up only the steel watch components or tools made by Jaeger
LeCoultre and then quench them - or allow them to cool slowly. It is not om
a secret, on the contrary, the small department is particularly popular - saileimalai
with visitors, as something moves there and it positively smells of hard
work. Yet the individual procedures, an exact knowledge of the in-
volved and very varied techniques for working the materials are the
| result of a long, continuous learning process. This process exists in the

IN THE JAEGER-
LECOULTRE FORGE

minds and skills of the technicians and ts passed on by the elder ones to
the younger ones. In this respect the “forge” forges a link between the
present and the roots of the watchmaking craft. A link the observer
readily understands,

There would not be much point in trying to track down the
deeper secrets of optimal heat treatment by meticulously recording
how a special watch component such as a steel pinion, a part of the
stopwork or an escape-wheel is heated up to 800 or 850° C in the
furnace, is quenched, re-heated to 300° C and then slowly cooled again
in a hole in the floor for 24 hours. Each process, when repeated a
hundred or a thousand times, adds a little bit to the department's body
of knowledge. Michel Maumary demonstrates this graphically simply by
picking up a spring that has just come out of the “hot” furnace and has
been tempered. It is as hard as glass. He bends itwith the tweezers anda
piece snaps off like a dry branch. An identical part which has also been
“softened” again at only 300°C and then cooled down slowly can easily
be bent and yet springs back.

There is an almost infinite number of grades between “good” and
“adequate”. It is experience which will decide. Some file cards kept
ina little box on the table record, for almost all the parts to be worked,
the recommended values in time and degrees Celsius for the quench
hardening, subsequent annealing and further annealing in order to
improve the flexibility of a component. The file cards are passed down
from one generation to another and, even today, recently-retired Mr.
Edouard Delafosse returns regularly to supervise their updating. The
R alarm clock-sized 50-year-old stop watch from the Chronoflight series,
- which was made by Jaeger-LeCoultre during the Second World War for
these are the stages involved the French and British Air Forces, has found its place on a metal plate
next to the furnace and even today is still keeping good time ~ a truly
convincing long-term test for a watch. Other methods used for many
work operations surprise the observer with their technical refinement
and attention to detail: for example, the metre-long cylinder, in which

This prevents the parts from turning an undesirable bluish-violet colour

But conversely, by deliberately not using the gas, colouring effects can
be produced ike those frequently found in a movement as a sign of
watch culture; blue-annealed steel parts are a fine example of this — but,
of course, only if the remaining Components correspond to the an-
nounced high quality standard, Otherwise it is merely an annoying
optical illusion.

The remaining components include, for example, the two
hundred ratchet wheels which Mrs. Meszaros is placing on a shiny stee!
disc the size of a saucer, one next to another. The process is called
‘annealing between plates”: a second steel plate is laid on the steel disc
with the evenly distributed wheels, and both discs are then fastened
firmly to one another with a torque spanner and five or six thick screws
The “sandwich”, or several of them, moves into one of the steel cylin-
ders with a salt solution through which the gas stream is passing, and
together with this into the furnace. The wheels spend several hours
there at 300° C, time enough to rid themselves of all inner tensions, until
the guardians of the tire are convinced they are now so relaxed - metal-
lurgically - that they will undergo no further changes all their lives. When
afterwards M. Maumary hangs the heavy chain of the block and pulley
through an eye of the cylinder weighing several hundredweight and
pulls the latter out of the furnace, he and his colleague Thierry still have
to wait at least 24 hours until they can be absolutely certain they have
done a good job. The cylinder is first lowered into a hole in the floor. At
a constant “cellar temperature” the parts will be able to mature slowly
like a good wine. Not until the next day does M. Maumary unscrew the
lid of the container and check on the success of the lengthy procedure
under the diamond-set sensor of the comparator: the steel parts have to
exhibit between 850 and 900 degrees of hardness on the Vickers scale,
depending on the application - and they must not be discoloured. By
comparison, the blade of the world-famous Swiss Army Knite manages
just 200-300 Vickers. Even stamps for pressing and other precision tools
hand-made in Jaeger-LeCoultre’s metalworking shop pass through the
furnace before being used. In this respect jaeger-LeCoultre is still deeply
rooted in the ancient tradition of the Valley’s old master-watchmakers,
Who if possible made everything themselves ~ the only people they
trusted to make it as well as they wanted.

to the man with the sharp eye and the unerring comparator, It is als,
obvious that everything described here using the 846 calibre as an
example also applies to all the Manufacture’s other movements, except
tor the “Reverso 60th”, the limited 500-piece anniversary edition. On
this unique watchmaking achievement, with its golden bottom plates
and bridges, the craftsmanship is further enhanced in such a way as to
deserve the label “unique”. The entire next chapter is devoted to this
watch, which has opened up a whole new future for the “Reverso
concept”. But this cannot detract from the enthusiasm about the long
impressive process of making a “normal” mechanical movement. And
finally the old, ordinary Reverso which celebrated its 60th anniversary as
a watch classic in 1991, gave the impetus for the new heights attained by
mechanical watchmaking in Chauvot's old reversible case. Even though
it may be lacking a power-reserve display or a calendar, it has certainly
deserved such detailed interest. As the proverb goes, one can tell a
gourmet by the bread he eats, not by his cake.

Our calibre 846 does not have long to go until its 93 individual
components can form a small, reliably-working timepiece. The barre
partially mounted, the wheels riveted to the arbors and subjected to a
load strain test in the direction of rotation. In the gear chain to be estab-
lished, each wheel has to be able to transmit and withstand a very
precise, calculable force. Each wheel with arbor that has been mounted
is also subjected to a synchronization test in which the arbor rests on
two jewelled bearings while the wheel is rotated with a small air pump
It is just like diagnosing a wheel wobble on a bicycle. Even if the wheel
only wobbles a little it has lost the game against the testing technician
Every single toothed wheel and every pinion have, of course, already
been examined under the microscope to determine whether their
teeth and leaves respectively are complete and undamaged.

We had promised we would have another encounter with an
especially small and yet so important part: the pallet, this truly ingenious
link between the escape-wheel and balance, without which orderly
regular operation of the gear train would be impossible. The pallet with
its two ruby-set arms, the pallet fork and the guard-pin, is the actual
distributor of time. About fifty mainly manual operations alone are
necessary to equip this complicated, three-m

HIDDEN BEAUTY

the mechanical accuracy it will require for high-stress long-term opera.
tion in accordance with the Manufacture’s technical standards. When
the pallet blank has been cut out, trimmed, ground, re-milled in detail,
hardened, drilled and polished, the pin-shaped ruby pallet-stones are
pushed by hand, under the microscope, into the recesses of both Pallet
arms, precisely aligned and fixed with a special varnish in a heat-
treatment process. The atmosphere in the bright, modern workshop in
which this takes place is like the muffled silence of the lending depan-
ment in a bank - which is understandable, as any loud laughter would
sweep the tiny components from the table like a hurricane the palm
trees on the promenade at Miami Beach. A reverent silence, in which
a lot of serious work is done not only for the precision but also for
the well-concealed beauty of a movement. In a final operation a techni-
cian carefully places each individual, ready-mounted pallet on a small
stick the size of a toothpick and with flowing movements sweeps it past
the hairs of a small polishing disc, checks the results every few seconds
under the magnifying glass and finally polishes the part every few
seconds by hand with a small, ordinary-looking piece of wood - elder
pith - which he brings home from time to time after a walk. There is
nothing better for spotless polishing, and precision watchmaking has
been using the method for centuries. It is nice to see there are a few
things that still have to be done by Nature even in the “high tech” age.

This world of microscopic dimensions also forms the setting for
the work of the timer, who assembles the delicate, precise combination
of the balance and balance-spring. But as a hand-picked specialist, she
belongs — and this is also an old tradition — to another profession, that of
the actual “watchmakers”. She also has her place among them in Jaeger-
LeCoultre’s “bel étage”, where life is breathed into the many carefully
manufactured parts for the first time.

High up in the old main building, just beneath the roof but worlds away
from the noisy machines down in the depths of the tactory, reside the
true masters of time measurement: the watchmakers and timers who
work on the Reverso. Bright daylight floods into Jaeger-LeCoultre’s
spacious “bel étage” through large windows on three sides and onto the
workbenches standing in front of them. In the days of founder Antoine
LeCoultre it was already every watchmaker’s natural privilege to work
in front of his own special window. Even today the recently renovated
facade on the rue de la Golisse side, with three rows of closely spaced
windows on each floor, still testifies to the origins of this sensible cus-
tom. Of course, in those days there was still no such thing as electricity
But the wonderfully clear days of the 3,000 foot-high Valley with its pure
air and undistorted light are still among the most loyal allies of the
world’s best watchmakers

Ever since it was founded Jaeger-LeCoultre has expanded in the
typical style of this district, taking advantage of the smallest available
space. The extensions and annexes are lined up and stacked on top of
one another like boxes, with scant regard for architectural considera
tions. But they are certainly practical. The large watch-movement
assembly hall, divided down the middle by a row of shoulder-high
wooden cabinets with drawers, takes up almost the whole at
of one of the older buildings. The benches on the left-hand side are
occupied by those who, in Jaeger-LeCoultre’s jargon, are called the
“electronic watchmakers” i.e. those who assemble the quartz move-
ments; while the “mechanical watchmakers”, who assemble mechani-
cal movements, sit on the right. If there is a subtle hrerarchy between
these two species of watchmaker, it may perhaps be seen in the tact
that on their side the “mechanical watchmakers” have a splendid view
out to the northeast over the turquoise-coloured Lac de Joux, where the
sun rises every morning; whereas the “electronic watchmakers” have to
wait until the evening sun shines into their windows over the melan-

storey

choly meadows, on which brown and white cows are grazing at the end
of the lake. It is indeed a subtle distinction that underlines the fnendly
competition between the left and nght-hand sides of the hall: although
everyone here knows, of course, that Jaeger-LeCoultre Owes its world
fame and exclusive reputation mainly to its high-precision mechanical
movements,

their machines now have to be brought to lite here. The finished moye-
ment easily fits onto a thumbnail: 93 precision components, 17 func-
tional jewels, bridges decorated with Geneva stripes, 15.2 mm long,
13 mm wide, 2.9 mm high and weighing 2-167 grams: the mechanical
hand-winding calibre 846 for the Reverso. With 21,600 semi-oscillations
per hour, the balance that is adjusted to five different positions will
ensure an accuracy that deviates by between zero and a maximum of
ten seconds trom absolute standard time per day - an astonishing
value for this small, robust calibre (broad smiles on the left-hand side of

the hall)

Incidentally: Jaeger-LeCoultre’s calibre 101 with 74 parts and
measuring 14 mm x 4.8 mm x 3.4 mm, which is still the world’s smallest
mechanical movement, weighs only 0.9 grams. It is almost incon-
ceivable that this tiny mechanism actually works. It was assembled tor
the first time on these Jaeger-LeCoultre benches as early as 1929. At her
coronation Queen Elizabeth II received a richly-decorated watch in
which a Jaeger-LeCoultre calibre 101 ticked almost inaudibly. The minr
mechanism was further reduced in weight in 1991, so that it now weighs
in at only 0.4 grams on the precision scales (respectful silence on the
left-hand side of the hall).

All of which proves that Jaeger-LeCoultre’s watchmakers are
capable of working independently and self-confidently even at the
extreme limits of micromechanical engineering. As on so many occ
sions, they themselves have redefined those very limits. This also
applies, for example, to the flattest pocket watch repeater movements
or chronographs. But the watchmakers and timers are naturally aware

This spirit may be seen, for example, in a man like René Rochat,

splendid view. They are watchmaking personalities who all belong
to the elite of their profession, Ultimately, a watchmaking firm's quality
has always been measured by the skill of its specialist watchmakers
particularly timers and finishers. They are the virtuosos of watch preci-
sion. And the quality of a mechanical watch can be gauged - not illog-
cally - by the accuracy with which it works, provided it was adjusted
optimally. For centuries the profession of highly-specialized timers has

With

precision of a t

‘on, the umer optimizes

ur sides of the

JAEGER-LECOULTRE'S
“BEL ETAGE”

able to do more than simply hear in order to detect a deviation at all, To
do so requires a kind of sixth sense plus, for the next Operation, the
steady hand of a brain surgeon: with an electric diamond milling cutter
reminiscent of a dentist's drill, the timer is removing ultra-thin particles
of metal crosswise on the four opposite faces of the balance rim, Why?
The effect of the balance of a mechanical watch within the whole
escapement system with escape-wheel, pallet and balance-spring is
like that of a rotary pendulum. This rotary pendulum which, through
the pallet, ultimately controls the regular succession of tooth cycles of
the escape-wheel and thus also accuracy, only runs evenly when its
weight is completely evenly distributed - that is, when it has no unbal-
anced mass at any point.

Everyone is familiar with the unpleasant effects of a badly-

balanced wheel on a car. It is obviously rather more difficult to balance
perfectly a balance rim which on the calibre 846 is only a few millimetres
large. Even the minutest differences in the molecular structure of
the material can cause imbalances. In the larger calibres of the high-
accuracy category, for example that of the “Reverso 60th”, this precise
balancing of the balance is further perfected by adding small weights on
the balance rim, although of course this makes the balance heavier. The
effect was felt in the days when there were still no shock absorbers for
the jewel bearings of the balance arbor to cushion sharp blows. In those
days the finely turned pivots of the balance arbors frequently snapped
when subjected to shocks. There are other precision regulation systems
using weight distribution, in which small eccentric wheels can be
moved along ~ usually — two balance arms. However, the weight distri-
bution here is not ideal over the whole balance rim. The crucial initial
basic setting of the Reverso calibre 846 is effected on the timers’
benches by removing from the balance rim itself on four sides a few
thousandths of a gram of material until the weight distribution is bal-
anced right around the rim. Deviations occurring later as a result of
normal stabilization of the parts can be corrected subsequently by
means of the index (regulator) by changing the effective length of the
balance-spring. But the original operating accuracy the timer endows
the movement with is the design optimum of achievable accuracy -
unless at some time the balance or the whole escapement system were
to be replaced.

242

It has, of course, taken longer than planned to observe the Jaeger-
LeCoultre watchmakers at work, to hear their stories about “straight-line
levers” and “right-angle levers” with their superior accuracy, and to learn
about escape-wheels, balances, balance-springs and balance-spring
studs — in short everything that goes to make up the pulsating heart of
a watch, which they fit and adjust precisely every day. It is obvious
that they derive pleasure from their creative work.

Several “Atmos” clocks are lined up on a window-sill for long-
term observation. It takes weeks to adjust them but their life expectancy
is about 600 years from the time they leave the Manufacture’s premises.
Time, after all, is relative. But it has practical implications. The ladies and
gentlemen who work on the “bel étage” are as precise about one very
practical aspect of their work as they are about their movements: the
short breaks they take during their working day, from 8.50 to 9.00 a.m.
and from 2.50 to 3.00 p.m. As it is now 2.49 p.m. it is time to bid them
“au revoir”. Besides, in another workshop employees are waiting impa-
tiently for the arrival of the adjusted calibres 846, as they are eager to
complete the Reverso’s long journey through the Jaeger-LeCoultre
workshops before the end of the working day.

How the Reverso

EVENTUALLY THE TIME
COMES TO SAY GOODBYE

Everything has been prepared for the Reverso’s final appearance in the
Manufacture’s workshops: the case is lying on the table ready for fina|
assembly, now that it has passed the last test without the movement
fitted. It has already been fitted with a carrier plate complete with the
two horns which will accompany it all its life. The movement has now
been fitted, adjusted and screwed tight onto the casing-ring. The dial
and hands now have to be mounted, which requires a very steady hand

The case provided for final assembly is then opened, the guard ting
screwed to the bezel is first dismantled and then secured in place again
with the “contents”, i.e. the finished movement itself together with the
casing-ring, The seal is put in position between the bezel and the guard
ring. The bare winding stem now protrudes from the crown tube and
receives the crown fitted with an O-ring seal. The lower seal is carefully
placed in the groove of the inner case on the bridge side, the bottom is
pressed slightly, the lateral screws are tightened again et voila.

It is true that things only go as smoothly as described here
because the many preceding stages and checks have ensured that the
parts which belong together do actually fit together and that no part is
fitted and leaves the workshops uninspected. For the last stage of
assembly, the lateral screws and thus the horns are loosened from the
bottom plate and raised on one side, thereby allowing the case with the
lateral pivots to be inserted into the groove. The screws are tightened
and it is ready. The first “click” heard as the case latches home indicates
that the completed Reverso has come into the world, like a baby crying
for the first time, but more quietly. What it has to go through now
exceeds any comparison with a new-born child, which, though closely
inspected by the doctor, is not checked nearly as critically as the young
Reverso is by Michel Rochat. For he regards his job as head of final
inspection as being slightly different from an obstetrician’s, Mr. Rochat is
more akin to the final instance of a rigorous process of law. All those
who have worked on the creation of the Reverso — and as we have seen,

that means many people - are subjected to his judgment.

Final inspection begins again with a visual inspection of the case.
Next, he tests the pivot mechanism and is only really happy when the
Reverso glides smoothly and pleasantly in the lateral guide slot when
reversed and latches home with a satisfying click. Wearing the magni-

244

fying glass and holding his fingers on the crown, he checks in several
positions to see whether the hands are correctly positioned. In other
words, whether they coincide exactly at 12 o'clock and form a straight
line in the longitudinal direction at 6 o'clock. But in addition, they must
also be properly synchronized, which means that when rotating, both
hands must always keep the same horizontal distance from one another
and the glass.

Finally, his professional mistrust even goes so far as to subject
the Reverso’s “railway” to another close inspection. “Railway” 1s the
term used in the flowery in-house jargon for the minute circle of the
conventional dial models, which is indeed reminiscent of miniature rail-
way tracks. Detective-like, he hunts between the “rails” and in the hour
markings area for any chance fingerprint, so as promptly to hand over
the culprit from assembly. Should the colleagues have forgotten a
minute when making the dial or have moved it by one jot, which hap-
pens very rarely, the dial would not have reached this stage. He has
already carried out the first “dial inspection” before fitting the dial and
has confirmed the accuracy of the hour and minute divisions with a
small signature on the reverse. He also takes a serious interest in the
rigidity of the crown, and of course in the precise functioning of the
movement. To do so, he first winds the spring up fully and checks opera-
tion in six positions, for 16 seconds each. The result is recorded on a test
strip. Thereafter the watch is allowed to perform its normal time-
measurement function undisturbed for 24 hours, betore Michel Rochat
picks it up again in order to repeat the procedure of operation testing
after expiry of the half spring tension. From the two results he calculates
the average value of the maximum deviation, which is never allowed to
exceed 10 seconds plus in 24 hours — but in most cases it is less than this
value. Then the test candidate moves into his large steel cabinet for
another 20 hours, the period during which the movement has to con-
tinue working in order to attain the guaranteed power reserve totalling
44 hours after full winding. Nothing is estimated or promised here
which has not first been checked in each individual case, A volume test
in a modern pressure testing apparatus provides further intormation
about watertightness, as the case has been opened since the last check
for fitting of the movement. For those interested in the details: the
displacement of the gentleman's model is 4 to 4.2 cubic centimetres

Farewells should not be long drawn-out affairs. Eventually th

future, only two things remain to be done before despatch: the Many.
facture writes its name as well as the serial number and case number
with the pantograph on the back of the bottom plate. Finally, the brace.
let and clasp are mounted next door, in the same department. And that
is the finish.

No-one has ever counted the number of hand movements that
are necessary before a Reverso can be worn on its owner's Wrist, to
show ~ or to conceal ~ the time. What purpose would it serve? But as
Mr. Henry-John Belmont, the managing director, once said in answer to
the naive question of whether what his technicians were doing was
actually rational. “No”, he said, “but it is unique”.

For those readers who do not know, here is a brief description

ment of wheels rotating at different speeds. This constant rotational
movement is utilized — in this case - to drive the hour and minute kee
Two largely separate transmission chains are used for this Purpose: fet
the so-called gear train, which is usually partially visible on the brid <
side, the prime function of which is to produce the even, inset
movement in conjunction with the escapement system. The actual
rotation of the hands is provided by a so-called dial train immediately
beneath the dial, which is driven via the extended pinion of one, usually
the first, of the train wheels. _

Looking at the gear train first: the movement requires energy and
an energy storage device. This supply of kinetic energy is present in the
torm of the mainspring in the barrel - a round container which is closed
by a small cover. The mainspring is hooked to and coiled around an
arbor in the barrel. By means of the winding crown or winding stem and
eee, | a the spring may be coiled tightly around the rotatable

248

permits the transmission of kinetic energy from one arbor or shaft to
another. Thus, for example, wheel A engages the pinion of wheel B and
moves wheel B. Wheel B engages the pinion of wheel C and thus turns
wheel C, etc. Practically speaking, the barrel itself may be regarded as
the first wheel of the whole chain, whose teeth engage and turn the
pinion of the so-called centre wheel. Up to now It has been a “slow” and
barely perceptible movement. In relation to its pinion, the much larger
centre wheel does not, of course, rotate more frequently, but it does
rotate much faster on the outer circumference where its “many” teeth
are located if the motion is measured by the distance covered. And that
is the crux of the system. The centre wheel with its “many” teeth now
drives the small pinion of the next wheel, whichis called the third wheel.

wheel. But, unfortunately, the simpler part of the brief functional
description ends here. For what follows the escape-wheel is among
the most complex and yet fascinating features of a mechanical watch:
the escapement system with the pallet, balance and balance-spring. At
some point or other the sequence of movement described hitherto
must ultimately be stopped or, better still, be divided up into move- |
ments of equal amplitude, as otherwise the mainspring might, with |

Thus the escapement system at first interrupts the normal flow BL Arb
of movement and chops it up into very small movements of equal

amplitude. A “circular” movement is converted into a constant “stop |
and go” movement. This works as follows, simplifying somewhat: while |
the force emanating from the barrel would tend constantly to drive the |
escape-wheel forward, the pallet, which can only perform a very small

pendulum movement in between two limit pins, falls with one of its
two arms into the space between two heel-shaped teeth. To be more
precise: one of the forward-moving teeth of the wheel strikes one ot

the bevelled ruby pallet-stones of the pallet. The wheel stops. In the |

technical jargon this phase of a so-called tooth cycle is known as “rest”. ae

can only come from the mainspring in the barrel - i.e. from the eScapo
: é ape:
wheel that is urged forward. We will now see how.

On the balance arbor, beneath the balance rim there js a small
disc, the roller, to which a jewelled pin is attached. During the fits
oscillation of the balance this pin, known as the impulse-pin, falls into
the fork formed at the rear end of the pallet and “strikes” one of the two
fork horns. Which one it strikes depends on which pallet arm has just
fallen into the escape-wheel. If it is the left-hand arm of the pallet, for
example, the impulse-pin strikes the right-hand fork horn and thus raises
the fallen left-hand arm of the pallet slightly, until the bevelled side of
the ruby pallet-stone on the pallet is raised to the level of the front edge
of the heel-shaped tooth. This phase is referred to as “unlocking”. The
reader will remember that the escape-wheel is under “pressure”, i.e. it
is urged forwards in a clockwise direction. The tooth that is now moving
forward and that was blocked a second ago further raises the ruby
pallet-stone of the pallet by rapidly sliding beneath its oblique plane,
This phase is called “lifting”. In the process, of course, the fork at the
other end of the pallet completes a rapid swivelling movement and
imparts an impulse to the impulse-pin on the roller, the impulse-pin
being still in the fork notch. The impulse-pin leaves the swivelling range
of the fork and, together with the roller and the balance, begins a not-
quite-circular journey. The hitherto-blocked tooth of the escape-wheel
can push its way through under the raised pallet-stone of the rising
pallet arm. At this moment, however, the second arm of the pallet is
already falling into a space between two other heel-shaped teeth of the
escape-wheel and blocks the wheel once again. This phase is called
“dropping”. The escape-wheel is stopped until the balance-spring has
braked and reversed the rotating movement of the balance that has
been initiated. This time the impulse-pin on the roller releases the
blocking pallet arm by imparting an impulse to the other fork horn from
its blocked position, and so on.

The sequence of movements has been designed to withstand
external shocks: the so-called guard-pin between the fork notch of
the pallet, together with the safety-roller on the balance, prevents
premature unlocking should the watch receive a shock during a tooth

just a few words about the different materials used in a watch
ment: an old and sensible principle of mechanical engineering
; that materials of as different a hardness as possible should
state eonone another at points of heavy friction, especially if it is not
pia to have them constantly lubricated. A watch is lubricated rela-
et seldom. Watchmakers meet the requirement of optimal friction
cteristics that can be guaranteed on a long-term basis not only by
having steel pivots run in brass bearings (the bottom plate) but also
where steel pivots run in even harder synthetic-ruby bearings. The same
plies when, for example, toothed wheels made of softer brass mesh
sith the harder steel pinions of the next wheel. And the principle really
comes into its own with the heavily-stressed interplay of extremely hard
ruby pallet-stones and the steel teeth of the escape-wheel.

THE FUTURE
HAS ALREADY BEGUN

“Itis time again for the Reverso”. The sentence that stands at the end of

Larges finer and the the introduction to this book was not intended to bean empty phrase,
ee since the tribute to the sixty year-old reversible watch truly opens anew
pinnacle of mechanical chapter in the Reverso story. The watch’s future is certain to be even
watchmaldags Fram finer, more ambitious and more exciting, and will represent the pinnacle
of achievement in mechanical watchmaking. A cat has nine lives,

according to the proverb. It is tempting to transpose the adage to the
the Minute Repeater, Reverso, as its second, or in fact third, life has already begun:

a winter of 1989/90, to be precise. That was when the
from the “Grande

the jubilee watch to

in the
first preparations
and conceptual sketches for a Reverso “60th” to mark the anniversary

Taille” to the “Mini”. the following year were being formulated and put down on Paper,

Just as it was sometimes difficult to uncover the details of how
it, the original Reverso was created in 1931 and how itwas re-discovered in
$$ the seventies from the dust of the archives and to prise the information

from the memories of the eyewitnesses who were still alive, this time it
Was Correspondingly simple to record the story. A chronicler rarely has
an opportunity to witness the early stages of the creation of a “new
watch” in the workshops, which are well screened from prying eyes.
Perhaps this small effort will save posterity some laborious research
work in 60 years’ time, should they wish to celebrate the Reverso’s
120th anniversary. That would be in the year 2051. By then the watches
leaving the Manufacture today will be just as sought-after by collectors
as the Reversos from the thirties already are today. But how will time be
measured and reckoned in 2051? How will people manage their time?
Will one still want - or be able - to make such watches? Nobody can
answer these questions right now and only a few of today’s players will
be around in 60 years’ time to witness the answers provided by history.
Everyone else has to seek comfort in the fact that working on a new
watch is like opening a window to the future on a pitch-dark night. Only
as day breaks can one gradually see what lies beyond it.

But it is always people who open those windows, then as now.

No new developmentis set in motion without both its well-known and

many unobtrusive protagonists. Just as René-Alfred Chauvot, César de

Trey and Jacques-David LeCoultre have been remembered by posterity

THE INVENTORS’ HOUR

Winter has always been a very special season in the Valley, with snow |
: - and an icy cold outside. Indoors, in the well-heated offices and wi. |

How the “Reverso shops, one feels cut off from the rest of the world. In former time
= ae ee watchmakers made a virtue of the necessity of living with the hes
elements by regarding this isolation and peacefulness as an Opportunity
for meticulous, exacting work. The Jura winter was the mother of the
truly great watchmaking inventions.

Despite the telephone, fax machines and almost all-year-round
road and rail links, a noticeable trace of this beneficial seclusion has
remained to this day; because in winter the only people - apart from
holidaymakers — who travel to the Valley are those who absolutely have
to. Gunter Blumlein, the chairman of the board of directors, forinstance.
Thus one winter's day in 1989 he assembled a team of creators around
the long table in the conference room ~ a cosy room with creaking |
floorboards, small windows and an uninterrupted view of the snowy
white valley. The topic of discussion was the future of the Reverso, Itwas
to give rise to a whole series of long meetings.

A surprising discovery:

A complicated

“The product is the message”, was Blumlein’s clear entrepre-
neurial creed. It was this sentence that suddenly opened up, with com:
had already pelling logic, the still-hidden potential dormant in the Reverso, like a
long-lost key opening an old, well-guarded strongroom. For 160 years
without a break the Manufacture has been successfully translating a
message into the language of watchmaking: making high-quality, com-
It. plicated mechanical watches even better and more beautiful. As the
bearer of this key message, the “Reverso 60th” — or “soixantiéme” in

yi Be,
French - could thus only become an ultra-fine watch. |

complications require space, and there was no leeway for them jn -
dimensions of the previous “gentleman's” Reverso with the calibre 846
measuring only 5 by 6 lines. Ginter Blumlein then uttered the words
that solved the dilemma: “We'll make the Reverso larger, we'll open up
the previous case dimensions to some completely novel watch ideas

At first there was only silence and s¢ eptical looks; finally, relief, As \c
known, it is always difficult to depart from old, well-trodden tracks

But then a surprising discovery was made: one of the Manufac-
ture’s old watchmakers, who acts as a kind of guardian of the company’s
past, remembered that such an idea had already been mooted: his
documents contained some in-house correspondence, or rather a
written memo sent by the then chief calibre-maker Reymond to the
management in 1944. In the memo Reymond was apparently replying
to Jac ques-David LeCoultre’s inquiry as to whether it would be possible
to estimate the cost of manufacturing a complicated movement on the
basis of the calibre then used in the Reverso, the 11 U. In his reply
Reymond referred to the existing prototype of a complicated move-
ment, built in 1938, with a retrograde calendar (calendrier rétrograde),
display of the day and month ina window and a moon phase display. He
stated, however, that it was impossible for him even to estimate the cost
of manufacturing this individual piece. It may be inferred from these few
lines that he thought it would be virtually prohibitive to manufacture.
Thus the first genuinely complicated Reverso sank back into oblivion,
which meant the idea took a different course and was partially imple-
mented in two wristwatches in 1946 and 1949 respectively — one round,
the other rectangular - on the basis of the round calibre 486, although
there it had a rotating calendar and display of the day, month and moon

THE FRENCH CONNECTION

Paris, Gare de Lyon, one Friday evening. The orange-red and BICY Stripe
= —<— high-speed train “Pays de Vaud” glides slowly and NOst silently
From the spirit of into the terminus station in the 12th arrondissement aftera three-and-a
the Bastille half-hour, 300-mile journey from Vallorbe. The shadow of the gigantic
= —_—_—— steel-frame hall falls like a dark curtain over the drawings on the small
in Paris to the genius _ table in the compartment. Janek Deleskiewicz quickly clears away tho
| watchmakers in papers and stows them in his grip bag. The sliding door of the coach
| -__—— hisses open, just one more step and he is back on home ground. Eve y
Friday he goes through the same ritual: Arrive, bonjour Paris. Jacger-
LeCoultre’s designer is the link between the Reverso and its French
roots: every weekend he returns to the watch’s origins from little Le

IL. people, cars and noise by turning into quiet little rue Traversiére. The
flute-makers used to live here in the days of the French monarchy. One
last flute-maker has survived, with a tiny shop and workshop at the
beginning of the street. Today the numerous artists and small gallery-
owners in this district are waging a tough battle against the modernist
planners who, starting with the glass and concrete architecture of
the colossal Opéra de la Bastille, are eating deeper and deeper into the
district’s old core. An ordinary townhouse in the narrow, ravine-like
street is Janek’s destination and home. The top floor, with its old, bare
beams and cosy corners, which he himself converted years ago, is a pri-
vate haven of peace for him and his family in the perpetual motion of
the metropolis, reminiscent as it is of one of Jean Tinguely’s anarchic,
indefatigable mega-machines. Nobody could try to swim against the
tide of Paris without being overwhelmed. But the two n hab-
itants of Paris “intra muros” and the 20 million visitors who pour in each
year all become imbued with a touch of the wild, inexhaustible vita

of a city reputed to be a world centre of culture, art and libidinous night
life.

IGH MECH & CO

itis difficult to describe the character of Roger Guignard, who played -
important part in the creation of the “Reverso 60th”, without having
passed by the lage cinema in Le Sentier one evening in search of
entertainment. The brown-bearded man, who spends his days at the
Manufacture drawing the most complicated watch movements Ona
computer-aided design screen, shares his passion for 800d films twice a
week with the paying guests whom he lures into his small cinema. The
latest film posters pinned up near his place of work in the Manufacture
provide an early indication ot what his leisure-time activity is, It May be
under the steely gaze of Sean Connery trying to escape his Pursuers on
the submarine “Red October”, or under the eyes of Gérard Depardiey
as the eloquent Cyrano de Bergerac; either way, R. Guignard becomes
totally engrossed in the microcosm of a watch movement.

Once the new dimensions for the movement of a larger-sized
Reverso had been established and the other technical specifications
such as calendar, power-reserve and small seconds-hand had been
defined, R. Guignard looked inquiringly at his head of department,
Jean-Claude Meylan, who shook his head: there was nota single calibre
in the extensive record cabinets of Jaeger-LeCoultre, which over the
years had developed about 200 different movement calibres, that
would have fitted the large Reverso straight off. It had to be a very
traditional movement, with hand winding and the small seconds-hand
being driven directly via the extended axis of the fourth wheel — as ona
pocket watch or on the early Reverso calibres of the type 11 U and 11
UO. From the point of view of the train system and of the reduction and
multiplication ratios of the wheels and pinions, R. Guignard was able to
use the legendary LeCoultre hand-winding calibre 818/4. It is a 9-line
classic movement with 18,000 semi-oscillations, the characteristic
narrow train bridge and the clearly visible centre-wheel as the decora-
tive, visual centre. But this calibre was originally round and thus too
large. There was no alternative to the complicated computer-aided
design process, as the positions of the bridges and wheels had to be
recalculated and re-determined due to the completely different
amount of space available. In addition, the planned complications ~ a
calendar with a rotating hands display and a power-reserve display — had
to be re-designed and ncorporated into the movement. But Roger
Guignard was familiar with the workings ofa mechanical movement and

Anyone wishing to see Sylvain Golay and knocking on the
wooden door leading to Jaeger-LeCoultre’s top-level watchn
laboratory is well advised to leave his borsalino outside on the hat
~and not to forget it when leaving. Othenvise the grey item of he
might, when cut into small round discs, perform an extremely usef |
function - to the greater glory of the “Reverso 60th”, for example. Ba,
the story of how and by whom the Manufacture’s complicated a
ments are brought to the pinnacle of perfection actually begins quite
differently and elsewhere: on a wonderful summer's evening high
above the Valley in a small chalet on the edge of the forest, on which ie
moon happens to be shining. A picturesque backdrop for a cheerful
group of people who are meeting there after driving up through the
forest. They are surrounded by deep silence. In the chalet, owned by
Sylvain Golay, sit some members of the Valley's watchmaking elite at
the long wooden table, together with some friends and their wives. That
evening managing director Henry-John Belmont and chairman of the
board Gunter Blumlein are there too. The high esteem in which top
watchmakers are traditionally held crosses hierarchical lines. The fact
that some of S. Golay’s guests work as watchmakers for other renowned
manufacturers is of no consequence. The people have known each
other for years. On the wood-fired stove one of them, who is of Italian
origin, is preparing a polenta with rabbit which would make the head
chef at Hotel de la Lande in Le Brassus weep with envy. The dinner is
washed down with wine from Tuscany — a superb meal, crowned with
fresh strawberries and a bottle of champagne from the “cellar” beneath
the solid wooden floorboards. Only two people at the table are wearing
a “Reverso 60th”, S$. Golay and H.-J. Belmont. Noblesse oblige.

“stand
adgear

Next morning at his workplace we meet Sylvain Golay, the head
of Jaeger-LeCoultre’s smallest and finest department, which only
assembles movements that are extremely complicated or that require
an outstanding level of perfection and finish. It is a relatively small room
with a low ceiling and a venerable, highly-polished parquet floor. In the
corner just behind the door stands a neat row of large grey felt slippers.
Their purpose is not to protect the historic floor on which generations of
watchmakers have walked, but to muffle the noise of footsteps when
one of the watchmakers leaves his workbench. Silence and concentra-
tion are essential here. Itis no exaggeration to say that all the employees

HATS OFF
TO THE REVERSO

who work in this department are true micromechanical enginecting
craftsmen who have had ample opportunity over the years to Prove
their outstanding skills - which are no mean feat at this level of Watch-
making. It goes without saying that the anniversary Reverso is assembled
here, and only here. This also applies to other special watches with com-
plications.

Few non-professionals have an inkling of what “Movement
assembly” means at this lofty level. It has something to do with practis-
ing a love of watchmaking, with a form of devotion to the highest
possible perfection of detail, which far exceeds practical benefits and
extends into the realm of pure craftsmanship. This work is inconceivable
without being rather obsessed with watchmaking. If it is complex
enough, a machine can also make a watch from 193 individual compo-
nents; but making a “Reverso 60th”, for example, would definitely
exceed its understanding, for where a machine’s capabilities end js
precisely where top-level watchmaking begins. Wheels, levers and
springs, which have each previously passed stringent quality controls,
are not simply assembled, but are checked and adjusted by the watch-
maker when actually functioning together so as to ensure they are
synchronized and have the correct separation and bearing clearance.
A good assembly technician will effect tiny corrections even if all
components are absolutely in order. For example, the angle of the
calendar control lever may still need to be corrected slightly or a spring
straightened so that it can move without rubbing against other com-
ponents. It remains difficult to explain this spark of watchmaker’s intui-
tion that turns a good mechanism into an optimally-attuned one. It calls
for a dedication to craftsmanship that transcends the normal concept

of quality.

This is particularly true of all the actions that make a movement
not only better but more beautiful. For someone like Sylvain Golay,
the two concepts are indissociable. It is not something that can be learnt
at school. Only a craftsman who immerses himself in the centuries-old
tradition of exclusive watchmaking, as it is staunchly defended in the
Vallée de Joux, can find a meaning to the question: Why must a move-
ment not only work properly but be beautiful down to the last screw
head? A number of self-built tools can be found here on the benches

S. Golay does not like to be observed too closely when making his ye :

from him. Oh yes, ladies’ hats are also among the objects this wat h-
maker covets, on account of their softer felt. He appears in the M
ture with his extensive home-made collection of prepared Polishing
discs, softer and harder, larger and smaller, and uses them to give the
mechanical movement of the “Reverso 60th” a true polish. For the
technique by which a connoisseur ultimately recognizes the outstand-
ing quality of a watch movement is what is known as chamfering. This
means that all sharp edges of even the smallest steel parts are smoothed
and polished, that is, “chamfered”. This applies to the tiny pallet, no part
of which shows any sharp edges after the time-consuming, intric
procedure, just as it does to the steel parts of the winding and hand-
setting mechanism, which afterwards disappear under a bridge. S. Golay
comments, “The advantage of my method is that it allows one to break
ate disk for each the edges and at the same time optimally to polish the part”. Only
ymponent and each maternal where even the tiniest disc would be too rough, for example on t
arbor pivots or on many corners of the pallet, does he polish by hand

“eversare” meaning “to turn over, rotate”. However, the word is not
- very common. In Latin literature the verb “revertere” is more commonly

used for “turn over”. But the name “Reverso” could not have been

derived from that verb without some grammatical sleight of hand.

that the Reverso is more than a technical timepiece — and more than
a nostalgic relic from the thirties. The watch remains unchanged as the
IL, expression of a sovereign, independent way of handling one’s time,
— - - Turning the case corresponds exactly to the noble gesture with which
Our grandfathers pulled their hunters from their waistcoat pockets,
snapped open the covers and then put “artificial time” away once again.
It remained in their pockets until their own inner clock required another
synchronization with the mechanical kind. Today this basically very 9
human aspect makes the Reverso something special. Adapting a well- : Whee i
known expression, one might say: Reverso, ergo sum(’). Our apologies
to Descartes (1596-1650), whose fundamental basis of philosophy:
“cogito, ergo sum — | think, therefore | am’, is in no way profaned by this
slight adaptation. Rotation, turning away and turning back, the re-orien-
tation and verification of one’s own standpoint, which the Reverso
symbolizes with its turning mechanism, are an inseparable part of
life. Hence: “Reverso, ergo sum” - | turn, therefore | am! Nothing is as
durable as change.

No-one knows this better than those who give shape to constant
changes of taste: designers. The idea of confronting three internationally
famous designers with the Reverso, that is, an object whose external
appearance has not changed for more than 60 years, therefore ap-
peared to be particularly intriguing. It was an experiment with, and a test
of, the asserted timelessness of the Reverso principle: Hartmut Esslinger,
Ferdinand A. Porsche and Bruno Sacco each approached the Reverso
in their own way, critically, searchingly and bearing in mind the question
of whether the Reverso, in their view, can still be contemporary. Three
different designers, three very different replies.

=== PAGE 156 ===
An interview with

F.A. Porsche

Porsche-style design

in a telephone

“THE REVERSO IDEA IS STILL
VALID EVEN TODAY”

Little Zell am See lies way up in the Austrian Alps, though not so far y >
of course, that tourists cannot find their way there in summer as 7
Winter, But twenty years ago Ferdinand Alexander Porsche disc oveied
there what they are looking for: a quiet place where one has time to
reflect on the important things in life. One of the best-known designers
of our age, who in 1962 designed the dream car bearing the Magic
number “911” at the rear, believes in putting down roots, in adherence
to traditional values like hard work, soundness and quality. The impres-
sion he gives ts of pleasant modesty rooted in firm self-confidence. Cars
are in fact only a sideline for him today. Born in 1935 and grandson of the
legendary cat-maker, he was thrown out of the Bauhaus-oriented
Design College in Ulm after the basic course — and he still does not
know exactly why to this day. In the fifties and sixties he first designed
Formula | and Formula II racing cars for his father’s sports car firm in
Stuttgart, then the racing sports car “904” and, finally, the Porsche “911”,
But in those days he was already interested in many other objects with
which he could fulfil his design creed: “Good design is not externally
imposed styling but an attempt to reduce objects to their essential func-
tion”. And that is precisely what he has done with cameras, furniture,
lamps, cooking utensils, televisions, computers, spectacles, cigarette
lighters, bags, telephones — and watches, to mention just a few exam-
ples. Almost all these objects are characterized by a kind of “Porsche
effect”, which makes them that little bit better, more practical, more
useful and more beautiful.

No-one would guess from the outside of his small design studio
on the edge of Zell am See that successful world-class product designs
are regularly devised, drawn and modelled behind its timber-clad walls
and large windows. Even the simple business name “pd” — for Porsche
Design — at the entrance is a stylish piece of understatement. The head
of the business, who was just coming out of the design room, where he
had been discussing a new design with one of his employees, fits the bill
exactly: unpretentious, deliberate and clear — just like his designs. The
discussion about the Reverso on the table in front of him developed
into a studium generale about people and the things they use. But more
about the people who should derive benefit from the objects he and

“THE REVERSO IDEA IS STILL
VALID EVEN TODAY”

He attaches particular importance to one distinction: “Design is
different from art”. For years F.A. Porsche has been defending the same
theoretical position as his colleague Ot! Aicher, who died in 1991 and
who vehemently fought to prevent design from being subjected to the
personality cult of the design market and from being turned into an
object of speculation. Of course, “As soon as one has made a name
for oneself, one is used to represent a high standard and thus also a
corresponding price,” said F.A. Porsche and proceeded to tell how
a customer once came to him with a finished ob

ject and simply asked
him for permission to add the name Porsche. “We don’t do that”, he
replied, “but we can develop a new product for you”; which they did,
and it became a classic.

He knows perfectly well that his name is the subject of expensive
“marketing”, which basically he deplores. What he and |

his team can do is
to try to prevent a discrepancy from developing between the high expec-

tations people have of an object bearing the name “Porsche” and its actual
value or practical value. Therefore he attaches particular importance to
ensuring that the objects he has designed are sufficiently functional. He
does not wish to be a mere packaging artist whose role is to conceal
a product’s weaknesses. His products are largely black, grey or metal-
coloured, “because the shape and not the colour is to be highlighted”,

“Good design never exists in a vacuum”, he said, “
think further than other people who re-design things, and also incorpo-
rate new production methods and materials’. He reminded us of
Leonardo da Vinci, who for example had already foreseen the helicop-
ter 500 years ago, but was unable to build one, given the production
facilities available at the time. Asa designer F.A. P

orsche thinks radically:
when starting an assignment, he asks the question: How would an
everyday object be made today if it were to be re-invented in the light of

today’s production facilities? The object may be a pair of spectacles, ora
speedboat, cigarette lighter or motorcycle crash helmet. For example,

THE MAN WHO DANCES
WITH THE STAR

It he happens not to be unveiling a new car in Detroit, then perhaps it
's in Geneva or Tokyo. When looking for Bruno Sacco one also has to
think of one of the company’s outposts in New York, Los Angeles or
Portland. Sometimes, however, he and his forceful personality do
animate the transparent Plexiglass desk in his home office at Mercedes-
Benz in Sindelfingen near Stuttgart. “Glasnost’ is also visible on the Wall
behind him: the glass showcases fixed there replace the Modigliani or
Chirico. The cases contain row upon row of model-sized Mercedes — in
other words almost nothing but genuine “Saccos”. For more than
30 years Mercedes-Benz’s chief designer, who confidently heads a team
of 300 employees, has had closer control than anyone else of the cars
so prized by Swabians. On a Mercedes, car design even becomes the
portrayal of national identity by other means, since for customers all
over the world the make blends proverbial Swabian hard work with the
German predilection for perfection to form a highly symbolic object.

This poses no problem for designer B. Sacco, an “Italian-Swabian”
with a German passport, who was born in Udine, Italy, in 1933. On the
one hand, he believes in his role of guardian of a tradition that dates
back in his firm to the very origins of car-making - after all, Daimler and
Benz did invent the automobile. The tradition requires that “The owner
of an older model must not be put to shame by a newer one”. On the
other hand, B. Sacco has caused the generations of cars from Stuttgart,
which he has designed to a large extent, to evolve gradually towards his
philosophy: “Good design may be recognized by a renunciation of all
superfluity”.

When he joined the Group in 1958 aged 25, he had completed a
traineeship as a land surveyor, had caught up academically by taking his
A-levels, completed a mechanical engineering course in Turin and
acquired his first design experience with Ghia and Pininfarina. As a
young designer, he worked in Stuttgart on the large Mercedes “600”, the
classic Roadster “230 SL” and on research using the “C 111” prototypes.
By 1975 he was already head of the main Design Department. There
design had long outgrown its tacit place as the “spare wheel” of brand
marketing and was acknowledged as a decisive factor of product de-
velopment. Since the end of the seventies his signature has been on all
of Mercedes’s automobile and commercial-vehicle models, including

308

he new “SL” generation, the new S-class (1991) and the corresponding
: hols which he was unveiling in Detroit when he sent us his impres-
oupes, :

Sas of the Reverso in January 1992.

B. Sacco, the man who ideally blends the Italian culture of his
birth with slightly cooler Germanic rationality in technological matters,
is indisputably an authority on style. His appearance is impressive too.
? r colleagues occasionally maintain that he resembles Hemingway,
court of his steely-grey beard. The mature Sean Connery would
; k like him, if he, like B. Sacco, always wore a double-breasted suit
a a fashionable tie. The fact that he gained his first experience of cars
Oh rents’ Fiat 500 and that his early ancestors were pirates on the
i ee are just two aspects of his colourful life and background.
haps his ancestors’ piracy lives on, albeit tempered, in his Passion
for speedboats, in which he likes to cruise off Rapallo. Connoisseurs,
and they often come from far away, know that Sacco is Sacco. The man
who dances with the star. Or to be more precise: on the star of car
design. His department's design drawings, which are only finalized at
the end of alengthy decision-making process, set the standard. They are
the moon at which the competition has to bark.

Car designers, admittedly, regard themselves asa special breed of
designers. But when their work is done everyone thinks he is competent
to judge it because the car — even a car one does not own — is —
property. The Bauhaus-inspired design dogma that form has to fo low
function does not mean much to B. Sacco, as with cars it is rather point-
less to philosophize about function. But he does like lengthy discus-
sions about car design as an external expression of the pleasure of
technical innovation, or as a means of making automobiles safer and
more habitable. Can design also be a sin? Clearly it can. But the long
line of cars he has designed contains not a single flop.

The distances Bruno Sacco frequently travels today are nothing
unusual for the chief designer of a world Group. His ‘apelin
team in Stuttgart will establish contacts anywhere, if ares
Incidentally, he faxed the handwritten text of his mont ie ne
appraisal of the Reverso from Detroit, where he had just unvet

THE MAN WHO DANCES
WITH THE STAR

“I’m not a watch connoisseur. When judging and selecting timepieces
| have usually allowed myself to be guided more by emotions than by
critical, objective knowledge. When my father went to war in 1941 | was
able to admire a fine gold Omega on his wrist. | remembered this for a
long time as a standard for everything connected with watches.

Over the following years | used several watches until | was finally
caught by the fashionable Swatch craze. Then Manfred Fritz asked me
whether | was prepared to take a critical look at a Reverso from Jaeger-
LeCoultre. The maker’s name already meant something to me, but not
the particular model.

l agreed because, as a humanist, I’m also curious. The Reverso
model made in 1991 arrived; with hindsight | cannot say it was love at
first sight. Nevertheless | decided to start my appraisal, removing my
mass-produced chronometer and pulling tight the light ostrich leather
strap of the steel Reverso.

| did have some points to criticize. | have never really liked
watches with a square or angular dial, as a circular dial with a simple divi-
sion into twelve is the right solution from a functional point of view. The
literature supplied enabled me to learn something about the Manufac-

ture and the philosophy behind this watch. | now knew as well why it
was called “Reverso”.

The idea of developing a watch whose dial could simply disap-
pear if rough treatment was expected has certainly been worth pursu-
ing. This inspired idea was first put into practice in 1931. The turning case
made a strictly rectangular design essential, because of the need for two
parallel sides, thereby overcoming my main criticism of the rectangular
dial. For the rationally-minded ergonomist, however, one question
remains unanswered: What does the wearer do if, with the watch in
reversed position, he should wish to glance furtively at the dial? The
hand he now needs to turn the case back, that is, to execute a typical
re-reversing movement, is firmly grasping a tennis racquet ora polo club
if the watch is being used as the inventor intended.

Several months have now passed since my first encounter with
the Reverso. It has been my constant companion. Inconspicuous and

310

yet exciting. A clear piece of Art Deco which, whenever | looked at it, re-
minded me of familiar, prestigious objects from the early thirties such as the
aransatlantico” Rex, the Daimler-Benz “Mannheim”, the Martini skyscraper
in Genoa, the government building on Avenida Presidente Vargas in Rio de
Janeiro and many other symbols of that period of our recent history.

Benz “Mannheim

| saw the finest Reverso yesterday: the “Reverso 60th” on the
wrist of my acquaintance Domingo P., a true lover of fine timepieces.
Everything is perfect on that watch, which in “reversed” position reveals
the mechanical movement, even the “Reverso” idea is developing
further, because instead of the original need to protect the watch from
shocks by a turning movement, it is now possible to look into the move-
ment without having to remove the watch - a much more interesting
option for aesthetes. | cannot help having the impression that most
Reverso owners over the years have purchased their watches not in
order to play polo but simply because they were guided by emotion, as
| would be.

By the way, I’m looking for a “Reverso 60th”.
Bruno Sacco
Detroit, January 7, 1992”.
