THE “FROG KING”
AND THE REVERSO

His hallmark is a wide-eyed little green frog poised to jump. Not a bad
subject for an interpretation exercise: Where is the princess hiding who
kisses the frog, so that it changes into a prince? As itis a real-life story, not
a fairy-tale, the solution to the riddle is also more down-to-earth: some
twenty years ago in a small village called Altensteig in the Black Forest
there lived a young man who was soon to complete his design studies,
During the holidays he designed and built ina garage a completely new
stereo system with round, pleasing forms and was promptly awarded a
highly-esteemed German industry prize for good design. The company
“Wega”, at the time an up-market home entertainment electronics
firm, noticed the designer's talent, signed a contract with him and was
tremendously successful. Suddenly the whole world knew the de-
signer’s name: Hartmut Esslinger. In other words, it was almost a
modern-day fairy tale.

The connection with the frog, which in 1982 became the brand
symbol of the firm H. Esslinger founded, “frogdesign”, can only be
understood properly if the reader knows the age-old traditions of small
Black Forest villages. Their inhabitants are mockingly associated —
usually by neighbours in the nearby villages — with a species of animal
that is particularly abundant there: foxes, crows, slow-worms ~ and
frogs. Hartmut Esslinger soon turned his success into a tribute to his
home village. The small frog from Altensteig quickly conquered the
wide world in great leaps: today, with 60 employees and branches in
California, Japan, Taiwan, Singapore and France, “frogdesign” is one of
the biggest design firms, having designed no less than 1,000 products
which are used by about 150 million people: computers, television sets,
refrigerators, bathroom fittings, cameras, saucepans, motorcycles,
synthesizers and industrial robots. The successful design of Apple’s
“Macintosh” computer also originates from the “frogs”, as does Sony’s
“Trinitron” television. frogdesign’s customers include Lufthansa and
Marklin, SEL and Sharp, Vickers and Villeroy & Boch, Eastman Kodak,
Logitech and Sun, NEC and Olympus, Sony, Toyota and Yamaha. Apart
from industrial design, the dynamic firm today offers comprehensive
marketing consultancy services.

Hartmut Esslinger and his team have re-written the design law

“form follows function” to read “form follows emotion”. Many of the
products designed by “frogdesign” give a soft, rounded, functional

312

impression and they flatter the eye or hand that uses them. The Euro-
pean president of “frogdesign”, Mr. Thomas Gerlach, recently described
the firm’s self-set task as follows: design makes visions visible. “frog-
design” regards itself as a specialist in designs of the future,

Hartmut Esslinger, the chairman of “frogdesign” worldwide, runs
the office in California and spends most of his time there. He likes
Porsche and Mercedes cars, playing jazz on the piano, mountaineering
and rock-’n’-roll. He also designs clocks: he designed the radio clocks in
Junghans’s “Mega” line. These are totally accurate timepieces which are
linked via regular radio impulses from a long-wave transmitter near
Frankfurt to the “atomic clock” in Brunswick. But the watch with two
faces, which has been made in Le Sentier since 1931, strikes something
in him that he also imparts to his products: “emotions”:

“Time is the fourth dimension; it complements our notion ot
space.

Time is the limitation within which we have to fulfil our lives

Time is also the reward for a process of learning and feeling that
searches for the deeper meaning of things - until we finally understand it.

Why is the Reverso such a classic expression of time?

It is a perfect reflection of the “Zeitgeist” for which it was made —
Art Deco.

Life was difficult at the beginning of the thirties. The deadly battle
between fascism and the free world had already begun. Culture and re-
ality were drifting apart. It became important tor people to make small
dreams come true.

When contemplating the Reverso today, one notices that its
inventive concept and timeless beauty have survived unchanged. The
Possibility it offers of simply turning away time still seems to be just as
topical and contemporary, as this masks a deep-telt human need. One
feels this need, for example, when meeting a good triend again alter a
long absence, Time should be of no importance on such occasions.

That is why | like this watch’.

FALLING IN LOVE WITH
THE REVERSO

“Ever since | made this film with Fréderic Dard I’ve hardly taken my
Reverso off”, Jeanne Moreau told us. The film was “La vieille qui marchait
dans la mer”. The doyenne of French cinema, who has acted in well over
Claude Brasseur, 50 films with such famous directors as Bufuel, Orson Welles, Vadim,
Truffaut, Fassbinder, Antonioni and Louis Malle, made an unusual decla-
ration of affection: “Before shooting ‘La vieille’ | was given a choice of
watches and | immediately fell in love with the Reverso, | was fascinated
by its originality and beauty”. Jeanne Moreau was captivated by the
turning case. She most likes wearing the Reverso with the engraved side
upward: “I find it reassuring that the beautiful moments remain unaf-
fected by the tide of time”. The actress who appeared in such famous
films as “Les amants”, “Jules et Jim”, “The Trial”, “Mata Hari”, “Viva Maria” and
“Jeanne la Francaise” prizes the mysterious and hidden side of her Reverso
and only turns it over when she wants to know the time. What could be
more suitable for a brilliant actress who, as a woman with so many different
faces, fascinates an audience of millions with her mysterious charm?

Jeanne Moreau,

Claude Lelou When actor Claude Brasseur looks at the Reverso on his wrist

FALLING IN LOVE WITH
THE REVERSO

not looking: he used to play with it, so as to try to figure out the secret
of the everyday “magic trick” with which Pierre Brasseur was able to
make time appear and disappear again with a flick of his fingers. Claude
Brasseur recalls that at the time he secretly wanted to be able to do the
trick everywhere, just like his father, but he could not manage to turn
the case over. Thus the watch kept its fascinating secret from him for a
long time.

But one day Pierre Brasseur’s old Reverso, which his son wore
later in life, disappeared. So a few years ago Claude’s wife Michéle
surprised her husband with a gift that gave him immense pleasure: a
new Reverso, which now has a dual sentimental value for him. The back
is decorated with the monogram “CEB”, for Claude Espinasse Brasseur,
which is the full name of the artist who has achieved worldwide fame
with his roles in films like “La boum”, “Taxi boy”, “Joyeuses Paques”,
“Descente aux enfers” or “Détective”. Claude Brasseur has worn it with
affection ever since, and likes telling the story of his first encounter with
the Reverso. In one respect he follows the tradition of his father Pierre,
who never openly wore a watch when invited out in the evening
“because the host might think one was in a hurry to leave again”. The
Reverso, of course, makes it easy for Claude Brasseur to avoid giving
that impression - a turning movement suffices.

If ever there was an enthusiastic watch lover among film directors,
it is Frenchman Claude Lelouch. He has found not only the subject of
time particularly appealing but also the myth of his Reverso: on the back
is engraved C. Lelouch’s lucky number “13”. In his film “Y’a des jours et
des lunes” with Gérard Lanvin, Vincent Lindon and Patrick Bruel, the
Reverso at one point even makes a screen appearance. A Reverso also
features in “La belle histoire’: on the wrist of his wife, actress Marie-
Sophie Lelouch.

It is impossible to count or list all the minor film appearances the
Reverso has made over the last 60 years. Many directors incorporated
the charming, curiosity-arousing gesture into a plot. Ina Wim Wenders
film an eccentric scientist is constantly playing with his Reverso. A
Reverso made an appearance in the 1991 British production “The
Favour, the Watch and the Very Big Fish”, along with Natasha Richardson
and Jeff Goldblum. While on the subject of Great Britain, some time
ago singer Elton John disproved the prejudice widely believed on the

316

Continent that the British have no sense of good taste. In a Paris shop
window he glimpsed a Reverso the like of which he had never seen
before. He quickly made up his mind, entered the store and emerged
soon aftenvards with a Reverso on his wrist.

Admittedly, it was not always as easy for retailers as in this case.
Léon Constantin, who as chief salesman spent years travelling the world
With the Reverso, has many exciting stories to tell about the watch,
featuring not only successes but also disappointments. A selection of
these anecdotes deserves to be recorded for posterity.

boarded the plane with the collection suitcase to go and promote sales
of the Reverso in Japan.

“Have you seen how they are dressed, Mr. Constantin?” the agent
in Tokyo asked him straight away. “All the same: a white shirt, dark
tie, grey suit, the same hair and eyes, they are all Mr. Mikimotos and
want to set themselves apart, at least in trivial details”. Young Japanese
set themselves apart through a brightly-coloured tie, which either repre-
sented, or was supposed to represent, the current fashion trend in Paris.
The other, even more inconspicuous way of being slightly different was
to wear a watch on the wrist without the name “Seiko”. L. Constantin
saw the sense of this. And he quickly got to know another special
feature of Japanese life, of which he had hitherto been unaware: the
people work and concentrate very hard all day. Ataround seven or eight
o'clock in the evening the men leave their offices or banks; they do not
go home, but out to bars. Frequently they spend half the night drinking
and merrymaking with the same colleagues with whom they have
already shared the working day.

Léon Constantin at once developed an advertising strategy for
the Reverso in Japan: “The watch that helps you to forget the time” — by
being turned over in between the work-filled day and the work-free
evening. By alluding to the special Japanese-style way of spending their
evenings after work he propagated the Reverso as a “Non-watch” for
the stress-free evening hours. In addition, a lavish special service guaran-
teed the customer delivery - within 24 hours — of his Reverso with the
a monogram or one of the popular Asian signs of the zodiac on
the back.

This campaign met with only limited success, as L. Constantin
noted later with some regret. The Reverso philosophy he wanted to
convey with style was perhaps too European and the advertising not
yet professional enough to translate effectively such an idea into the
Japanese lifestyle. But he learnt a lesson from the Japanese that he
would never forget in his long career: There are times for work, and
there must be a time in which there is no time”. Also when selling
watches.

the Reverso off the ground in Japan turned into a dramatic race against

322

IN THE INVENTORS’

BASILICA

On March 4, 1931 engineer René-Alfred Chauvot went to the patent
section of the Ministry of Trade and Industry in Paris, His briefcase
contained several pages of description and 17 drawings relating to his
invention: a watch “capable of sliding in its support and being com-
pletely turned over”. As of 1.15 p.m. on that day his invention Was pro-
tected by law against imitation. The original of the patent specification
which was Officially granted on August 3rd under the number 712 868, is
currently stored together with almost one million other Pen doe
ments at the Institut National de la Propriété Industrielle (INPI), 73, rue
dAmsterdam, Paris — the French basilica of technological and scientific
progress and inventiveness. Mr. Chapard, the administrator of this vast
collection of patents in diverse fields of physics, mechanical engineering
and chemistry, among others, led us through the heart of the INPI — the
impressive library housing bound volumes of patents in the grandiose
former refectory of St. André’s cloister.

Inventors are fascinating people. Even today their discoveries and
inventions still arouse the curiosity of numerous students and patent
agents who come to the INPI every day. The last application to be regis-
tered before Chauvot’s is dated March 2, 1931: one Hippolyte Coinin,
fom the Isere department of France, applied fora patent on a box that
afforded the public at large protection from all types of gases — a kind of
people’s gas mask. The man must have been a pessimist... or perhaps
simply a realist, Half an hour after Chauvot on March 4, 1931 a Monsieur
Camille Contal filed an application relating to a projection apparatus for
advertising purposes. Shortly afterwards it was a German by the name of
Heinrich Miiller who patented in France a doll with movable limbs and

4 mechanical motor.

Patent language tends to be very dry as it is drafted to preclude all
conceivable misunderstandings, and thus any disputes with a previous
Orsubsequent applicant. We are reproducing on the next four pages the
actual text of René-Alfred Chauvot’s invention, an historic document
Which is, as it were, the Reverso’s “birth certificate’. The original
drawings in this document were later supplemented by those con-
fained in subsequent first and second additions to the original patent.
The combination of all these drawings gives a clearer picture of the

development and refinement of the original idea, up until a very close

fepresentation of the future product.

Paris,

rue d’Amsterda

home of the orig

1931 Reverso pat

THE ORIGINAL

REVERSO PATENT AS

FILED IN PARIS

French Republic
Ministry of Trade
and Industry
Directorate of Industrial
Property
Group 12 — Class 1

A watch capable of sliding in its support and
being completely turned over

Mr. René-Alfred CHAUVOT, domiciled in
France (Seine)

Application filed on March 4, 19317 at1.15 p.m
in Paris. Patent granted on August 3, 1931. -
Published on October 14, 1931

The present invention relates to a watch
capable of sliding in its support and being
completely turned over

This watch, comprising a case, a support of
said case and, where applicable, means
permitting the attachment of this support to
any suitable object, may by means of these
devices advantageously be combined with,
in particular, a strap, a belt, a bag or any other
article

The invention ts characterized by the follow-
ing principal features, which may be present
individually or in combination

a. The support forms a guide track in which
the case can slide when the dial is in the
visible position; this support and this case are
connected to one another by two pivots
provided on the side walls of one of these
two elements, either the case or the support;
said pivots can, under certain conditions,
slide and swivel in two grooves provided in
the side walls opposite the other element, in
such a way that the case, which ts normally
folded over between the side walls which
form the guide track of the support, the dial
thus being concealed in the folded-over
position, may be turned over by swivelling
through half a revolution on the pivots so as
to render the dial visible, and then be en-
again in this position between the side
walls of the support, by sliding the pivots in
the grooves or reciprocally b. One or several
in devices, for example a spring-loaded
arranged in such a way that the
are removably fixed in
the dial

gaged

snap-
ball, 1s or are ¢
case and the support
ther two possible positions: with
either folded away or visible
Thanks to this invention the watch may be
worn optionally in one or other of these two
positions, and it is extremely simple and easy
to change from one position to the other
The invention further comprises other fea-
tures which may be derived both from the
iollowing descnpuon and from the drawing

attached by way of an example and in which:
Fig. 1 isa front view of a watch case according
to the present invention; fig. 2 is a cross-sec-
tional view along the line A to A of fig. 1; fig. 3
isa top view of the bezel; fig. 4 isa top view of
the case, showing the slide and snap-in ele-
ments; fig. 5. and 6 are a frontand side view of
one of the springs bearing the pivot; fig. 7 and
8 are a front and side view of an alternative
method of mounting the pivots; fig. 9 shows a
variant of the snap-in device; fig. 10 is a bot-
tom view of the support; fig. 11 Is a cross-sec-
tional view of the support along the line B-B
of fig. 10; fig. 12 1s a cross-sectional view along
the line C-C of fig. 10; fig. 13 1s a perspective
view showing the design details of a side wall
of the support; fig. 14 is an end view of the
assembly in the visible position; fig. 15 isa top
view; fig. 16 shows a top view of the watch in
the visible position at the end of its sliding
movement; and fig. 17 is a top view of the
watch in the folded-over position

The watch proper consists of a case 1 with
lower projections 2 (figs. 1 and 2) and exhibits
two holes 3 and 4 on two of its sides. The case
further comprises a square-shaped central
portion 5 which is designed to receive the
watch movement. The case is normally
closed by a bezel 6 bearing the glass; it further
comprises (fig. 4) a hollow 23 into which are
inserted the winding crown 24 and a tab 22,
whose function will become clear subse-
quently.

Between the side walls of the case bearing
the holes 3 and 4 and the square-shaped cen-
tral portion 5 is left a space sufficient to house
with a close sliding fit two springs 7 of the
same length as the corresponding sides of the
case. These springs may be of the same type
as those shown in figs. 5 and 6, to one of the
ends of which is riveted a pin 8 designed to
slot into the holes 3 of the case; alternatively
it is possible to use, as shown in figs. 7 and
8, ordinary springs 7 combined with angle-
brackets 9 bearing the pins 8; these angle-
brackets are then inserted into the inside
corner of the case, against which they are
maintained by the spring, the centre of which
is supported by the square-shaped centre
portion.

At the other end, these springs exert pressure
on, for example, balls 10 (fig. 4) which fit into
the holes 4 and project therefrom. These balls
may also be replaced by round-tipped pins 11
(fig. 9), the head 12 of which is in contact with
the spring

Fig. 10 is a bottom view of the support which
forms the guide track, This support consists
essentially of a baseplate 13 presenting ribs 14
or any equivalent arrangement whose pur-
pose is to assure its rigidity; this baseplate

324

extends and on two of its sides only
with angled parts 15, each comprising a ah
cess or guide groove 16 approximately half
way up and, in the lower part, a groove 18
ig. 13), which is closed at its rear end in 18
and at the front by a circular part 19 whose
centre coincides with the centre of the
rounded end 17 of recess 16. A hollow 20 is
provided in the lower part of groove 18 anda
limit-stop 21 is executed in the rear part of the
support
Assembly of the watch in the support which
serves as a guide track is extremely simple.
The lower projections 2 of the case 1 are
inserted from the front (fig. 13) into the
grooves 18 of the support until the watch
occupies the central part of the support
and its rear edge abuts against the projection
21. Then the springs 7 are inserted, which
maintain both the pins 8 in the grooves 16
and the balls or similar elements 10 in the
same grooves. The watch is thereby fixed to
the sliding support, so that it is now possible
to fit the movement, which is covered by the
bezel 6. The result obtained is the watch
shown in figs. 14 and 15 in the visible position.
The presentation of this watch is similar to
that of usual watches and it may be used as a
wristwatch, a watch attached to an axis, a belt
or in any other form. The design of the sup-
port fixing elements will obviously vary
depending on the application for which the
watch is intended. Figs. 14-17 show fixing bars
that permit its use as a wristwatch.As stated in
the preamble, this watch may be used either
in the visible position (fig. 15) or in the re-
versed position (fig. 17); or it may be raised
momentarily from the reversed-dial position
to read the time, before being returned to
the original position.
In order to reverse the dial, it suffices to
execute a pulling movement to bring it into
the position shown in fig. 16. During this
sliding movement the pins 8 which are
located in the rear part (fig. 13) of the grooves
16 slide in the latter until they come into con-
tact with the front end 17; the balls are
pushed in, thereby compressing the springs 7
and allowing the assembly full freedom ot
movement. During this sliding movement
the lower projections 2 of the case slide in the
grooves 18 and prevent the case from lifting
in relation to its support.

THE REVERSO
OPENS UP A NEW ERA
FOR WRISTWATCHES

César de Trey, who marketed
the Reverso in the early years
through his “Societe Speécialites
horlogeres, was probably one of
the pioneers of modern market-
ing. The first Reverso sales cata-
logue appears to have been
published in about 1933/34.
Although an exact date cannot
be proved, an approximate
year can be deduced from the
models reproduced in the cata-
logue. We thought it would be
of partic ular interest to repro-
duce the catalogue, not only
as an historical document but
also as an aid to collectors, who
will find it contains all the watch
models and face designs made
at that time. The “monograms”
section gives an idea of the
Art-Deco-style monogram
culture practised at that time — The invention of this case marks an

‘ ; important stage in the watch industry's
a custom well worth imitating, incessant progress. This watch is not
a fanciful toy but essentially a practically
designed object.
The Reverso is worn like any other wrist
watch with a visible face, but if necessary
it can be reversed and protected against
shocks or rough treatment. With the en-
graved initials highlighted by dark varnish
or by the wearer's coat of arms on the
now-visible back, it becomes an elegant
evening watch which perfectly, yet dis-
creetly, expresses the wearer's personal
style. As a wristwatch, the Reverso plays
a far more important role than modern
hunter watches played in the history of
pocket watches. A watch - especially
@ wristwatch - is always exposed to the

risk of damage or accident during all
kinds of social and sporting activities

The classic Reverso is produced in a single
model. with a ladies’ size and a gentle-
man’s size, thereby meeting the wishes of
the retail trade, which can achieve maxi-
mum sales with a minimum number of
watches in stock. The Reverso is made in
two different versions: The “luxury” version
is characterized by a specially-made move-
ment, sapphire glass, an outstandingly
beautiful dial and a top-quality leather
strap. The “standard” version possesses a
robust but less luxurious movement, a nor-
mal watch glass and a less richly decorated
but nevertheless outstandingly crafted

er dial and strap. The difference in price
between the two qualities is entirely war-
ranted by the difference in cost between
the materials used

GW = Gentleman’s watch

h “Staybrite” steel is used for the
mie vit LW = Ladies’ watch

Reverso, as it is undoubtedly superior to
all other so-called stainless steels. That is
why it is so difficult to distinguish a steel
Reverso from a white-gold model. The
steel model retains its shiny surface for
much longer and ts infinitely more resistant
than a case made of any other metal

ngth. We have developed a special

Sapphire glass

All “luxury” models of the Reverso are
fitted with a sapphire glass which ts set
into the bezel and rendered watertight by
4 special material. The illustration gives a
striking impression of the cost of making a
sapphire glass. Only a very few watchmak-
ers know that a sapphire glass fora normal-
sized gentleman’s wristwatch costs 7 or 8
golden francs ex-works ~ if one thousand
pieces are ordered! Normal crystal is
frequently sold to watch dealers and the
public fraudulently as “sapphire glass”

The monogram

One of the most effective sales arguments
for the Reverso is doubtless the option
the wearer has of having his coat of arms,
initials or club insignia engraved on the
back. An engraved Reverso is a personal-
ized timepiece. Just as a signet ring Is
engraved with the owner's Coat of arms.
so can the Reverso receive a personal
engraving and in this way become an
unmistakeably individual piece

As “Staybrite” steel ts extraordinarily ¢

only tour days we can return your watch

varnish in black, black and red or dark
However, in order to enable you to meet

will be pleased to send you on request a

that dries faster than varnish, as well

he contract was signed with the inventor of the Reverso op
July 25, 1931, LeCoultre & Cie had no movement that could be Used in
this special case. Itwas therefore decided to develop the 11-line calibre
11 U forthe gentleman's model and the 9-line calibre 9 U for the ladieg
model. In the meantime, until the development and production of
these calibres had been completed, the first Reversos were fitted with
movements made by the firm Tavannes & Co. These were:

When t

calibre 064 for the gentleman’s model

and
calibre 050 for the ladies’ model.

Work started on designing the 11 U calibre during the summer
of 1932 and the first watches were fitted with it and delivered at the
beginning of the second half of 1933,

In the winter of 1933 the design drawings were completed for
the 9 U calibre. Delivery of watches with this movement started at the
beginning of 1934.

Both calibres were improved several times, which makes the task
of a watchmaker who has to effect a repair a little more difficult, But, on
the other hand, this difficulty can be helpful for the watch collector, asit
enables him to determine the age of a watch to within a few months.

Quite a rare phenomenon occurred ten years after the launch of
the Reverso: although the Reverso’s case was still successful, the 11 U
movement then being used was replaced by a series of new calibres:
11 L, 11 LSC and 11 LO.

Several different inscriptions were possible on the Reverso’s dial:

From 1942 onwards the Reverso
was sull in demand and

being made. The Manufacture
deaded to combine the often-
modiiied and improved calibres
of the 11 U type in a new series
of movements designated 11 L,
11 LSC. and 11 LO. These are
witually the last U calibres to
have been made. The 17 L and
11 LO are similar in appearance,
which is why the illustration
shows only the basic 11 L calibre,

referred to internally as the 424

Unlike the other Wwo move-
ments, the 11 LSC calibre
beanng the number 437 had
@ centre seconds-hand and
consequently an altered train
with a transmission wheel
above the third wheel and

cenual seconds-hand Pinion
=== PAGE 174 ===
THE NEW CALIBRES

From 1975 onwards the Reverso was fitted with a new movement, the
hand-winding 846 calibre, without a small seconds-hand, which is still
used today in the gentleman’s model and the mechanical ladies’ model.
The golden hand-winding calibre 824 with date display, power-reserve
display and small seconds-hand was introduced in 1991 for the limited
500-piece special series to mark the Reverso’s 60th anniversary. On the
basis of this movement the calibre 822 was developed - with some
technical modifications — for the series Reverso “Grande Taille”. This is also
a hand-wound movement. The table below enables the reader to com-
pare the most important data for these three calibres, although it should

be borne in mind that modern watchmakers now use some different
parameters for the technical description of a movement than in the past.
The data were compiled by Jean-Claude Meylan, head of the Technical

Department.
Comparison 846 824 822
of Calibres Reverso Reverso Reverso \

Acknowledgements

Heidelberg, spring 1992

A book such as this could not have been written without the assistance
of many people, to whom | am deeply indebted. | would like to thank
them all very sincerely, especially Messrs. Léon Constantin and Jean
Lebet, who were kind enough to grant me many hours of interviews and
who furnished the information which has enabled me to gain a more
precise idea of this impressive Manufacture and its history. | am also
grateful to all the Jaeger-LeCoultre employees, whose invaluable assis-
tance has sometimes been accompanied by touching kindness. | am
thinking in particular of the ladies in the Communication Department -
Jasmina, Corinne and Sandrine -, Mr. G.A. Ashworth for his assistance
with revising the English-language edition, as well as Mr. Henry-John
Belmont and the managerial staff, who opened so many doors for me.

| also wish to thank the designers Hartmut Eslinger, Ferdinand
A. Porsche and Bruno Sacco, who readily agreed to talk about the
Reverso; | am grateful to the photographers, Stefan Kresin and Bernd
Sumalowitsch; and to the team of graphic artists led by Thomas Hoch,
all of whom made a decisive contribution to the prompt publication
of this book in the desired form. My thanks also to my wife, Jeannine,
who helped me overcome many problems of a linguistic nature.

Last but not least, | express my compliments to Mrs. Olivia Cattin
and her team, who have done a brilliant job in adapting the German
text for the English, French and Italian editions.

Manfred Fritz
